are of Israel; neither are all they that are the seed 
of Abraham, children.” Continue, then, according to 
this, and say, “similarly not all who are of Jacob are 
to be reputed of the house of Jacob.” For Jacob and 
Israel are one and the same. Therefore consider none 
to belong to the house of Jacob except such as are 
found to be perfect in the faith of Jacob; or rather 
recognise these as the spiritual and eternal house of 
Jacob, in which the Lord Jesus shall reign for ever. 
Where is the man amongst us who, according to the 
signification of the name Jacob, supplants the devil 
in his heart and struggles against his vices and con- 
cupiscences, so that sin may not reign in his mortal 
body, but that, instead, Jesus may reign in him, here 
by grace and hereafter by glory? Oh, how blessed 


- is he in whom Jesus shall reign for ever, because he 


also shall reign with Jesus, “‘ and of his kingdom there 
shall be no end”! Oh, how glorious is that kingdom 
in which “‘ the kings of the earth shall assemble them- 
selves and gather together ” in order to praise and 
magnify Him Who is above all, “ King of kings and 
Lord of lords,’ from the contemplation of Whose 
divine splendour “‘ the just shall shine as the sun in 
the kingdom of their Father”?! Oh, if only the Lord 
Jesus would remember sinful me “in the favour of 
His people ” when He comes into His kingdom! Oh, 
if on that day when He is to “ deliver up the kingdom 
to God and the Father,” He would condescend to 
“visit (me) with His salvation, that (I) may see the 
good of His chosen, that (I) may rejoice in the joy of 
His nation, that He may be praised with His inherit- 
ance ” even by me! Meantime, “ come, Lord Jesus,” 
and remove all scandals from Thy kingdom, which is 


IIO ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


my poor soul, so that Thou Who art her rightful King, 
mayest reign in her. For avarice has entered and claims 
me for its own : vainglory desires to rule over me; pride 
wishes to be my king ; sensuality says, “ I will reign ” ; 
ambition, envy, jealousy, and anger contend in me 
concerning myself, as to which of them shall have the 
greatest power over me. I resist them as well as I 
can : I struggle against them according as I am assisted 
by grace, I proclaim that the Lord Jesus is my only 
King. For Him I defend myself, because I acknowledge 
myself to be His subject. I adhere to Him as my 
Lord and my God, and I declare that I have no king 
but the Lord Jesus. Come, then, O Lord, and “ scatter 
them by Thy power,” that so Thou alone mayest reign 
in me, because “‘ Thou ait Thyself my King and my 
God, Who commandest the saving of Jacob.” 

“ And Mary said to the Angel: How shall this be 
done, because I know not man? ” At first she pru- 
dently held her peace, whilst, still in doubt, she 
“thought with herself what manner of salutation 
this should be” ; for, being humble, she chose rather 
to answer nothing at all than rashly to say what she 
knew not. But now she has pondered the matter well 
and has been comforted, the Angel reassuring her ex- 
teriorly and God encouraging her interiorly—for He too 
is present to her, as the Angel has testified, saying, “ The 
Lord is with thee.” Therefore, being thus fortified, 
and having conquered fear by faith, and maidenly 
reserve by gladness, she asks the Angel, “ How shall 
this be done, because I know not man ? ” It is not 
that she doubts the fact : she only inquires concerning 
the means and the manner. For she does not ask 
“whether shall this be done? ” but, “ How shall this 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 117 


be done?” As if she should say, “ Since my Lord, 
Who is the witness of my conscience, knows that His 
handmaid has vowed to preserve her virginity, by 
what means and in what manner is He pleased that 
this be done ? Should it be necessary to set aside my 
vow, in order to become the Mother of so great a Son, 
I shall rejoice indeed because of the Son, but I shall 
grieve at having to sacrifice my virginity: however, 
may His will be done. But if I am to conceive as a 
virgin and to bring forth as a virgin,—which, if He so 
please, is certainly within His power to accomplish— 
then I shall know in truth that ‘He hath regarded 
the humility of His handmaid.’ ” * 

“ How shall this be done, because I know not man ? 
And the Angel answering said to her : The Holy Ghost 
shall come upon thee, and the power of the Most High 
shall overshadow thee.” Already she has been de- 
clared full of grace; and now it is said to her, “ The 
Holy Ghost shall come upon thee and the power of 
the Most High shall overshadow thee.” But how can 
she be full of grace without having the Holy Ghost, 
since He is the Giver of all graces? And if the Holy 
Ghost is already within her, what means this promise 
of Him, as if He is to come upon heranew? Perhaps 
we have the explanation of this difficulty in the fact 
that the Angel does not simply say, “ the Holy Ghost 
shall come into thee ” (veniet in te), but, “the Holy 
Ghost shall ‘supervene’ upon thee ” (superveniet in te) ; 


= because He has come to her before with an abundance 


of grace, and now it is announced that He shall 
“ supervene,” on account of the fulness and super- 


* Cf. A Lapide, Comment. in Lucam, c. i. 34 ; also St. Thomas, 
Sum. Theol., III. q. xxx. a. 3. 


118 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


abundance of grace which He is prepared to pour 
down upon her. But again, if she is already full of 
grace, how is she capable of receiving more? And if 
she has this capacity for further grace, how can she 
be said to be already full? Shall we suppose that 
the first fulness has filled her soul whilst the second is 
intended to fill her womb, so that the plenitude of 
the Divinity which even now dwells in her spiritually, 
as it has dwelt in many other holy persons, shall 
begin to dwell in her corporally also, a privilege 
accorded to no other saint ? 

The Angel therefore says, “The Holy Ghost shall 
come upon thee, and the power of the Most High 
shall overshadow thee.’’ What does he mean by the 
words, “‘ And the power of the Most High shall over- 
shadow thee” ? Let him who can, comprehend them. 
But is there any creature—except perhaps her who 
alone merited to have in herself this most blessed 
experience—is there, I ask, any creature capable of 
comprehending with his intellect or of discerning by 
his reason how the inaccessible Splendour of the God- 
head poured Itself into the Virgin’s womb, and how of 
that small portion of her body which It animated and 
united hypostatically to Itself, It made a shadow, as it 
were, for her whole being, in order that she might be 
able to endure the approach and the presence of such 
intolerable brightness? And possibly for this reason in 
particular it was said to her, “ The power of the Most 
High shall overshadow thee,” because, namely, the 
matter was thus shadowed in mystery, and because 
the knowledge of what the Blessed Trinity was pleased 
to accomplish, of Itself alone and directly, in and 
with the Virgin alone, was to be communicated to 


SS a a a LL TEE LE CO eee 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 119 


none except her to whom alone was to be granted the 
experience. In this sense, then, (as I suppose), the 
Angel said to her, “ The Holy Ghost shall come upon 
thee ’—in order by His presence to make thee fruitful, 
“and the power of the Most High shall overshadow 
thee,” that is, “ the manner in which thou shalt con- 
ceive by the Holy Ghost shall be so veiled and covered 
and overshadowed in His most secret counsel by 
Christ, the Wisdom and Power of God, that none shall 
have knowledge of it save Him and thee.” It was as if 
Gabriel had answered the Virgin thus: ‘‘ Wherefore 
askest thou me concerning that which thou art soon 
to experience in thyself? Thou shalt know, thou 
shalt know, I say, and the knowledge shall fill thee 
with happiness: but none can instruct thee in this 
mystery except Him Who is its Author. My mission 
is not to effect but only to announce to thee thy vir- 
ginal conception. And how this is to be accomplished, 
as it cannot be made known except by Him Who shall 
accomplish it, so neither can it be learned except by 
her in whom it shall be accomplished. And therefore 
also the Holy Which shall be born of thee shall be 
called the Son of God. That is to say, since it is not 
by man thou shalt conceive, but by the Holy Ghost, 
and since thou shalt conceive the Son of God, Who is 
the Power of the Most High, therefore also the Holy 
Which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of 
God. I mean by this, that not only He Who (as being 
the. Power of God) shall come from the Father’s Bosom 
into thy womb and overshadow thee, but also what- 
ever He shall unite to Himself of thy substance shall 
be called the Son of God : just as, on the other hand, 
He Who is born of the Father before all ages shall 


120 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


be reputed henceforth thy Son also. But in such a 
way shall the Son Who is born of the Father be thy 
Son, and the Son born of thee be the Son of the 
Father, that nevertheless there shall not be two Sons 
but one. And although there is an infinite difference 
between the Flesh Which this Son obtains from thee 
and the Divinity Which He derives from the Father, 
He shall not be only in part thy Son, and in part the 
Father’s, but wholly and entirely the same Son of 
both thee and the Father.” * 

“ And therefore also the Holy Which shall be born 
of thee shall be called the Son of God.” Notice with 
what reverence the Angel said, ‘‘ the Holy Which shall 
be born of thee.” For wherefore did he speak of “ the 
Holy ” thus simply and without addition ? The reason 
is, as I believe, because he had no proper and suitable 
name for that most excellent, most magnificent, most 
venerable Something Which was to be taken from the 
Virgin’s spotless flesh, and (with Its Soul) to be united 
to the Only-Begotten of the Father. Had he called It 
“the holy Flesh,” or “the Sacred Humanity,” or 
‘the holy Infant,” he would have seemed to himself 


* That is to say, Christ is not merely the (natural) Son of 
God according to His Divinity and the Son of Mary according 
to His Humanity, but He is the Son of both His Father and 
His Mother according to His whole Self as the Word incarnate. 

“She (Mary) is not merely the Mother of Our Lord’s man- 
hood, or of Our Lord’s Body, but she is also to be considered 
the Mother of the Word Himself, the Word incarnate. God 
in the Person of the Word, the Second Person of the All-glorious 
Trinity, humbled Himself to become her Son. ‘ Non horruisti 
Virginis uterum,’ as the Church sings, ‘ Thou didst not shrink 
from the Virgin’s womb.’ He took the substance of His Human 
Flesh from her, and clothed in It lay within her, and He bore 
It about with Him after birth as a sort of badge and witness 
that He, though God, was hers.’ —Newman, Discourses to 
Mixed Congregations, p. 383. 

Cf. St. Thomas, Sum Theol., III. q. xxxv. a. 4-5.—(Translator.) 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 121 


to be speaking of It unworthily. He therefore referred 
to It indefinitely as “the Holy,” because whatever 
That might be which was to be born of the Virgin, 
It was certainly Something holy and singularly holy, 
by the sanctification of the Spirit and by Its personal 
union with the Word. 

‘“ And behold thy cousin Elizabeth, she also hath 
conceived a son in her old age.” Why was it necessary 
to announce to the Virgin that her hitherto sterile 
cousin had also conceived? Is it that she was still 
perhaps doubtful, still hesitating to assent to the 
heavenly oracle, and therefore the Angel desired to 
convince her by the evidence of this other wonder ? 
God forbid! We read of how Zachary’s incredulity 
was punished by this same Angel, but nowhere do we 
find him reproving Mary for anything: rather we hear 
Elizabeth in the spirit of prophecy commending her 
faith and saying, “ Blessed art thou that hast believed, 
because those things shall be accomplished that were 
spoken to thee by the Lord.” No, the true reason why 
her cousin’s pregnancy was announced to the Virgin 
was in order that her joy might be multiplied by 
hearing of such a succession of prodigies. For it was 
necessary that she, who was destined soon in the joy 
of the Holy Ghost to conceive the Son of the Father’s 
charity, should be prevented and flooded with an un- 
precedented inundation of gladness and love ; because 
it was only the most loving and the most joyous of 
hearts that could contain Him Who is the infinite 
Treasure of divine love and sweetness. Or perhaps 
for this reason was Elizabeth’s pregnancy made known 
to Mary, because it was manifestly fitting that the 
Virgin should hear from the Angel before learning 


122 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


from men a fact which was soon to be everywhere 
published. Otherwise it would have been thought that 
the Mother of God was excluded from the counsels of 
her Son, if she were kept in ignorance of what was 
taking place so near her on the earth. Or again, 
Mary may have been informed of her cousin’s condi- 
tion, to the end that, being notified now of the Saviour’s, 
and now of the Precursor’s coming, she might the 
better remember the time and order of the events, 
and so be better able to make known the truth after- 
wards to the apostles and evangelists, as one who had 
been fully and supernaturally enlightened concerning 
all these mysteries from the beginning. Or, finally, 
the purpose of telling the Virgin that Elizabeth had 
conceived may have been this: that the news of the 
elder cousin’s condition might remind the younger of 
the claims of charity; so that the Virgin’s hasty jour- 
ney to the hill country to visit her kinswoman might 
afford the unborn Prophet an opportunity of rendering 
the first-fruits of his service to His still younger Lord ; 
and that, whilst the meeting of mothers and infants 
excited the joyous devotion of all, wonders should be 
followed by wonders more astonishing. 

But, O Virgin, do not entertain the hope that these 
magnificent promises which thou hast heard from the 
Angel are to be fulfilled by him. And if thou wouldst 
know by whom, then, they shall be accomplished, attend 
to what the Angel adds, ‘‘ Because no word shall be 
impossible with God.” As if he should say, “ For the 
fulfilment of the promises which I have faithfully made 
to thee, I presume not on my own strength, but on the 
power of Him Who has sent me; because no word 
shall be impossible with God.” For what word can be 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 123 


impossible to Him Who has made all things by His Word? 
Nevertheless I should like to know why the Angel did 
not say, “ because no deed”’ shall be impossible with 
God, instead of, “‘ because no word.” But perhaps he 
preferred the latter expression, because as easily as 
men can say what they will, even when they lack 
the power of accomplishment, with the same ease, nay, 
with incomparably greater ease, God is able to fulfil 
in act whatever can be expressed in speech. Let me 
put it more plainly. If it were as easy for men to 
accomplish as to say what they will, then for us also 
in the same way no deed would be impossible. As 
the case is, however, the familiar adage holds true, 
namely, that there is a wide difference between doing 
and saying, yet only for men, and not also for God. 
For God alone it is the same thing to do as to say, 
and the same thing to say as to will. Consequently, 
“ no word shall be impossible with God.” To take an 
example. The prophets were able to foresee and to 
foretell that the Virgin and her barren cousin would 
conceive and bring forth: yet surely they had not 
the power to bring to pass what they were capable of 
announcing. But God Who gave His prophets the 
gift of foresight, with the same ease with which He 
could predict what He pleased through them, could 
afterwards, when He chose, accomplish by Himself 
whatever He had promised. For with God there can 
be no discord between intention and promise, because 
He.is Truth ; nor between promise and accomplishment, 
because He is Power ; nor between accomplishment and 
mode, because He is Wisdom. And consequently, “ no 
word shail be impossible with God.” 

O happy Virgin! Thou hast heard what is to be 


124 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


accomplished in thee, and what is to be the mode of 
accomplishment. Each is a matter for wonder, yet also 
and equally an occasion of joy. Therefore, “ rejoice 
greatly, shout for joy, O Daughter of Jerusalem.” And 
since “to thy hearing has been given joy and gladness,” 
let us also hear from thee the reply of gladness which 
we so eagerly long for ; and then “‘ the bones that have 
been humbled shall rejoice.” Thou hast heard, I say, 
what is to be accomplished in thee, and thou hast 
believed ; believe also what has been explained to thee 
concerning the mode of accomplishment. Thou hast 
been told that thou shalt conceive and bear a Son: 
thou hast heard that this shall be not by man but 
by the operation of the Holy Spirit. Behold the Angel 
now awaits thy answer: “it is time that he should 
return to the Lord Who sent him.” We also, O Lady, 
await from thy lips the sentence of mercy and com- 
passion, we who are so miserably groaning under the 
sentence of condemnation. For lo! the price of our 
salvation is now offered to thee: if thou wilt only 
consent, we shall at once be set at liberty. We have 
been created by the eternal Word of God, and behold 
we die: by thy momentary word we must be renewed 
and restored to life. O Virgin most loving, Adam, 
now exiled from Paradise with all his miserable off- 
spring, implores this favour of thee. For this does 
Abraham entreat thee, for this David, for this all the 
other holy fathers, thine own ancestors, who are now 
dwelling in the region of the shadow of death. See, 
the whole world, prostrate at thy feet, awaits thy 
answer. And not without cause. For on thy word 
depend the consolation of the miserable, the redemp- 
tion of the captives, the pardon of the condemned, the 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 125 


salvation of all the children of Adam, of the entire 
human race. O Virgin, delay not to answer. Speak 
the word, O Lady, speak the word which all on earth, 
and all in limbo, yea, and even all in Paradise are 
waiting to hear. Christ Himself, the King and Lord 
of all, longs for thy answer with a longing equal to 
the ardour wherewith He “hath desired thy beauty,” 
because it is by means of thy consent that He has 
decreed to save the world. Hitherto thou hast pleased 
Him by thy silence, but now thy speech shall give 
Him more pleasure. For behold He calls to thee from 
heaven, saying, “O fairest among women, let thy 
voice sound in My ears.” Therefore if thou wilt give 
Him to hear thy voice, He will give thee to see our 
salvation. And is it not for this thou hast been sup- 
plicating, and sighing, and pouring out prayers with 
tears by day and by night ? What then? Art thou she 
to whom this hath been promised “or look we for 
another ? ?” Nay, thou thyself art she, and there is 
none other. Thou, I say, art she who has been pro- 
mised, who has been expected, who has been yearned 
for, through whom the holy Patriarch Jacob hoped to 
receive eternal life, when, being now about to die, he 
exclaimed, ‘‘I will look for Thy salvation, O Lord.” 
Thou art she in whom and by whom “God our King 
before the ages hath decreed to work salvation in the 
midst of the earth.” Wherefore dost thou expect 
from another what is now offered to thyself? Why 
hopest thou to receive through another that which 
through thee shall be soon given to the world, pro- 
vided only thou wilt consent and say the word? 
Make haste, therefore, to answer the Angel, or rather 
to answer the Lord through the Angel. Say the word 


126 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


and receive the Word. Utter thy human word and con- 
ceive the Divine Word. Pronounce the transitory word 
and embrace the Word everlasting. Why dost thou 
hesitate? Wherefore dost thou fear ? Believe, consent, 
and receive into thy womb the Word of the Father. 
Let thy humility take courage, let thy modesty be 
confident. It is in nowise expedient now that thy 
virginal simplicity should be forgetful of prudence. O 
Virgin most prudent, in this matter alone thou mayest 
put aside all fear of presumption, because although 
modesty pleases by its silence, yet more necessary for 
us now is the charity of speech. O happy Virgin, open 
thy heart to faith, open thy lips to consent, open thy 
bosom to thy Creator. Behold the Desired of all 
nations is standing outside and knocking at thy door. 
Oh, if He should pass on whilst thou delayest to open, 
and thou shouldst have to begin once more to seek 
with sorrow “ Him Whom thy soul loveth”! Arise, 
therefore, and make haste to open to Him. Arise by 
faith, make haste by devotion, open by consent. 
“And Mary said: Behold the handmaid of the 
Lord, be it done to me according to thy word.” Divine 
grace is always found in intimate union with the virtue 
of humility, because “ God resisteth the proud and 
giveth grace to the humble.” The Virgin therefore 
replies with humility, in order that the seat of grace 
may be prepared in her. ‘“‘ Behold,” she says, “ the 
handmaid of the Lord.” Oh, how sublime is the 
humility here manifested, which knows not how to 
yield to honour or to be elated with glory! She is 
chosen to be the Mother of God, and she calls herself 
His handmaid! Assuredly it is a sign of a more than 
common humility not to forget humility in the presence 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 127 


of such glory. To be humble in abjection is nothing 
very great ; but it is great virtue indeed, and as rare 
as great, to be humble in the midst of honours. For 
instance, were the Church, deceived by my hypocrisy, 
to advance such a miserable wretch as I to some post 
of honour, even though not very exalted, God per- 
mitting this either on account of my own sins or 
because of the sins of those under me: should I not 
immediately forget what I really am and begin to 
suppose myself such as I am reputed by men, who 
cannot see the heart? Undoubtedly: I should give 
credit to public opinion, and turn a deaf ear to the 
voice of conscience. And estimating virtue by honour, 
not honour by virtue, I should account myself the 
more holy in proportion as I should find myself 
elevated above others. You may see very many now 
in the Church, who, after being raised from a mean 
condition to honourable rank, and from penury to 
affluence, have become suddenly inflated with pride 
and forgetful of their former abjection, so much so 
indeed that they are ashamed of their kindred, yea, 
even disdain to acknowledge their humble parents. 
You may also see some wealthy persons aspiring to 
all kinds of ecclesiastical honours ; and then, having 
changed their dress without changing their morals, 
they congratulate themselves on their sanctity. They 
deem themselves worthy of the positions which they 
have obtained by intrigue, and attribute to merit the 
honours which (if I may venture to affirm it) they 
have purchased for money. However, I will say 
nothing more about such as these, who are blinded 
by ambition, and to whom honourable positions 
furnish occasions for pride. 


128 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


But there are others to be seen, who (I grieve to say 
it), after despising the glory of earthly pomp, appear 
to have learned pride in the very school of humi- 
lity, and in the cloister itself, under the wings of the 
meekest and humblest of masters, to have become more 
arrogant and impatient than ever they were in the 
world. And what carries perversity further still, many 
are unable to put up with the least contempt in reli- 
gion, who, whilst living in the world, could make them- 
selves appear nothing better than contemptible. Their 
motive for entering the cloister would seem to be this, 
that since there was no chance of honours for them 
where honours were sought for by others, there at least 
they might hope for some honours where such things 
were avoided by all but themselves. Yet others I see— 
and the sight is an afflicting one—who, after entering 
the army of Christ, entangle themselves again in 
worldly affairs, again give themselves up to earthly 
solicitudes. You may behold them displaying much 
zeal in the building of houses, whilst they neglect the 
care of their conscience. Under the pretext of the 
common good, they sell their words to the wealthy 
and their greetings to matrons ; aye, and contrary to 
the edict of their Divine Emperor, they covet the 
goods of others, and contentiously strive to recover 
their own, not attending to the Apostle, who announces 
in the name of his Sovereign ; “ Already indeed there is 
plainly a fault among you, that you have lawsuits one 
with another. Why do you not rather suffer yourselves 
to be defrauded? ” Is it thus they have crucified the 
world to themselves and themselves to the world, 
who, whereas they were once scarcely known in their 
own hamlet or village, now passing from province to 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 129 


province and from court to court, are reckoned amongst 
the acquaintance of kings and the familiars of princes ? 
What shall I say of the religious habit itself, which 
they no longer esteem for its worth even as a com- 
fortable covering, but only for its value as a graceful 
ornament? And they pay more attention to the 
material habit which clothes their bodies than to the 
moral habits of their minds. It is a shocking thing to 
say: the vain woman is now outdone in her own 
special art by the religious who looks more to costliness 
in matters of dress than to use or necessity. Even 
the outward form of religion is discarded by these 
soldiers of Christ, who value its livery not as a pro- 
tection for their souls but as a becoming decoration 
for their bodies,—who, when they ought to prepare 
themselves for battle and to advance against the 
“ powers of the air” beneath the banner of poverty 
(which the adversary so greatly fears) prefer in their 
soft garments to exhibit the sign of peace, and thus 
voluntarily, and without striking a blow, place them- 
selves helpless in the hands of their enemies. The 
cause of such dreadful evils is to be found in the fact 
that the humility through which we abandoned the 
world has itself been abandoned by us, with the 
consequence that we are compelled once more to 
pursue the vain objects of worldly ambition, becoming 
as dogs that go back to the vomit. 

Let us, therefore,—I mean all who recognise them- 
selves as belonging to this class—let us listen, I say, 
to the answer of her who, even when she was chosen to 
be the Mother of God, did not lose sight of humi- 
lity. ‘ Behold the handmaid of the Lord,” she replied, 
“ be it done to me according to thy word.” The words 


T. I 


130 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


“ be it done to me ” are an expression not of doubt but 
of desire. Hence, when she says, “be it done to me 
according to thy word,” we must understand her as 
giving utterance to an ardent wish, and not as re- 
quiring proof after the manner of the incredulous. 
Nevertheless, it is also possible to regard these words 
as a prayer, since no one ever prays for anything 
unless because he has faith and hopes to receive it. 
And God wills that we should pray to Him even for 
what He has promised. Perhaps the reason why He 
first promises what He designs to bestow is in order 
that the promise may excite our devotion, and that thus 
what should otherwise be given gratuitously, may now 
be rendered as the reward of devout supplication. So 
does our loving Lord, Who desires that all men should 
be saved, endeavour to extort merits for us from 
ourselves ; and He prevents us by bestowing upon us 
gratuitously what He may reward, because otherwise 
the reward itself should be given gratuitously. The 
prudent Virgin was not ignorant of this when to the 
prevenient grace of gratuitous promise she joined the 
merit of fervent petition, praying, ‘‘ Be it done to me 
according to thy word.” As if she should say, “‘ Con- 
cerning the Word of the Father be it done to me 
according to thy word. According to thy word, let 
the Word Who was in the beginning with God be made 
flesh of my flesh. Let the Word, I pray, become to 
me not as a spoken word which passes away, but a 
Word conceived and abiding; a Word enclosed in a 
body of flesh, not a word expressed by the sound of 
the voice. Let Him become to me not an audible 
word which sounds in the ear, but a visible Word 
that my eyes may see Him, a tangible Word that 


FOURTH SERMON ON THE VIRGIN MOTHER 131 


my hands may hold Him, a portable Word that I may 
carry Him in my arms. And let Him not become for 
me a written and silent word, but a Word incarnate 
and living: that is to say, not a word inscribed in 
dumb characters upon dead parchment, but the Word 
_ of God in human form impressed upon the living page 
of. my chaste bosom, impressed, I say, not by the 
agency of mortal hand, but by the operation of the 
Holy Spirit. Let Him become to me what He has 
become to none before and shall become to none after 
me. God ‘at sundry times and in divers manners 
spoke in times past to the fathers by the prophets,’ 
and the Word of the Lord is said to have come to the 
mouth of some, to the ear of some others, and even 
to the hand of other some: but my prayer is that, 
according to thy word, He would vouchsafe tocome into 
my womb. I do not ask that He should be orally pro- 
claimed to me, or that He should be symbolically re- 
presented, but that He be silently infused, personally 
incarnated, corporally enclosed. Therefore let the Word 
of God, Who in Himself has neither the need nor the 
power to be made, let Him, I pray, condescend to be 
made flesh in me and for me, according to thy word. 
According to thy word, let Him be made flesh for the 
whole world, but in a more particular manner for 
me,” * 

* The Saint is here taking the words “‘fiat mihi secundum 
verbum tuum ” to mean; Let him (the Word) be made (flesh) 
for me according to thy word. It is the teaching of holy and 


enlightened men that the Incarnation was more for Mary’s sake 
than for all the world beside.— (Translator), 


132 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


St. BERNARD’S APOLOGY FOR PRESUMING TO COMMENT 
UPON A PASSAGE OF SACRED SCRIPTURE ALREADY 
EXPOUNDED BY OTHER AUTHORS. 


I have explained this passage of Holy Scripture as 
well as I could. I know that my remarks will not 
obtain universal approval; on the contrary, I feel 
that I have made myself a butt for the censure of 
many. Some will regard my attempt as superfluous, 
others will condemn it as presumptuous: they will 
be displeased with the modern author who has ven- 
tured to compose a new commentary on a text which 
the fathers have exhaustively treated. Nevertheless, it 
appears to me that if some one coming after the fathers 
has something to say which is not opposed to the 
fathers, neither the fathers nor anybody else has a 
right to complain. But with respect to what I have 
borrowed from the fathers, if whilst thus escaping the 
reproach of presumption, I do not lose at the same 
time the fruit of devotion, I shall patiently endure to 
re be considered superfluous. However, let me tell those 

who find fault with me for undertaking what they 

judge to be a needless and useless work, that it has - 
not been my purpose to expound the Gospel, but rather 
to take occasion from the words of the Gospel to dis- 
course on themes which it is a delight for me to dwell 
upon. And if I have done wrong in composing these 
homilies for the purpose of exciting my own devotion, 
rather than for the sake of the common good, the 
loving-kind Virgin, to whom this little work is most 
affectionately dedicated, has influence enough with her 
merciful Son to obtain for me the pardon of this sin 
L also. 


SERMONS ON PSALM XC. 


ST. BERNARD’S PREFACE. 


DEAREST brethren, when I think of your labours and 
sufferings, my heart overflows with compassion. I 
look about for something to console you, and earthly 
comforts suggest themselves. But then I reflect that 
these, instead of helping, would do you incalculable harm; 
For even a small subtraction from the seed means no small 
diminution of the harvest ; and the cruel kindness that 
would now relax your penitential labours would likewise, 
little by little, rob your crown of its gems. What, there- 
fore, shall I do? where shall I find the meal of the 
Prophet ? * For without this seasoning, “ death is in 
the pot.” You are slain, dearest brethren, all the day 
long, “in labour and painfulness, in much watchings, 
in hunger and thirst, in fastings often . . . besides those 
things which are” within, namely, grief of heart and 
a throng of temptations. You are slain indeed, my 
brethren, but it is for the sake of Him Who was slain 
for you. And if the “ sufferings of Christ abound ” in 
you, “so also by Christ (shall your) comfort abound,” 
and your hearts, which refuse all other consolation, 
shall “delight in the Lord.” For even the very 
sufferings we endure for Him become through His love 
* Cf. 4 Kings iv. 38-41. 
133 


134 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


a source of consolation. Is it not certain that the 
burden of your tribulations is beyond what is cus- 
tomary for monks, beyond the power of nature, beyond 
human strength ? It is carried, therefore, not by you 
but by Another, by Him, doubtless, Who, according to 
the Apostle, “ upholdeth all things by the Word of 
His power.” And is not the enemy thus slain with 
his own sword, when the very weight of sorrow where- 
with he is wont to tempt us, is made the means of 
conquering his temptations, by giving us infallible 
assurance of the presence of God? For what have we 
to fear whilst He is with us Who “ upholdeth all 
things”? “The Lord is the Protector of my life,” 
sings the Psalmist, “ of whom shall I be afraid? For 
though I should walk in the midst of the shadow of 
death, I will fear no evil, for Thou art with me.” What 
is it that sustains the whole mass of this ponderous earth ? 
On what does the entire universe rest for support? And 
if there be some one body which serves as a foundation 
for all the others, by what is that supported? By 
nothing else, assuredly, than the Word of power Who 
upholdeth all things. For “ by the Word of the Lord 
the heavens were established, and all the power of 
them by the Spirit of His mouth.” 

Wherefore, in order that you may have consolation in 
the words of the Lord, during these days especially, when 
your labours and privations (as is only right) are some- 
what greater than usual, it will not be amiss to expound 


ST. BERNARD’S PREFACE 135 


for you some part of Holy Scripture. Indeed, I have 
been requested to do this by more than one of your- 
selves. And it seems to me that I ought to select that 
particular psalm which Satan made use of to tempt 
the Lord, so that the very words which the malignant 
one attempted to turn to his advantage may become 
the means of destroying his power. Here I would 
remind you that he is manifestly an imitator of the 
enemy of souls, whosoever makes an unholy use of any 
part of Sacred Scripture, whosoever would “ detain the 
truth of God in injustice,” as some are frequently 
found to do. Beware of this, my dearest brethren, 
beware of this, I say, because it is the work of the 
devil, and all who participate in it prove themselves 
thereby to belong to him, wresting to their ruin what 
was written for their salvation. But I will not dwell 
any longer on this subject: it is enough, I think, to 
have touched upon it briefly. Let me now, with the 
help of the Lord, attempt a discussion and exposition 
of the psalm I have chosen. 


FIRST SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE THREE CLASSES OF MEN WHO DWELL OUTSIDE 
THE AID OF THE Most HIGH. 


“ He that dwelleth in the aid of the Most High, shall abide under 
the protection of the God of heaven.” * 


You will most easily understand, my brethren, who 
he is “ that dwelleth in the aid of the Most High,” if 
I describe for you the different classes of persons who 
do not make their home in the divine help. There are 
three such classes, namely, those who have no hope, 
those whose hope is weak, and those whose hope is 
vain. For he does not dwell in the aid of the Most 
High who “ hath not made God his Helper, but trusteth 
in the abundance of his riches ” and in his own strength. 
Such a one is deaf to the voice of the Prophet, who calls 
out to him, saying, “ Seek ye the Lord while He may be 
found ; call upon Him while He is near.” For, pursuing 
temporal goods alone, he is “ emulous of evildoers,” 
and “‘envieth them that work iniquity,” “seeing the 
prosperity of sinners ” ; and thus he removes himself far 
from the help of the Most High, which he does not 
consider necessary for the attainment of such objects. 
I am not without fear, brethren, lest even amongst our- 
selves there should be some, who, instead of dwelling in 
the help of the Most High, “ trust in their own strength 
and glory in the multitude of their riches.” There 
may be someone, for instance, who, buoyed up with 

* The Douay Version has “the God of Jacob,” for which 
there seems to be no authority.—(Translator.) 


136 


FIRST SERMON ON PSALM XC 137 


fervour of spirit, feels nothing but delight in his watch- 
ings, in his fastings, in his labours, and in the other 
austere observances imposed by our Rule: or perhaps 
he seems to himself to have acquired an immense 
treasure of merits during a long course of years. Well, 
relying on this, he loses little by little, the fear of God ; 
then, influenced by a feeling of false and pernicious 
security, he begins to indulge himself more easily in 
curiosity and sloth, and commits faults of murmuring, 
detraction, and rash judgment. Now it is certain that, 
if he dwelt “in the aid of the Most High,” he would 
look to himself better, and would fear to offend One 
Whose help he realised to be still so necessary to him. 
And his fear of the Lord and his anxiety to please Him 
would be all the greater in proportion to the gifts he 
has already received. For without God’s assistance, we 
are not able even to preserve and guard what He 
bestows upon us. Nevertheless—though I cannot speak 
of or witness it without sorrow—there are some here 
who, in the beginning of their conversion, were fearful 
and careful enough, until they had made some little 
progress in the way of perfection and were given the 
taste of spiritual joy ; then, when their desires ought 
rather to have been intensified, according to what is 
written, “ They that eat Me shall yet hunger,” they 
began to say, at least, by their conduct, “ Why should 
we serve the Lord any longer, since we already possess 
all that He has promised to giveus ? ” O my brother, if 
thou only knewest how little comparatively that is 
which thou hast obtained, and how easily it will be 
lost, unless He Who gave it helps thee to keep it! 
These are considerations which should render us all 
very careful and very obedient to God, so that we shall 


138 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


not belong to the number of those who therefore dwell 
not “in the aid of the Most High,” because they 
regard His aid as unnecessary to them. And it is of 
such I have said that they are without hope in the 
Lord. 

The second class is of those whose hope is weak, of 
those, namely, who considering their own powerlessness, 
entirely lose heart and succumb to “ pusillaniminity of 
spirit.” They dwell not “ in the aid of the Most High,” 
but in the helplessness of their own flesh, and are so 
fond of contemplating their own miseries, that they are 
ready at any moment to recite the full list of what 
they have to endure. For weacquire a facility for doing 
the things in which we are constantly engaged. Such as 
these, I say, do not “ dwell in the aid of the Most 
High ” ; they do not so much as know that aid, because 
they are unable to raise their thoughts high enough to 
think of it, even occasionally. In the third class are 
they who hope indeed, but in vain. For they trust 
themselves so securely to the mercy of the Lord that 
they take no pains to get rid of their sins. Hope of 
this kind is utterly useless. It is a hope which con- 
foundeth, because it is devoid of charity. And to 
such as entertain it the Prophet says, “ Cursed is he 
that sinneth in hope.” * The Psalmist, on the other 
hand, tells us that “‘ the Lord taketh pleasure in them 
that fear Him, and in them that hope in His name.” 
Observe how significantly he speaks of the fear of the 
Lord in them that please Him, before referring to their 


* This text is found neither in the Latin nor in the Greek. 
Mabillon refers it to Jerem. xvii. 5. But what we read there 
is something entirely different, ‘‘ Maledictus vir qui confidit 
in homine” (Cursed be the man that trusteth in man).— 
(Translator.) 


FIRST SERMON ON PSALM XC 139 


hope. For he hopes in vain who by his presumption 
drives far from him the grace of God and empties his 
hope of all merit. 

Therefore no man belonging to any of these three 
categories “ dwelleth in the aid of the Most High.” 
The first class dwell in their own merits, the second 
in their miseries, the third in their sins. The dwelling- 
place of the third is unclean ; that of the second, un- 
comfortable ; that of the first, unsafe and stupid. For 
can anything be more stupid than to dwell in a house 
scarcely yet begun? Perhaps it appears to you to be 
finished ? But according to Holy Scripture, ‘‘ When a 
man hath completed then shall he begin.” For this 
dwelling of man’s merits is at best but a tottering building, 
which, instead of occupying, we must rather prop up and 
sustain. Is not this present life fragile and uncertain ? 
Therefore similarly uncertain and fragile must be all 
that is founded upon it. Who indeed would have faith 
in the stability of a house built upon a shifting founda- 
tion? Dangerous, my brethren, is the dwelling of those 
who confide in their own merits, dangerous, I say, 
as wanting solidity. Uncomfortable I have called the 
home of them that are plunged in despair by the con- 
sideration of their own miseries, because, as I have said, 
they dwell in torments. For not only are they over- 
whelmed with present woes, which refuse them a 
moment’s respite, but they suffer also and more eruelly 
from the foresight of troubles not yet experienced, and 
even from the dread of others which perhaps they shall 
never feel, so that in their case not “‘ sufficient for the 
day is the evil thereof.” Can any torture be more 
terrible, any hell be more insupportable than such a 
condition ? And what renders their wretchedness more 


140 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


wretched still is the fact that, although burdened with 
such a weight of miseries, they have no heavenly 
bread to support them. These are they who dwell 
not “in the aid of the Most High,” because they have 
abandoned hope. The first class do not seek the help 
of God because they do not realise its necessity. And 
the third class are far removed from it, because they 
seek it in a way in which it cannot be found. For 
they alone ‘ dwell in the aid of the Most High,” who 
desire to obtain it alone, who fear to lose it alone, 
and who meditate on it with anxious and assiduous 
thought. This is the piety of divine worship. Blessed 
indeed is the man who thus “ dwelleth in the aid of 
the Most High,” because he “shall abide under the 
protection of the God of heaven.” Which of all the 
creatures under heaven can harm him whom the God 
of heaven wills to protect and deliver ? Now, every- 
thing with power to hurt is under heaven. Under 
heaven are the aerial powers, under heaven is the 
present wicked world, under heaven is the flesh which 
“lusteth against the spirit.” 

Rightly, therefore, is it said, “ He shall abide under 
the protection of the God of heaven,” so that all who 
render themselves worthy of this protection have 
nothing to fear from anything under heaven. And the 
same is true even if we suppose that the sense of this 
first verse depends on the second in the following 
manner, “ He that dwelleth in the aid of the Most 
High, he (that) shall abide under the protection of the 
God of heaven, hfe shall say to the Lord: Thou art 
my Protector.” According to this reading, the words 
“He (that) shall abide under the protection of the 
God of heaven,” are explanatory of the preceding, 


FIRST SERMON ON PSALM XC I4I 


“ He that dwelleth in the aid of the Most High.” Or 
rather the second clause seems to be ampliative of 
the first, as implying that we must seek not only help 
for well-doing, but also protection and deliverance 
from evil. But observe attentively how the Psalmist 
does not say, “ He shall abide in the presence of the 
God of heaven,” but, “ He shall abide under the pro- 
tection of the God of heaven.” The holy angels exult 
in the presence of the Lord ; but it were enough for 
me if only I could abide under His protection. His 
presence inundates them with happiness ; my prayer 
is that His protection may render me secure. And 
He is here called the God of heaven, because, although 
really present everywhere, yet His presence in heaven 
is such that, in comparison with it, He seems to be 
without any presence on earth. Hence we say in the 
Lord’s Prayer, “ Our Father, Who art in heaven.” 
Thus, the soul is in every part of the body, and never- 
theless exists in the head in a more excellent and par- 
ticular manner. For all the senses reside in the head, 
whereas the other members have but one, namely, the 
sense of touch, so that comparatively with her mode 
of presence in the head, the soul seems rather to rule 
than to occupy the rest of the body. In the same 
way, therefore, when we consider the presence of God 
which the blessed angels enjoy in heaven, the presence 
implied in the protection granted us on earth will 
appear hardly worthy of the name. Nevertheless, 
blessed is the man who merits even this protection. 
For it is he that “ shall say to the Lord: Thou art 
my Protector.” But I will reserve this verse for 
another sermon. 


SECOND SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


On THE LorbD’s CARE OF His ELECT. 


“ He shall say to the Lord : Thou art my Protector and my 
Refuge : my God, in Him will I trust.” 

“ He that dwelleth in the aid of the Most High,” 
sings the Psalmist, “ shall say to the Lord: Thou art 
my Protector and my Refuge: my God, in Him will 
I trust.” He shall say this in thanksgiving, returning 
thanks to the mercy of the Lord for the twofold assist- 
ance granted him. For everyone who as yet dweileth 
not in the kingdom, but “‘ in the aid of the Most High,” 
has often need to take flight and sometimes falls. It 
is necessary for us, I say, so long as we abide in this 
body, to flee from the face of pursuing temptation. 
And it happens occasionally, when our flight is not 
swift enough, that we are “pushed” and “ over- 
turned,” as the Psalmist says; “ but the Lord sup- 
porteth us.” He is, therefore, our Refuge, to Whom 
we may run from the demon when he comes to pelt 
the sluggards with filth,* and so we shall escape that 
deep dishonour. He is also our Protector, so that, even 
when we fall, the same Prophet assures us that we 
“shall not be bruised, for the Lord putteth His Hand 
under ’’ us. Whenever, therefore, we feel in our minds 
the violent impulse of temptation, let us immediately 
have recourse to Him, and beg His assistance. But 
if sometimes the enemy surprises us (as happens not 


* Cf. Ecclesiasticus xxii, 1-2. 
142 


SECOND SERMON ON PSALM XC 143 


seldom, because we do not run to the Lord for pro- 
tection as quickly as we should), then, my brethren, 
we ought to beseech Him earnestly to support us with 
His Hand. It is a necessity * for men to fall occasion- 
ally, so long as they live here below ; but whilst some 
are “ bruised ” by their fall, others are not, “ for the 
Lord putteth His Hand under them.” But how shall 
we be able to distinguish these, and to “ separate the 
sheep from the goats,” the just from the wicked, as 
the Lord shall do at the judgment? “For (even) a 
just man shall fall seven times, as it is written.” 
However, there is this important difference between 
the fall of the just man and the fall of the wicked, 
that the former is supported by the Hand of the Lord 
and consequently rises again stronger than ever ; 
whereas the latter when he is fallen “ shall rise again 
no more,” because he falls either into pernicious shame 
or into utter shamelessness. For either he will en- 
deavour to justify what he has done, and this is the 
“shame that bringeth sin,” or he will harden his fore- 
head, so that he now, like the judge in the Gospel, 
“ feareth not God nor regardeth man,” but “ proclaims 
abroad his sin as Sodom.” But when the just man 
falls, he falls upon the Hand of the Lord, and in such 


* That is to say, a moral necessity, due to the corruption 
of our nature. It is in the same sense Our Lord says (Matth. 
xviii, 7), “ It must needs be that scandals come.” Such 
necessity does not, of course, exclude responsibility. The 
Saint, as is evident, is not speaking of mortal sin, at least not 
exclusively, since the falls in question do not prevent a man 
from being just. Hence his doctrine here is identical with the 
teaching of the Council of Trent, according to which no man 
can “‘ during his whole life avoid all sins, even those that are 
venial, except by a special privilege from God, as the Church 
holds with regard to the Blessed Virgin Mary ”? (Can. xxiii, 
de Justificat).—(Translator.) 


144 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


a manner that, wonderful to say, his very fault co- 
operates unto justice for him. For “ we know that to 
them that love God all things work together unto 
good.” And surely our sins “ work together unto 
good ” for us when they render us more circumspect 
and humble.* Is not he who falls upon humility sup- 
ported in his fall by the Hand of the Lord? “ Being 
pushed,” says the Psalmist, “ I was overturned that 
I might fall ; but (the enemy who pushed me gained 
no advantage, because) the Lord supported me.” 
Therefore the faithful soul “shall say to the Lord: 
Thou art my Protector.” Every creature can say to 
Him, “ Thou art my Creator.” The beast of the field 
can say to Him, “ Thou art my Shepherd.” There is 
no human being that may not say to Him, “ Thou art 
my Redeemer.” But to say to the Lord, “ Thou art 
my Protector,” belongs to him alone who “ dwelleth 
in the aid of the Most High.” Hence he addresses 
the Lord as “ my God.” Wherefore does he not rather 
say “ouy God” ? The reason is, my brethren, because 
although the Lord is the God of all men with regard 
to creation, redemption, and the other universal 
benefits; nevertheless each of the predestinate pos- 
sesses Him in his temptations as if Something proper 
to himself. For He is so ready to support every one 
of His elect who happens to fall, and to deliver him 
when he runs for protection, that He seems to be

soling doctrine, and perhaps it is to this Tennyson is alluding 
where he says so beautifully (In Memoriam, i.) :—

SECOND SERMON ON PSALM XC 145 


concerned for him alone and to have abandoned the 
care of His other creatures.* 

Consequently, it is expedient for every man to be 
always attentive to God, not only as to his Helper in 
necessity, but also as to the Witness of his conduct. For 
how can negligence take hold of him who never forgets 
that the eyes of the Lord are upon him? Or how 
can he help regarding God as peculiarly his own, when 
he beholds Him so watchful of himself that He never 
ceases, even for a moment, to consider both his interior 
and his exterior, and to scrutinise and examine as well 
the most secret movements of his soul as his external 
actions? Such a one, clearly, can say with confidence, 
“ My God, in Him will I trust.” And observe that the 
Psalmist does not speak in the past tense, “ I trusted,” 
nor yet in the present, “ I trust,” but in the future, “I 
will trust.” As if he should say, “ This is my desire, 
this is my resolve, this the intention of my heart, 
‘This, my hope, is laid up in my bosom,’ and in it I 
will persevere. ‘ In Him will I trust.’ I will not lose heart; 


* “ Jesus is the most loving of lovable friends—there never 
was a friend like Him before, there never can be one equal

gentle Heart is a throb of intense affection for me, His sacred

who is clinging so lovingly to Him.” (Father William Doyle,

neither will I hope in vain, because ‘ cursed is he that

THIRD SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE SPIRITUAL SNARES AND HUNTERS, AND ON 
THE NECESSITY OF PENANCE AND FEAR. 


“ My God, in Him will I trust. For He hath delivered me from 
the snare of the hunters, and from the sharp word.” 


These words, my brethren, fill me with compassion 
for myself and with sorrow for the miseries of my 
soul. Listen: “ He hath delivered me from the snare 
of the hunters.’’ Are we therefore only beasts of the 
field? Beasts, and no more! For “ man, when he 
was in honour, did not understand: he is compared 
to senseless beasts and is become like to them.” Men, 
consequently, are beasts, wandering sheep, that have no 
shepherd. O man, wherefore art thou proud? Why 
dost thou boast of thy little knowledge? Behold, 
thou art become as a wild beast, so that even the 
hunters set their snares to catch thee. But who are 
these hunters? They are hunters of the greatest 
malice, wickedness, craft, and cruelty. They are 
hunters who sound no horn, so that they may not 
be heard, but “shoot in secret the undefiled.” They 
are “the rulers of the world of this darkness,” most 
cunning in evil and in devilish guile, so that, in com- 
parison with them, the most astute amongst mortals 
is only as a beast before the sportsmen. I except, of 
course, such men as can say, like the Apostle, “ We 
are not ignorant of his (Satan’s) devices.” For to 
these it is given in the wisdom of God to discover the 
deceits of the malignant one. I beseech you, therefore, 


147 


148 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS. 


ye “new plants ” of the Lord, ye whose minds are not 
as yet sufficiently disciplined to discriminate between 
good and evil, do not follow the counsels of your own 
heart, do not ‘‘ abound in your own sense,” lest, being 
still inexperienced, you be ensnared by the crafty 
hunters. The devil openly spreads his net for the 
wild beasts of the forest, that is to say, for the men 
of this world, because he has no doubt that they will 
allow themselves to be easily caught. But you, my 
brethren, are like the prudent stags which slay the 
reptiles and long after the living fountain. For you, 
therefore, the hunters lay more cunning snares, and 
devise more crafty means of deception. I beg of you, 
then, to ‘‘ be humbled under the mighty Hand of God,” 
your Shepherd, and to follow the advice of those who 
are acquainted with Satan’s wiles, taught by the study 
of many years, and by the constant experience of 
themselves and others. i 

I have now explained to you who these hunters are, 
and what the beasts. The next question for discussion 
is the meaning of the snare. I will not excogitate 
any theory of my own, nor will I offer you any doubt- 
ful interpretation. The Apostle himself shall inform us 
what that snare means, for he is well acquainted with 
the hunters’ crafty designs. Tell us, then, O blessed 
Paul, what is the infernal snare which the faithful 
soul rejoices to have escaped ? “ They that will become 
rich,” he answers, “ fall into temptation and into the 
snare of the devil.’’ Consequently, my brethren, -the 
wealth of this world is a snare of Satan. Alas! how 
few do we find to congratulate themselves on escaping 
this snare! How many rather lament that they have 
not been caught in it as fully as they desire, and still 


THIRD SERMON ON PSALM XC 149 


do all in their power to entangle themselves in its 
meshes more and more! But you that have left all 
things and have followed Him Who had not where to 
lay his Head, the Son of man, rejoice and say, ‘‘ For 
He hath delivered me from the snare of the hunters.” 
Confess to Him with your whole heart, with your 
whole mind, and with all your strength; render Him 
thanks with all the power and sincerity of your souls, 
saying each for himself, “ For He hath delivered me 
from the snare of the hunters.” And that you mayunder- 
stand how great a benefit this is, that you “may know 
the things that are given you from God,” attend to what 
follows: “ And from the sharp word.” Oman, or rather 
beast, hast thou no fear ofthe snare? Well, at least be 
afraid of the bludgeon. “From the sharp word.” 
What is this “sharp word,” my brethren, but the 
cries that resound through the place of everlasting 
woe, as the shouts of the hunters when the quarry is 
taken: “ Hither with him, hither with him; strike 
him ; tear him ; kill him at once; take away the spoil 
quickly ” ? What, I ask, is this “ sharp word ” if it 
be not that which we read in the Prophet, “ Take 
away the impious that he may not see the glory of 
God?” * Oh, how the hunters will exult when they 
have run down the poor beast! How joyously they 
will cry, “ Slay him, slay him; pierce him with the 
spits; place him over the fire; put him into the 
boiling pot.” That also was a “sharp word” which

the “ provoking house,” the Jewish people, uttered of 
old, ‘ Away with Him, away with Him ; crucify Him, 
crucify Him.” Oh, how sharp it was, how cruel, how 
horrible! Verily, their “ teeth were weapons and 
arrows, and their tongue a sharp sword.” This “ sharp 
word ” Thou didst endure, Lord Jesus. And wherefore, 
unless in order to deliver us from that other “ sharp 
word” ? Grant us now in Thy mercy that we may 
not have to hear that to save us from which Thou 
hast vouchsafed to suffer so much. 

When we try to persuade worldlings to do penance 
they are wont to answer us, “ This saying is hard and 
who can hear it?” This is what we read in the 
Gospel. The Lord was discoursing on penance, but 
figuratively, because He was speaking to men to 
whom it was not “ given to know the mystery of the 
kingdom of God.” When, therefore, they heard Him 
declaring, “ Except you eat the Flesh of the Son of 
man and drink His Blood, you shall not have life in 
you,” they uttered these words, “ This saying is hard 
and who can hear it?” And many of them “ went 
back and walked no more with Him.” But why 
should we not understand the expressions, “eat My 
Flesh ” and “ drink My Blood,” as an injunction to 
participate in His sufferings and imitate the example 
which He has given us in the flesh? * The same lesson 

* St. Bernard, following the example of many other Fathers, 
is here giving a secondary and symbolic meaning to words which 
refer a and directly to the Holy Eucharist. According 
to A Lapide (Comment. in Joan. vi. 52) he is only developing 
an idea suggested by St. Augustine, De doctrina Christiana, 
1. iii., c, xvi., where the illustrious Bishop says that the command 
to eat Christ’s Flesh and to drink His Blood is a command to 
communicate in His Passion. But elsewhere both the Doctor 


of Grace and the Doctor of Love understand these words of 
the Eucharist. In the short Treatise, De Corpore Domini,

is taught us in the most holy Sacrament of the altar, 
wherein we receive the Lord’s Body ; that, as in this 
mystery, the species of bread visibly enters our bodies, 
so we may know that, by the example of His earthly

“ dwell by faith in our hearts.” For when justice 
enters our souls, He also enters “ Who of God is made 
unto us justice,” as St. Paul speaks. And according to 
St. John, “ He that abideth in charity, abideth in God 
and God in Him.” Nevertheless, many still repeat to 
us, “ This saying is hard.” Hard, then, do they con- 
sider “ that which is at present momentary and light 
of our tribulation,” and “ which worketh for us above 
measure exceedingly an eternal weight of glory” ? 
Is it hard to redeem with a very light and short-lived 
labour those pains and torments which shall endure 
for ever, which no created mind is capable of con- 
ceiving ? Do the words, “ Do penance,” seem a “ hard 
saying ” to you? If so, you labour under an illusion. 


St. Bernard combines the two interpretations thus: “‘ Nisi 
manducaveritis Carnem Filii hominis et biberitis Ejus Sanguinem 
non habebitis vitam in vobis ( Joan. vi. 54). Vis ergo manducare 
Carnem Filii hominis ut habeas vitam aeternam ? Ut dignus 
sis, recollige ea quae pro te passus est, assidua recordatione 
memoriae tuae commenda passiones Animae, et Corporis necessi- 
tates, revolve per singula omnia usque ad mortem crucis, et 
sic exhibe suo exemplo et imitatione, corpus et animam tuam 
‘ hostiam viventem, sanctam, sibi placentem,’ ut te totum ex- 
hibeas Ei sacrificium acceptabile in odorem suavitatis, sicut 
Ipse se totum in tua redemptione expendit. Ultimo vero 
crede firmiter, absque, omni mentis scrupulo sanctum Panem 
et sanctum Vinum super altare secundum formam sanctae 
Ecclesiae verum suum effici Corpus et Sanguinem in manu 
sacerdotis.” Indeed, since the literal interpretation has been 
adopted by three General Councils (the Council of Ephesus, the 
second Council of Nice, and the Council of Trent, s. xiii., c. ii.) 
it is not lawful for Catholics to put it aside. Cf. A Lapide, 
loc. cit. ; also Wiseman’s Lectures on the Blessed Eucharist, 
i. and ii.—(Translator.)

And some day you may hear what is in truth a “ hard 
saying,” yea, a “ sharp word,” and an “ evil hearing ” : 
“ Depart from me, you cursed, into everlasting fire.” I 
say to you, therefore : fear this “ sharp word ” ; esteem 
this a “ hard saying.” So shall you find out for your- 
selves that the yoke of the Lord is sweet and His 
burden light. For even if that yoke should not in 
itself appear sweet to you as yet, you certainly must 
esteem it very sweet in comparison with the bitterness 
of final reprobation. 

But as for you, my brethren, you are those “ winged 
ones ” before whose eyes, as the Psalmist sings, “ the 
net is spread in vain.” You that have renounced 
altogether the riches of this world, being thus delivered 
from the “snare,” what have you to fear from the 
“sharp word” ? You are that happy Iduthun,* to 
whom several of the psalms are inscribed, because you 
have passed through the “snare,” and from the 
“ sharp word ” are fled afar. For to whom will Christ 
address the words, “‘ Depart from Me, you cursed, into 
everlasting fire which was prepared for the devil and 
his angels: for I was hungry and you gave Me not 
to eat ’’—to whom, I ask, are these words to be addressed 
except to those who shall have abounded with the goods 
of this world ? Do not your hearts leap with joy at what 
I am now saying to you, and are not your souls filled 
with sweetness? Does your poverty not appear more 
precious in your eyes than all the treasures of earth ? 
For your poverty it is that delivers you from the 
“sharp word.” How indeed can God require from us

—(Translator.) 


THIRD SERMON ON PSALM XC 153 


what we have voluntarily renounced for the love of 
Him? And nevertheless you do also really accomplish 
what is only an obligation for the rich, since by the 
labour of your hands you feed and clothe Jesus Christ 
in His poor, so that nothing may be wanting to you.* 
Give thanks, therefore, to God, and say, “ For He 
hath delivered Me from the snare of the hunters and 
from the sharp word.” Rejoice, I say, but as yet let 
your joy be tempered with fear. I would have you to 
be always joyous, but—in this life—never secure, 
always rejoicing in the Holy Spirit, yet always fearful 
too, and guarding yourselves against a relapse. 

Is there, then, something which you ought be to 
afraid of still? Yes, my brethren, there is yet one 
cause of fear for you, and that the gravest of all. It 
is the sin of Judas, the sin ofapostasy. You have done 
well in taking wing like the dove and flying away to 
be at rest. For there is no rest in the world, nothing 
but labour, and sorrow, and affliction of spirit. What, 
therefore, can be a source of fear to the soul that has 
taken flight, except that possibly she may perceive on 

* As regards the care for the poor which distinguished the: 
medieval monasteries, it is worth while quoting the following 
testimony from a Protestant historian, Rev. P. Newcome: 
“ Their utility appeared in these respects, that they exercised 
great hospitality towards the poor ; and this was done at one- 
tenth of the expense which the poor now create, by being 
maintained by a legal provision : the monasteries were in general 
the houses of reception for all the sick, who were here nursed 
and cured ; they generally employed masters to teach the poor 
children of the neighbourhood... . In spite of all the calumny 
that was thrown out against them, nothing so well proclaims 
their utility as this : that they maintained themselves in repute, 
some of them one thousand years, and many for the space of 
five, four, and three hundred years ; and that when they were 
dissolved (in England), Edward VI. and his counsellors found 
it necessary to endow new hospitals, build new schools, and

Alban’s, p. viii. (Preface).
