the earth below her a carcass or something similar, 
and drawn down by the desire of it, may be seen by 
those most cruel hunters and caught in the snares they 
have set for her? Then shall her last state be worse 
than her first. This, I repeat, is greatly to be feared, 
lest any of us, either in heart alone, or both in heart 
and in body, should return to the vomit. For we read 
of the children of Israel that they returned in their 
hearts to Egypt. And they might have returned in 
body also were they not prevented by the Red Sea, 
whose waters had come together again after their 
crossing. Yes, my brethren, this is what every one 
of us ought to fear most of all, lest perhaps we should 
ever go so far in offending God that we shall be mani- 
festly rejected by Him and vomited out of His Mouth : 
or if shame, like the Red Sea, prohibits a visible apos- 
tasy, lest tepidity, little by little, should bring about in 
us an apostasy of the heart ; so that under the habit 
of religion we should conceal the hearts of worldlings, 
and greedily embrace whatever of earthly consolation 
we may be able to discover in the cloister. For we are 
not holier than was the Apostle, who yet was afraid 
lest, when he had preached to others, he should himself 
become a cast away. And this fear must remain with 
us until “the snare is broken and we are de- 
livered,” when the soul lays aside this mortal body. 
For the body is itself a snare. Hence we read in the 
Prophet Jeremias, “ My eye hath wasted my soul.” 
Consequently, no man has a right to feel secure so 
long as he carries this snare about with him; but it 
is safer for us all to “ dwell in the aid of the Most 


High” by Whose power alone this snare can be 
avoided. 


FOURTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON WHAT IS MEANT BY THE SHOULDERS OF GOD, AND 
ON THE FouR BENEFITS WE RECEIVE UNDER THEM. 


“ He will overshadow thee with His shoulders: and under His 
wings thou shalt trust.” 


To the soul that humbly confesses and devoutly 
returns thanks for favours received, greater benefits are 
deservedly promised. For does not the Lord say 
that he who “hath been faithful over a few things ” 
shall be placed “ over many things” ? On the con- 
trary, he is unworthy of obtaining anything more who 
proves himself ungrateful for what he has already 
received. Therefore, to the thankful acknowledgment 
contained in the preceding verse, the Spirit now replies : 
“Not only hath thy God delivered thee from the snare 
of the hunters and from the sharp word, but He will 
even overshadow thee with His shoulders.” It is my 
opinion that by the two shoulders of God we are to under- 
stand a twofold promise, namely, “ the promise of the 
life which now is and of that which is to come.” For 
had God only promised the kingdom, and left us without 
the succour which we require on the way, men would 
have cause to complain and to say, “ Great indeed is 
that which is offered us, but the means of reaching it 
have been withheld.” Consequently, He Who has 
promised us a blissful immortality in the life to come, 
in His provident mercy offers us a hundred-fold even 
in this. Now, O man, what excuse remains to 


155 


156 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


thee? Surely now “the mouth is stopped of them 
that speak wicked things.” What more is left the 
enemy wherewith to tempt thee, except the possi- 
bility that thy life on earth shall be long? But even if 
“ thou hast still a great way to go,” wherefore shouldst 
thou fear, since thou hast been given the bread of the 
strong so that thou canst not fail on the road? An 
angel brought food to the Prophet Elias. It was only 
bread and water, the vilest food used by men. And yet 
from this food the holy man derived so much strength 
that he was able to walk for forty days without ex- 
periencing either fatigue or hunger. Dost thou desire 
to receive such nourishing food from angelic hands ? 
It would be passing strange if thou didst not. 

But if thou feelest a hunger for this heavenly bread, 
delay not to solicit the angelic ministrations, not proudly, 
however, but with humble entreaty ; and remember 
what is written of the Lord: how, when the devil 
tempted and urged Him to change stones into bread, 
He refused and made answer, “ Not in bread alone 
doth man live, but inevery word that proceedeth from 
the mouth of God,” and how, after He had vanquished 
the temptations and put the tempter to flight, “ angels 
came and ministered to Him.” Therefore do thou also 
shun the consolations of the world and resist the sug- 
gestions of the demon, if thou desirest that the angels 
should minister to thee. Thy soul must refuse all 
comfort from others, if thou wouldst delight in the 
presence of the Lord. When thou art hungry, the 
devil will tell thee to run for bread : but attend rather 
to Christ, Who has said, “ Not in bread alone doth 
man live.” Wherefore then dost thou distract thyself 
with such a variety of cares (instead of being content 


FOURTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 1597 


with what is barely necessary for the life of the body), 
so that now thou art solicitous as to what thou shalt 
eat, now as to what thou shalt drink, at one time 
thinking of what thou shalt put on, at another of 
where thou shalt sleep—why such manifold solicitude, 
since thou canst discover all these things in one, that 
is to say, in the word of God? For the word of God 
is manna “having in it all that is delicious and the 
sweetness of every taste,” as well as the fragrance of 
every odour. It is repose also, true and perfect, 
pleasing and salutary, holy and sweet. 

This, my brethren, is the promise of the present 
life. But who shall explain to us the promise of the 
life to come? If even “the expectation of the just 
is joy,” and joy so great that there is nothing amongst 
the desirable things of this world worthy to be com- 
pared with it, what shall be the realisation? Hence 
the words of the Prophet Isaias, “‘ The eye hath not 
seen, O God, besides Thee, what things Thou hast 
prepared for them that love Thee.” Such, then, are 
the “‘ shoulders ” of the Lord, and under these shoulders 
four benefits are conferred upon us. For under them 
we have a place of concealment ; under them we are 
protected from the attacks of the spiritual hawks and 
kites, that is, from the aerial powers ; under them we find 
acooling shade which defends us from the scorching heat 
of the sun; under them lastly, we are fostered and fed. 
In another psalm the Royal Prophet says, “ He hath 
hidden me in His tabernacle, in the day of evils,” that 
is, whilst as yet the days are evil and we sojourn in a 
foreign land, which is given into the hands of the 
impious, which is not the kingdom of peace, nor has 
the God of peace reigning in it. For if God already 


158 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


reigns in this world, why do we say in the Lord’s 
Prayer, “ Thy kingdom come” ? It is therefore ne- 
cessary to conceal during our earthly life whatever of 
good we possess, because “ the kingdom of heaven is 
like unto a treasure, which a man having found hid 
it.” This is the reason why we hide ourselves, even 
bodily, in these woods and cloisters. And if you desire 
to know how much we gain by such concealment, I 
give it to you as my firm belief, that there is not a 
single person listening to me, who would not be vener- 
ated as a saint, aye, reputed as an angel, were he to do 
in the world the fourth part of what he is doing here, 
where he obtains no better recognition than daily 
reproofs and reproaches for his negligence. Does this 
seem to you, my brethren, a slight advantage, that 
you are not esteemed as saints before you are saints 
in truth? * Are you not apprehensive lest, if you 
obtain here the worthless wage of human esteem, you 
shall have no reward in the life to come? It is ne- 
cessary, therefore, that you should be concealed, not 
only from the eyes of others, but much more from your 
own. This is what the Lord recommended when He 
said to His disciples, ‘‘So you also, when you have 
done all these things that are commanded you, say: 
we are unprofitable servants ; we have done that which 
we ought to do.” For woe to us if we have not fulfilled 
our obligations! It is beyond doubt high virtue and 
great security to live holily, and nevertheless to attend 
more to what is yet wanting to one than to what one 
seems to have already acquired, and “ forgetting the 


* An allusion, possibly, to the sixty-first of St. Benedict’s 
“ Instruments of good works ” : “Not to wish to be called 
holy before one is really so; but to be so in reality that one 
may deserve the title.” —(Translator.) 


FOURTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 159 


ce 


things that are behind”’ to “stretch oneself forth to 
those that are before.” This, therefore, is the kind of 
concealment which is prepared for us, as I have said, 
under the shoulders of God. And perhaps it was a 
similar shelter, or overshadowing, the Holy Spirit gave 
to Mary in order to hide the incomprehensible mystery 
of the Lord’s incarnation. 

Concerning the divine protection also the Psalmist 
sings in another place, “O Lord, Lord, the Strength 
of my salvation: Thou hast overshadowed my head in 
the day of battle.” For,as the mother-bird, on ob- 
serving the approach of a hawk, spreads out her wings 
in order that her young ones may enter under them 
and there find a safe asylum ; so does the infinite and 
ineffable loving-kindness of our Lord prepare itself to 
shelter us, expanding its bosom, as it were, and opening 
wide its arms. Hence, in a preceding verse, it is said, 
“Thou art my Protector and my Refuge.” But it is 
not alone protection from our enemies we find under 
the shoulders of God : we find also a wholesome shade. 
The corporeal sun which shines in the heavens is very 
useful and very necessary. Yet unless its heat is 
tempered it will injure our weak heads, and its light 
needs to be moderated or it will hurt our delicate eyes. 
This is no fault of the sun’s, but is due to our own 
fragility. It is the same with the Sun of justice. 
Therefore it is said, “ Be not over just.” Not that 
justice is not good in itself, but because it is necessary 
for us, in our present weak condition, that even our 
virtues should be tempered with grace, lest perhaps we 
should otherwise fall into the vice of indiscretion or 
vainglory. For how comes it that, in spite of our 
unceasing prayers and supplications, we cannot attain 


160 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


to that abundance of devotion which we long for so 
eagerly ? Do you think it is because God has become 
either niggardly or poor, either impotent or inexor- 
able? Away with such a thought! No, my brethren, 
but He remembers our weakness and overshadows us 
with His wings. Nevertheless, we must not therefore 
desist from petitioning Him ; because although we shall 
not obtain all we desire, we certainly shall receive as 
much as we have need of; the Lord, whilst protecting 
us from excessive (and therefore noxious) heat, will 
foster us like a mother, and communicate to us the 
warmth of His own Bosom. And this is the fourth 
benefit which I said we obtain under the shoulders of 
God, namely, that we are warmed and cherished there, 
as young birds under the wings of their mother, lest, 
if we wandered abroad, charity should grow cold, and 
thus we should suffer a spiritual death. For “‘ the 
charity of God is (not) poured forth in our hearts 
(except) by the Holy Ghost Who is given to us.” 
Under these wings of His, therefore, “ thou shalt 
trust ” securely, because the bestowal of present gifts 
is a sure pledge of future favours. 


FIFTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE INEVITABLENESS OF TEMPTATION. 


“ His truth shalt compass thee with a shield; thou shalt not be 
afraid of the terror of the night.” 


“Watch ye and pray that you enter not into 
temptation.” Whose words these are you know, my 
brethren, and the occasion you know. For they were 
spoken by the Lord on the eve of His passion. But 
observe that although it was He Himself Who was 
to suffer and not His disciples, nevertheless it was 
not for Him He said they should pray, but for them- 
selves. Hence His earlier words, spoken to Peter, 
“ Behold, Satan hath desired to have you that he 
may sift you as wheat. But I have prayed for thee 
that thy faith fail not : and thou, being once converted, 
confirm thy brethren.” But if the apostles had reason 
to be so much afraid during the passion of the Lord, 
how great ought our fear to be, my brethren, during 
our own passion? ‘‘ Watch ye (therefore) and pray 
that you enter not into temptation,” because you are 
surrounded by temptations on every side, and, as you 
have read in Job, “ The life of man upon earth is a 
temptation.” * Since, then, temptations occupy our 
life in such multitudes that life itself may be justly 


* The Vulgate Version has ‘‘ Militia est vita hominis super 
terram ” (Job vii. 1). St. Bernards reading translates the 
Greek : “ merparhpiov gore ó Bios avôpórov éri tis yns.”—(Trans- 
lator.) 

us 161 L 


162 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


called a temptation, how much prayer and circum- 
spection do we not stand in need of if we would avoid 
falling into them! Therefore in the Lord’s Prayer we 
have the petition, “ Lead us not into temptation.” 
But because thou art thus encompassed with tempta- 
tion, “ His truth shall compass thee with a shield,” so 
that if there are enemies on all sides, on all sides too is 
protection. These words, “ shall compass thee,” make 
it manifest that the shield is to be understood spiri- 
tually, because only by a spiritual shield can one be 
compassed. But truth compasses ; for He is truthful 
Who makes the promise, and what He promises He 
fulfils. ‘‘ Godis faithful,” says the Apostle, “‘ Who will 
not suffer you to be tempted above that which you 
are able.” 

It is not without reason, my brethren, that the grace 
of divine protection is likened to a shield. Shields are 
broad and heavy on top, in order to defend the head 
and shoulders ; but at the lower extremity they become 
narrow, so as to be less of an impediment, particularly 
as the legs are slender and not easy to wound, whilst 
injuries received in these parts are not usually very 
dangerous. According to the same principle Christ 
provides His soldiers with only a scanty and slender 
allowance of temporal goods for the defence of their 
inferior parts, that is to say, their bodies. For He 
does not wish to embarrass them with a multitude of 
earthly solicitudes, but that, like the apostles, “ having 
food and wherewith to be clothed, with these (they 
should be) content.” But for the protection of 
their higher selves, that is to say, their souls, He be- 
stows on them a wealth and abundance of spiritual 
grace. For so we read, “ Seek ye first the kingdom of 


FIFTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 163 


God and His justice, and all these thingsshall be added 
unto you.” The Lord said this with reference to food 
and clothing, concerning which He told us that we ought 
not to be solicitous, because our Heavenly Father, in 
His unspeakable charity, will provide them. And He 
will do so for two reasons: first, lest we should look 
upon Him as our enemy, and thus fall into despair, if 
He refused to supply our temporal wants; secondly, 
lest an excessive anxiety regarding the things of this 
world should prove an obstacle to the interests of our 
souls. For without a sufficiency of earthly goods both 
the service of God and life itself would be impossible. 
But the less of them we can manage with the better 
for ourselves. 

The Psalmist therefore says, “ His truth shall com- 
pass thee with a shield: thou shalt not be afraid of 
the terror of the night ; of the arrow that flieth in the 
day ; of the business that walketh about in the dark, 
of invasion, or of the noon-day devil.” These four 
dangers, my brethren, represent four kinds of tempta- 
tion by which we are surrounded. Hence we have 
need to be surrounded also by the shield of the Lord, so 
that it may protect us on the right side and on the left, 
in front and behind. I wish you would constantly bear 
this in mind, that no one can live here below without 
temptation, and that when one temptation happens to 
leave us, we may always confidently, or rather fearfully, 
expect another,* and that we may only so pray to 
be delivered from them as never to look for or promise 
ourselves perfect repose and security whilst we abide 


* “ Alas! what kind of life is this, where afflictions and 
miseries are never wanting, where all things are full of snares 
and enemies ? For when one tribulation or temptation is gone, 
another cometh.” —Imitation of Christ, Bk. III. ch. xx. 


164 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


in “this body of death.” * And in these combats we 
must admire the merciful dispensation of the divine 
goodness in our regard. For the Lord often allows us 
to be troubled for a long time with certain temptations, 
in order to protect us from more dangerous ones ; and 
from some He delivers us quickly so that we may be 
exercised in others which He foresees shall be more 
profitable to us. But we have to consider (not now, 
however,) what are the four temptations referred to in 
the passage just quoted. For it is my belief that these 
are the principal amongst all temptations, and that 
they attack those who desire to change their lives, in 
the order in which they are there set down. 


* “ Disciple: ‘O Lord God, patience, as I perceive, is very 
necessary for me. .. . For notwithstanding all I can do to live 
in peace, my life cannot be without war and sorrow.’ Christ : 
‘So it is, son ; but I would not have thee seek for such a peace 
as to be without temptations.’ ’’—Imitation of Christ, Bk. III. 
ch. xii. 


SIXTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE FOUR TEMPTATIONS WHICH HAVE TO BE EN- 
COUNTERED BY EVERY SOUL AIMING AT PERFECTION. 


“ Thou shalt not be afraid of the terror of the night, of the arrow 
that fiteth in the day, of the business that walketh about in 
the dark, of invasion, or of the noon-day devil.” 


The word night, my brethren, is often used in Holy 
Scripture to designate adversity. And we know that, 
as a rule, the first obstacle which has to be surmounted 
by those who desire to return to God, is the difficulty 
of bodily mortification. For the flesh, hitherto unre- 
strained, does not suffer itself to be chastised and 
brought into subjection without a struggle ; but, being 
still mindful of its recently lost liberty, it violently 
“lusteth against the spirit,” especially in those 
austerities in which you die daily, in which you are slain 
the whole day long, which are contrary to human 
custom, above the power of human nature, beyond 
human strength. What wonder, then, if such hard- 
ships and privations weigh heavy upon you, and most 
of all upon those who are not yet inured to them, who 
have not yet acquired the habit of turning promptly 
to prayer, of having recourse to pious meditation, in 
order to obtain relief from the “ burden of the day 
and the heat” ? Very necessary to us, therefore, at 
the beginning of our conversion is the shield of the 
Lord, so that we may “ not be afraid of the terror of 
the night.” Observe that the Psalmist does not say, 


165 


166 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


“ Thou shalt not be afraid of the night,” but, “ Thou 
shalt not be afraid of the terror of the night.” And 
very properly, because the temptation is not in the 
affliction itself, but only in the fear of it. For we all 
endure the same hardships, yet not all are tempted 
therein. And they that are tempted, are more troubled 
by the fear of future sufferings than by their present 
tribulation. 

Therefore, since fear is itself a temptation, it is 
rightly said to him whom the Lord encompasses with 
the shield of truth, that he shall not be afraid of 
this temptation. He may be attacked, he may be 
tempted, he may be afraid of the night, but this 
fear shall not hurt him. On the contrary, he will 
sing with the Psalmist, “if it shall have no dominion 
over me, then shall I be without spot, and I 
shall be cleansed.” For those that are “ affrighted 
shall. purify themselves,” as it is written in Job. 
This fear, my brethren, is a furnace; but by the 
power of truth it is prevented from consuming and 
only suffered to cleanse. Fear is indeed night and 
darkness, yet the sun of truth shall easily dispel it. 
For this sun at one time reveals to the eyes of our 
heart the sins we have committed, so that, as the 
Psalmist says of himself, we are “ ready for scourges,”’ 
we “declare our iniquity and take thought for our 
sins ” ; at another, it shows us the everlasting punish- 
ment we have deserved, so that all our present sufferings 
appear to us as so many pleasures in comparison with 
what we have escaped; now it exhibits to our gaze 
the heavenly reward towards which we are advancing, 
and we often recall the words of the Apostle, that “ the 
sufferings of this time are not worthy to be compared 


SIXTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 167 


with the glory to come which shall be revealed in us ” ; 
and now it brings before us all that Christ endured for 
our sakes, so that, pondering on the sufferings borne 
by the Divine Majesty, in order to redeem His worthless 
servants, we may feel ashamed not to suffer even a 
little for our own sakes. 

‘But perhaps truth, especially truth so manifold and 
so encompassing, has prevailed so far-as not only to 
resist the assaults of this fear, but even to drive it 
away altogether. If this be so, for thee “ the night 
is passed.” Therefore as a son of light and day “ walk 
honestly”: but fear the flying arrow. This arrow 
speeds lightly and lightly penetrates; but not light, 
I can assure thee, is the wound it inflicts, for it quickly 
slays. It is, in fact, nothing else but vainglory. Con- 
sequently, the lukewarm and the pusillanimous have 
no need to be afraid of it. But such as appear to be 
fervent and devout, they have good cause to fear this 
arrow, and to be always on their guard, and to be 
particularly careful never to lay aside the shield of 
truth which renders them invulnerable. For what is 
so contrary to vanity as truth? I do not require thee 
to oppose to this arrow of vainglory any recondite and 
mysterious truth: only let thy soul know herself, and 
hold the truth which concerns herself. Unless I mis- 
take, it will certainly be a difficult thing for any man 
in this life to be so deceived by the words of flatterers 
as to become high-minded, if he carefully examines and 
scrutinises his interior by the light of truth. Surely, 
the man who realises the wretchedness of his condition, 
will say to himself in the words of Wisdom, “ Why is 
earth and ashes proud ? ” Surely he who considers his 
own corruption shall be forced to acknowledge that 


168 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


“there dwelleth not in him that which is good.” But 
if perchance he seems to possess something of good, at 
any rate, he shall not be able (as I think) to find an 
answer to the Apostle’s question, “What hast thou 
that thou hast not received ? ” And elsewhere in the 
same Epistle we read, “ He that thinketh himself to 
stand, let him take heed lest he fall.” Finally, if he 
sincerely examines the matter, he will understand 
without difficulty that he will not “ be able with ten 
thousand to meet him that with twenty thousand 
cometh against him,” and that “all our justices are 
as a filthy rag.” It is this same shield of truth that 
must be opposed to the other temptations which follow. 
For the ancient enemy will not give up, even now, when 
thwarted in his second attempt, but will probably have 
recourse to more cunning devices. He has found the 
castle of the soul strongly defended on both sides. It were 
useless to continue any longer his efforts to shake it, 
either on the left side by pusillanimity and fear, or on 
the right by human applause, since such means have 
proved inefficacious So he says to himself: “ It is 
plain to me now that I cannot succeed by open force ; 
but perhaps I may be able to gain my object by craft, 
with the traitor to help me.” Who do you think is 
this traitor? The traitor, my brethren, is cupidity, the 
root of all wickedness: the traitor is ambition, that subtle 
evil, that secret poison, that hidden pest, that deviser 
of mischief, that mother of hypocrisy, that parent of 
envy, that fountain of sin, that instigator of crime, 
that rust of the virtues, that moth of sanctity, that 
blindness of hearts, that vice which changes even 
remedies into maladies, and uses medicine to produce 
disease. “He has despised vainglory,’ says the 


SIXTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 169 


tempter, “ because it is vain. Perhaps he will accept | 
something more solid, perhaps he will accept honours 
or pleasures.” Oh, how many has not this “ business 
that walketh about in the dark” caused to be cast 
into the “ exterior darkness ’’ of hell, having first de- 
prived them of their “ wedding garment,” and robbed 
their virtuous practices of all the fruits of piety! 
What numbers has not this pest wickedly supplanted 
and shamefully cast down, so that ell others, who had 
not suspected the presence of the secret sapper, were 
struck with terror at their sudden fall! But what 
else supplies nourishment to this canker-worm except 
the mind’s estrangement from and forgetfulness of 
truth ? Or what else but truth can discover and un- 
mask the traitor, and expose the “ business that 
walketh about in the dark”? For it is truth that 
says to us, “ What doth it profit a man if he gain the 
whole world and suffer the loss of his own soul ? ” And 
also, “ The mighty shall be mightily tormented.” It 
is truth which by its constant suggestions represents 
to our minds how empty is the consolation of earthly 
honours, how heavy the judgment that awaits them, 
how short is the time they will remain with us, and 
how uncertain their ending. 

These three temptations, namely, the ‘terror of 
the night,” the “ arrow flying in the day,” and the 
“business that walketh about in the dark,” Satan 
tried against the Lord. But he did not care to attack 
Him with the fourth, the noon-day devil, which can 
be a temptation only for ignorance. For he saw that 
Christ possessed extraordinary wisdom, by which His 
answers were so regulated that in none of them was 


170 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


he given the information he wanted.* In the first 
temptation he suggested to the Lord to provide the 
means of satisfying His hunger by changing stones into 
bread. But without telling him whether He had this 
power or not, the Lord spoke of another kind of bread, 
saying, “ Not in bread alone doth man live, but in 
every word that proceedeth from the mouth of God.” 
In the second temptation he urged Him to cast Him- 
self down from the pinnacle of the temple, promising 
that if He were the Son of God He should receive no 
hurt, and the whole city, witnessing the miracle, would 
praise and magnify Him. Yet here again the Lord’s 
answer did not inform him whether or not He was the 
Son of God. The third was a temptation to ambition, 
when the devil promised to give Him “all the king- 
doms of the world and the glory of them if falling 
down” He would adore him. Do you not see, my 
brethren, that the way of ambition is adoration of the 
devil, since he promises his adorers that they shall 
attain to the honours and glory of this world? As for 
the fourth temptation, he did not presume, as I have 
said, to bring that against Christ, Whose wisdom he 
ascertained from the prudence of His answers. 

But how does he act in the case of all others who 
appear to him to love justice and to hate iniquity ? 
In this way: he endeavours to cover vice with the 
semblance of virtue. For those whom he recognises 
to be perfect lovers of good he will attempt to seduce 


* The Saint is supposing that Satan’s object in tempting 
Christ was to discover if He were really the Son of God and 
the Messias ; for at the baptism in the Jordan he had heard 
the voice from heaven saying, “‘ This is My beloved Son in whom 
I am well pleased ” (Matth. iii. 17). Cf. A Lapide, Comment. 
in Matth. c. iv.—(Translator.) 


SIXTH SERMON ON PSALM XC I7I 


by putting before them evil under the appearance of a 
perfect good, so that their very love of virtue will lead 
them to consent the more quickly, and they shall fall 
the more easily, the more rapidly they are advancing. 
In this temptation, therefore, Satan appears not only 
as a demon of the day, but even of the noon-day. 
Was it not through fear of this noon-day devil that 
Mary was “troubled” at the Angel’s salutation? 
Does not the Apostle refer to the same where he says, 
“we are not ignorant of his (Satan’s) devices ” ? And 
in another place he tells us that “ Satan himself trans- 
formeth himself into an angel of light.” The disciples 
also feared this noon-day devil when, seeing Jesus 
walking upon the waves, they supposed it was an 
“apparition,” and cried out in alarm. And notice 
how significantly the Evangelist states that it was the 
fourth watch of the night when the disciples began to 
look out for the fourth temptation. That it is truth 
alone which can strip falsity of its disguises, is, I think, 
so evident a proposition as not to need proof. Diligent 
consideration will easily discover these four temptations 
exercising the Church herself. For whilst yet in her 
infancy, was she not attacked by the “terror of the 
night,” when everyone who slew the disciples of Christ 
thought he was rendering service to God? Then, when 
the persecution ceased and the morning broke, she was 
more grievously troubled and distressed by the “ arrow 
that flieth in the day ” ; because some going forth from 
her, puffed up with the spirit of the flesh and desirous 
of vain and transient glory, sought to make a name 
for themselves by magnifying their own tongues and 
devising various and perverse doctrines. Now, how- 
ever, she has peace from the pagans, she has peace 


172 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


from the heretics, but she has no peace from her own 
false children. O Lord, “ Thou hast multiplied the 
nation, but hast not multiplied the joy,” because 
although “ many are called, few are chosen.” All are 
Christians, and nevertheless almost “ all seek the things 
that are their own, not the things that are Jesus 
Christ’s.” Even ecclesiastical offices and dignities have 
been converted into sources of filthy lucre and given 
over to the “ business that walketh about in the 
dark ” : they are no longer made the means of saving 
souls, but rather of procuring luxury and wealth. It 
is for this many have themselves tonsured, for this 
they frequent the churches, for this they celebrate 
Mass, for this they sing psalms. Nowadays we have 
candidates contending openly for episcopal, arch- 
diaconal, abbatial, and other ecclesiastical offices, in 
order to squander the revenues of the Church on vanities 
and superfluities. It only remains now that “ the man 
of sin be revealed, the son of perdition,” the devil,* 
not alone of the day, but of the noon-day, who “ trans- 
formed himself into an angel of light ” and even “is 
lifted up above all that is called God or that is wor- 
shipped.” For he shall lie in wait for the heel of 
mother Church with all the greater cruelty because he 

* Cf. 2 Thess. ii. It is the common opinion that by Anti- 
christ we must understand not a demon incarnate, or a moral 
person (such, for instance, as the Masonic Brotherhood, or the 
Spiritists), but an individual human being. Indeed, any other 
interpretation would seem to do violence to the language of

christ is to be understood as signifying the whole body of

SIXTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 173 


remembers with bitterness how she has crushed his 
head. This, my brethren, shall be a most terrible 
invasion, but even from this, Christ, Who is the sub- 
sisting Truth, will deliver the Church of the elect, 
shortening, for their sakes, the days of tribulation (as 
He has promised) and destroying the noon-day devil 
“with the brightness of His coming.” Let so much 
suffice on the subject of these temptations, which I 
remember to have treated similarly in one of my 
sermons on the Canticle of Canticles.* For there also 
we had question of the noon-day devil, suggested by 
the noon-day repose of the Bridegroom, the place of 
which the Bride besought Him to make known to her.

SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE PROTECTION GIVEN TO THE JUST AT THE HOUR 
OF DEATH, ON THE MEANING OF THE RIGHT AND 
LEFT HAND, AND ON THE PREFERENCE WE OUGHT 
TO GIVE TO THE INTERESTS OF THE SOUL. 


“ A thousand shall fall at thy side, and ten thousand at thy right 
hand ; but it shall not come nigh to thee.” 


We live in hope, my brethren, and we do not lose 
heart in our present tribulation, because we are looking 
forward to the enjoyment of everlasting bliss. And 
this expectation does not appear to us vain, nor this 
hope uncertain, for it is grounded on the promise of 
eternal Truth. Moreover, the gifts already bestowed 
upon us are a sure pledge of future favours, and the 
graces we have received may be regarded as a safe 
guarantee that we shall also infallibly attain to the 
happiness of the promised glory. For, as the Psalmist 
says, “ The Lord of virtues, He is the King of glory.” 
And in the Office of Lauds we sing, 


“The Father of glory lasting aye, 
The Father of grace invincible, 
With fervent hearts now let us pray 
To shield and guard us from all ill.’’* 


* “ Votis vocemus et Patrem, 
Patrem perennis gloriae, 
Patrem potentis gratiae, 
Culpam releget lubricam.” 


Third stanza of the Ambrosian Hymn beginning “ Splendor 
Paternae gloriae,” and used for Lauds in the winter season.— 
(Translator.) 


174 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 1495 


Also in another psalm, “ For God loveth mercy and 
truth, the Lord will give grace and glory.” Let our 
piety, therefore, manfully sustain the combat in this 
life ; let it be willing to endure with patience all manner 
of persecution. Why indeed should it not consent to 
suffer everything, since, as the Apostle says, it “is 
profitable to all things, having promise of the life that 
now is and of that which is to come’’? Yes, let our 
piety courageously repel the assaults of the enemy, 
because the Lord will be nigh to assist it in the 
struggle and to reward its victory with a most liberal 
hand. “ His truth,” sings the Psalmist, “shall compass 
thee with a shield.” 
And very necessary, my brethren, is the invincible pro- 
tection of divine truth to the soul, not only whilst she 
lives in the body, but also and especially at the moment 
of her departure hence. For if she requires the shield 
of truth during life on account of the perilous combats 
which she has to sustain, she has still greater need of it 
at the hour of death against the last fearful onslaughts 
of the malignant spirits. Thus, when the glorious St. 
Martin lay dying, the hostile power endeavoured to 
injure his most holy soul ; and the cruel beast, “‘ know- 
ing that he had but a short time,” with all the fury 
of his unwearied malice, was not afraid to approach 
him in whom “he had not anything.” Nay, he had 
the unspeakable impudence to draw nigh to the King 
of glory Himself, as we learn from the Lord’s own 
words, “‘ The prince of this world cometh, and in Me 
he hath not anything.” Happy the soul which thus, 
during life, repels the darts of temptation with the 
shield of truth, so that, allowing no taint of poison to 
remain in her, “ she shall not be confounded when she 


176 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


shall speak to her enemies in the gate” of death! 
_ ‘What dost thou here, cruel beast,” cried St. Martin, 
“thou shalt find in me nothing that is thine.” Happy 
he who is so encompassed by the buckler of truth that 
it protects his “ coming in” and his “ going out,” 
namely, his exit from this life and his entrance into 
the next, so that the enemy has no power to hurt 
him in front or behind! For at that awful moment 
(because of the horrible spectres which are wont to pre- 
sent themselves) he is more in need of a strong defence, 
of a faithful guide, and a wise consoler, than he was 
during life of help and protection against invisible 
seducers. 

Therefore, my dearest brethren, “ glorify and bear 
(Christ) in your body.” You shall find Him a sweet 
burden, a pleasant load, a salutary weight, and that 
even though He may sometimes appear to press heavily 
upon you, even though He should occasionally plunge 
the spurs into your sides and use the whip on your back 
to conquer your stubbornness, even though He should, 
from time to time, “ with bit and bridle bind fast your 
jaws ” and hold you tight. This is your happiness. 
Make yourselves, then, as beasts of burden, to carry 
the Saviour, yet not altogether as beasts of burden. 
“Man when he was in honour,” says the Royal 
Prophet, “did not understand: he is compared to 
senseless beasts and is become like to them.” Where- 
fore does the Psalmist here so greatly lament or reprove 
in man a likeness to the beasts ; especially because in 
another place he says of himself, speaking to God, and 
apparently not without some self-satisfaction, “I 
am become as a beast before Thee, and I am always 
with Thee”? It seems to me, I should rather say, 


ce 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 177 


it is manifest to me, that there is a certain kind of 
resemblance to the beasts commendable in man, not 
surely that which consists in the want of wisdom and 
understanding, but that which consists in the imita- 
tion of their patience. The Prophet would certainly 
not be understood to have spoken in anger, nor yet 
in sorrow, had he said, “ Man when he was under the 
yoke of God did not resist, but was as a beast before 
Him.” Who could do otherwise than envy exceedingly 
that poor beast on whose humble back the Saviour 
(wishing thus to commend His own ineffable meekness) 
condescended to sit, if along with the privilege of 
bearing so precious a burden, it had also the power 
to appreciate the honour? Be ye therefore not simply 
beasts, but in some respects as beasts. Bear your 
burden with patience, yet understand what an honour 
it is to be so laden. Reflect wisely and sweetly on 
the priceless worth of the load you carry and on the 
advantage to yourselves accruing therefrom. 

The illustrious martyr, St. Ignatius, who was a dis- 
ciple of “the disciple whom Jesus loved,” and who 
belongs to us in a special manner because his precious 
relics have enriched our poverty, this great man, I 
say, wrote many letters to one Mary* in which he 
addresses her as “‘ Christ-bearing ’’ (Christifera). Could 
there be a more splendid title of dignity or one ex- 
pressive of greater honour? For if to serve Christ is 
to reign, to bear Him must surely be more honourable 
than burdensome. Do you suppose there was any 
occasion to fear lest that ass’s colt, whereon the Saviour 
sat, should fall on the way beneath such a burden ? 

* Not the Blessed Virgin, but Maria Cassabolita, to whom 
the holy Martyr addressed several letters.—(Translator.)

178 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


Or with such a Rider had it any reason to dread the 
attacks of wolves, the ambush of robbers, the steep 
precipice, or other such dangers? Blessed is the man 
who has borne Christ in such a way as to deserve to be 
introduced into the holy city by the Saint of saints? 
There is nothing at all that he need be afraid of, neither 
obstacles in the road nor a repulse at the gate. For 
the holy angels will prepare the way for such a beast 
of burden, as the faithful people did of old for the ass 
that bore the Saviour. Hence the Psalmist sings, ““ He 
hath given His angels charge over thee, to keep thee 
in all thy ways. In their hands they shall bear thee 
up, lest thou dash thy foot against a stone.” However, 
these verses must await their turn: the order of ex- 
position must follow the order of the sacred text. 

“ A thousand shall fall at thy side, and ten thousand 
at thy right hand: but it shall not come nigh to thee.” 
This, as you know, is the verse which I have to discuss 
to-day. In my discourse upon the last preceding text, 
I explained, if you remember, how the shield of truth 
protects us from the four principal and most dangerous 
temptations which we have to encounter in this life, 
namely, from «the terror of the night,” from “the 
arrow that flieth in the day,” from “ the business that 
walketh about in the dark,” from “ invasion and the 
noon-day devil.” But what is said here, ‘‘ A thousand 
shall fall at thy side and ten thousand at thy right 
hand: but it shall not come nigh to thee,” has to be 
referred, as I think, to the future life. And you remem- 
ber, I suppose, the words cited from the Apostle at the 
beginning of the present discourse, in which he tells us 
that “piety is profitable to all things, having promise 
of the life which now is and of that which is to come.” 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 179 


Listen, then, and listen with joy of heart, to what appears 
to belong to the promise of the future life, and conse- 
quently to your hope. “ For where your treasure is 
there (should be) your heart also.” Ican bear witness to 
the attention with which you have heard what appertains 
to the life that now is. But still greater attention is due 
to that which concerns the life to come. For even the 
pseudo-prophet—Balaam I mean, as you understand 
who have read the narrative—even that impious man 
prayed that “ his soul might die the death of the just 
and that his last end might be like to them.” So 
precious are the fruits of piety, so great is the reward 
of justice, that even the wicked and unjust cannot 
help desiring them. Nevertheless, the “songs of 
Sion ” do not sound to advantage amongst the willows 
of Babylon. The true Israelite must therefore hang 
up his harp upon them, and rather begin a lamentation 
“ upon the rivers * of Babylon,” if perchance they can be 
persuaded to join in his weeping. But here manifestly 
is the place for song, where are not wanting those who 
with all alacrity of spirit will dance to the sound of the 
psaltery, to the music of Sion, and who, in the eagerness 
of holy desires, are impatient to fly thither, saying with 
the Psalmist, “ Who will give me wings like a dove 
and I will fly and be at rest ?”’ For what is it to exult 
(exultare) but to leap out of one’s self (ex se ipso saltare) ? 
Those who are still struggling for life in the midst of the 
ocean, tossed upon the waves, buffeted by the billows, 
find but little consolation in the accounts of the sere- 
nity and the loveliness, no matter how great, of the 

* The willows and rivers of Babylon are here taken to 
represent earthly-minded men who are easily swayed by their 


passions and lusts, and live for this world alone. Cf. Bellarmin 
on Ps. cxxxvi. 1-2.—(Translator.) 


180 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


land so far distant, and which they almost despair of 
reaching. In the same way, the worldly soul can dis- 
cover nothing to comfort her in the words which we are 
now examining. For no such soul has as yet merited 
to receive the promise, “ A thousand shall fall at thy 
side, and ten thousand at thy right hand.” Remember 
to whom these words are addressed, to him, namely, 
“ that dwelleth in the aid of the Most High,” and “ shall 
abide under the protection of the God of heaven.” 
Listen, therefore, you who “in thought and desire ”’ 
have already reached the haven of salvation, who, 
having cast out the anchor of your hope, so to speak, 
seem to cling irremovably to that delectable land, and 
who, like holy Job, “ all the days in which you are now 
in warfare, expect until your change come.” I tell you 
confidently that the principal and surest approach to 
the longed-for haven is the manner of life you are living 
here. For a divine vocation and supernatural holiness 
form the necessary preparation for our entrance into 
glory. It may be said that these two things, viz., the 
call of God and our sanctification, constitute a kind 
of bridge, connecting one eternity with another, the 
eternity of predestination with the eternity of 
glorification. I call them eternities, because whilst 
predestination has been bounded by no beginning, 
glorification shall be confined by no ending. But lest 
you should suppose it is only a fancy of my own 
that makes our vocation and justification a bridge 
between two eternities, listen to the Apostle, and 
see if he does not express the same idea when he 
says, ‘‘ Whom He (God) foreknew, He also predestinated 
to be made conformable to the image of His Son.” 
How, think you, or in what order, will He glorify 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 18x 


His elect ? For there is no work of God that has not 
order. Do you imagine you shall pass from predestina- 
tion to glorification by a sudden bound, as it were? 
If not, then build for yourselves a connecting bridge, 
or rather avail yourselves of the one already built. 
“Whom He predestinated,’ continues the Apostle, 
“them He also called ; and whom He called, them He 
also justified ; and whom He justified, them He also 
glorified.” 

This, my brethren “is the way which seemeth 
good” to many. And a good way it is in truth, with 
regard to the end whereof there is nothing to be feared. 
You have no cause to anticipate evil at its term. 
Advance, then, with courage, and all the more eagerly 
in proportion to your confidence that you are now 
nearing the goal. You possess the means already, how 
therefore can it be that you are not approaching the 
end? ‘Do penance,” says the Saviour, “for the 
kingdom of heaven is at hand.” But some one may 
answer me, “ Yes, but ‘ the kingdom of heaven suffereth 
violence, and (none but) the violent bear it away.’ 
It is only through the midst of hostile phalanxes I 
can find access to that kingdom. There are monsters 
to be met with on the road: they fly in the air, 
they beset the paths, and everywhere lie in wait for 
travellers.” Nevertheless, I say to thee: go forward 
with good courage and fear not. Thy enemies are 
powerful, indeed, and numerous. But what of that? 
“ A thousand shall fall at thy side, and ten thousand 
at thy right hand.” They shall fall round about 
thee, and shall never again have the power to do 
thee harm, yea, shall not be able to come nigh 
thee any more. For “the wicked one shall see and 


182 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


shall be angry ” ; but he can only attack thee from 
the side, because the mercy of thy God shall go before 
and follow thee, as I have said, guarding thy going out 
and thy coming in. Otherwise how could any human 
soul sustain the terrific onslaught of the “spirits of 
wickedness,” without being overwhelmed with in- 
supportable fear ? 

What think you, my brethren? If it were allowed 
even one of the innumerable angels of Satan to rush 
at this moment into your midst, and to show himself 
in all his fiendish ferocity and in the hideous ugliness 
of a body of darkness, which of you would have the 
courage or the power so much as to gaze upon him ? 
Not long since, as you well remember, a phantom 
of the night was permitted to terrify one of your 
number, at first in sleep and afterwards when 
awake, and to such a degree that almost during 
the whole of the next day the poor brother con- 
tinued in a state of terror and could not be calmed. 
Yea, you were all of you badly frightened when his 
blood-curdling cries sounded in your ears. You ought 
to feel ashamed that your faith seemed then to have 
slept even as your bodies. This occurrence was 
doubtless permitted for our instruction, in order to 
make us more earnest by reminding us with whom 
“our wrestling ” is, lest at any time we should be found 
either heedless of Satan’s malice or ungrateful for the 
divine protection. For it was the violent pain of im- 
potent jealousy that excited the demon’s inveterate 
hatred of us to such a pitch of fury, whereby he showed 
that he is more fiercely tormented than usual during 
these holy days by our increased devotion and penance. 
It is with the same fury of jealous rage, and even with 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 183 


larger liberty, that he attacks holy souls at the hour of 
death. But he can then only approach them from the 
side (as already remarked) : he is not allowed either to 
assault them in front, with open violence, as it were, 
or treacherously to creep upon them from behind. 
_But not even “ by the wayside ” shall he lay for thee 
a “stumbling-block,” because, as it is written, “he 
shall not come nigh to thee.” And not alone shall he 
not come nigh to hurt thee, but he shall not even 
approach so as to make thee afraid. Thou seemest to 
me to be apprehensive lest perchance an uncontrollable 
terror should seize thee at the view of such horrible 
spectres and so vast a multitude of ghoulish forms. 
But courage! Thou shalt have at thy side a most 
excellent Comforter, a most admirable Consoler, the 
Same of Whom it is written, ‘‘ Before Him the Ethi- 
opians shall fall down, and His enemies shall lick the 
ground.” Plainly, “in His sight the malignant is 
brought to nothing ” ; and thus will He glorify the 
soul that fears Him. Only be Thou at my side, Lord 
Jesus, and let my enemies rush upon me as they please, 
for all their efforts shall be fruitless: let them hurl 
themselves against me from all sides, for they shall 
be beaten back “‘ and perish at the presence of God as 
wax melteth before the fire.” Why should I be afraid 
of enemies who are already vanquished ? Why should 
I fear enemies already in panic ? Why should I tremble 
before enemies already cast down? No, but I will 
say with the Psalmist, ‘‘ Though I should walk in the 
midst of the shadow of death, I will fear no evils, for 
Thou art with me,” O Lord, my God. For as soon as 
the “day shall break and the shadows retire,” the 
powers of darkness shall fall on every side. If even now, 


184 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


whilst “ we walk by faith and not by sight ” amidst 
their malignant and secret suggestions, our faith con- 
quers and triumphs over all the hosts of hell, how shall 
not the clear perception of manifest truth more easily 
vanquish them then, with all their black and horrible 
phantoms? But lest perhaps thou shouldst be dis- 
concerted at their number, lest the very multitude 
of thy enemies should frighten thee, remember how, 
at the first command of the Saviour, a whole legion of 
devils went out from the body of a single man whom 
they had long possessed and occupied, and how without 
His permission they did not dare to enter even the 
swine. Much more, then, shall they fall around the 
departing soul which fights under His leadership, 
however numerous they may be. Yes, they shall fall, 
crying out in confusion and astonishment, “ Who is 
she that cometh forth as the morning rising, fair as the 
moon, bright as the sun, terrible as an army set in 
array ? ” Fearless and undaunted, rather I should 
say, exulting and praising God, ‘‘ thou shalt consider 
with thy eyes, and shalt see the reward of the wicked,” 
never more to sustain their assaults, never more to 
tremble at their fury. 

These remarks, as I think, would have sufficed for 
to-day, were it not for the strong suspicion I have that 
the minds of some of you are still strained in expectation. 
Unless I am deceived, the more reflective amongst you 
are asking themselves why it is that, according to the 
Psalmist, ten thousand are destined to fall at the right 
hand, and only a thousand at the left. I say “ the 
left,” because in my opinion that is what the Prophet 
intends we should understand when he speaks of the 
“side,” especially as he immediately designates the right 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 185 


hand by its proper name. And in truth it is not with- 
out a mysterious significance that we are told so many 
shall fall at the left hand, and ten times as many at 
the right. Nobody, I suppose, will be so dull and 
undiscerning as to think that the words, “‘ A thousand 
shall fall at thy side and ten thousand at thy right 
hand,” are meant to signify an exact proportion, and 
not rather a superabundant excess. At all events, 
that is not my way of interpreting the Holy Scrip- 
tures, nor is it the way of the Church of God. On the 
left hand, then, a thousand shall fall, and ten thousand 
shall fall on the right. And the reason why the enemies 
fall in greater number on the right is, because they are 
wont to attack this side in greater strength and with 
greater fury. If you will now turn your attention to 
the great body of the Church, you may easily observe 
that her spiritual members are accustomed to be more 
violently assaulted than such as are carnal: for it 
seems to me that these two classes of Christians may 
be regarded not unreasonably as representing the 
Church’s right and left sides. Against this mystical 
body the envious malice of the proud demon wages 
unremitting war, attacking more fiercely the more per- 
fect members, according to what is written, “ His food 
is what is most excellent,’* and “He will drink up 
a river and not wonder: and he trusted that the Jordan 
may run into his mouth.” He wages a relentless 
war, I say, against the whole Church, yet not without 
subserving a certain design of divine wisdom, which 
does not suffer even the weak ‘‘ to be tempted above 
that which they are able, but will make also with 


* “ Esca ejus electa.” These words are not found in the 
Vulgate.—(Translator.) 


186 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


temptation issue that they may be able to bear it,” 
whilst furnishing the perfect with occasions of more 
glorious and more numerous victories over the enemy. 
Consequently, the entire Church of the elect shall be 
crowned, because on both sides, right and left, she 
shall have “ lawfully striven,” mightily “ pushing down 
(her) enemies with (both) horns” during her earthly 
career, so that a little later they (the enemies) shall 
manifestly appear falling, a thousand on her left hand 
and ten thousand on her right. Thus of old, when 
David had given proof of superior prowess, and Saul’s 
reprobation was not yet known in Israel, “ the women 
sung as they played, and they said: Saul slew his 
thousands, and David his ten thousands.”’ 

But if you prefer to understand these words of each 
individual member of the Church, according to this 
view also, you can discover, as I think, an appropriate 
spiritual signification by consulting the records of your 
own experience. For you shall find that the “ opposing 
powers ” labour to wound us on the right side with 
for more earnestness and more manifold cunning than 
they show in their attacks upon our left : that they are 
more concerned to destroy our souls than to injure 
our bodies. We know that they envy us all kinds of 
good, material as well as spiritual, and that they 
endeavour to deprive us of both temporal and eternal 
happiness: nevertheless they would be much better 
pleased to see us losing the “ dew of heaven” than 
the “fatness of earth.” As to whether I have spoken 
properly in representing as the two sides of man the 
two substances whereof his nature consists, that, my 
brethren, is for you to judge. But if your verdict 
herein is favourable, I shall have no fear that you will 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 187 


find fault with me for assigning the goods of the soul 
to the right hand and the goods of the body to the 
left ; and the more especially, because it is your con- 
stant care and solicitude never to regard your right 
hand as your left, or your left hand as your right. 
Nor does the Wise Man approve of any other disposition 
than that we should hold “length of days, viz., our 
eternal interests, in the right hand and in the left 
riches and glory,” that is to say, the goods of time. 
It is very expedient for us to know this also: against 
what point the enemy directs his most violent and 
determined efforts. For we have to make the most 
resolute resistance there where the danger is most 
threatening, where the battle rages fiercest, where 
the issue is to be decided, where a shameful captivity 
awaits the vanquished and a glorious triumph the 
victors. 

Therefore, my brethren, it cannot be imputed to you 
as folly that you seem to have exposed your left side 
to the enemy to pummel as he pleases, in order that 
you may thus protect your right with greater diligence. 
For this is the prudence of the serpent, which Christ 
has so earnestly commended and proposed for the 
imitation of all His disciples: to be ready to expose 
the whole body, if necessary, for the protection of the 
head. This is the only true philosophy; this is the 
counsel of the Holy Spirit, that “ with all watchfulness ” 
we should “ keep our heart, because life issueth from 
out of it.” This, lastly, is ‘the grace of God and His 
mercy with His saints” and His “respect to His 
chosen,” that disregarding, as it were, their left side, 
during the time of their probation, He is ever standing 
near to defend their right. Hence it is that the Psalmist 


188 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


testifies of himself, ‘‘ I set the Lord always in my sight: 
for He is at my right hand that I be not moved.” 
And do you not suppose that the Lord likewise held 
the right hand, and the right hand alone, of holy Job, 
since He gave the enemy full power to work his will 
on his flesh and substance? ‘“‘ And the Lord said to 
Satan : Behold he is in thy hand” ; but added, “ yet 
save his soul.” O good Jesus, would Thou wert ever 
at my right hand! Would that my right hand were 
always held by Thee! For I know and am certain that 
no adversity shall have power to hurt meif“ no iniquity 
has dominion over me.” Let my left side, therefore, 
be stripped bare and beaten whilst I live here below. 
Let it be made a butt for injuries and insults. Gladly 
do I expose it, provided Thou, O Lord, dost defend me, 
provided Thou art “my protection upon my right 
hand.” * 

But perhaps we ought to understand the thousand 
who fall on the left hand to be wicked men rather 
than demons. For our human enemies only persecute 
us on account of temporal and transitory goods, which 
out of jealous malignity they hate us for having, or 
rather, from an unjust cupidity, lament that they have 
not themselves. They will endeavour, perhaps, to 
deprive us of the substance of this world, or of the 
favour of men, or even of the life of the body. But 
beyond this the malice of men cannot go: they have 
no power to injure the soul. But the demons, as you 
know, envy us the eternal happiness of heaven; not 
that they have any hope of obtaining it for themselves, 
since, as they are aware, it is irreparably lost to them, 


_ * Cf. St. Augustine’s prayer, ‘‘ Hic caede, hic ure, sed parce 
in aeternum.” —(Translator.) 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 189 


but because they cannot endure to see the “ needy 
lifted up out of the dung hill ” and enjoying the glory 
in which they were created* and have for ever forfeited. 
Consequently their obstinate malice rages against us 
and they are consumed with bitter jealousy at the 
thought that weak human nature is destined to obtain 
what they proved unworthy to keep. And even when 
they endeavour to inflict temporal loss on anyone, or 
rejoice to see it inflicted by other causes, their one 
motive is that the material injury may become an 
occasion of spiritual detriment either to the person him- 
self or to some other : just as, on the contrary, whenever 
men attempt either to induce us to do,or to do them- 
selves, what is prejudicial to our spiritual interests, 
that is not their principal object, but rather some tem- 
poral advantage which they expect shall accrue to 
themselves, or to us, or to somebody else: that is to 
say, they are mainly influenced by the desire of ob- 
taining temporal good or avoiding temporal evil. It 


* Concerning the original state of the angels, St. Augustine 
says similarly, ‘‘ The angelic creature, from the first instant 
of its creation, was made a participant of the divine eternity 
and closely united to God, and by the sweetness of most 
blissful contemplation was raised above all the mutability and 
vicissitudes of temporal things” (Confess., lib. xii. cap. 9). 
In another place (De Eccles. dogm., cap. lix.) he writes, “ The 
holy angels persevered in the happiness in which they were 
created.” Nevertheless, neither St. Augustine nor St. Bernard 
intended to contravene the certain doctrine that the angels 
(just as man) were created in a state of probation. The former 
in his Treatise De corrept. et Gratia, cap. x.-xi., distinctly sub- 
scribes the orthodox teaching, and the same is implied in many 
passages of St. Bernard, especially in cap. viii. and ix. of the 
work De Gratia et Libero Arbitrio. Therefore the glory and 
happiness which they suppose to have belonged to the angels 
at the first moment of their existence, must not be understood 
as an effect of the Beatific Vision, but either as resulting from 
the hope of this, or more probably, as a natural and super- 
natural felicity analogous to that enjoyed by our first parents 
before the fall. Cf. Suarez, t. ii. 569-74.—(Translator.)

may be, however, that a man is able to change himself 
into a devil to such a degree as to wish to behold 
someone whom he heartily hates condemned to eternal 
torments. 

Being thus in so many ways harassed and pursued 
by our spiritual enemies, why are we so wretchedly remiss 
in the care of our souls? Iam ashamed to acknow- 
ledge it, but the greatness of my grief will not suffer 
me to be silent: how many there are at the present 
day, even amongst those who wear the religious habit 
and are bound to the pursuit of perfection, how many, 
I say, shall you now find who seem to have fulfilled in 
themselves that awful imprecation of the Prophet, 
“Tf I forget thee, O Jerusalem, let my right hand be 
forgotten!’’ For by devoting all their care and soli- 
citude to the defence of their left side, they have ac- 
quired much wisdom, but it is the wisdom of this 
world, which they ought to have renounced: it is the 
wisdom revealed,not by the Father Who is in heaven, 
but by flesh and blood, to which they formerly ap- 
peared, like the Apostle, unwilling to “condescend.” 
Hence you may see them eagerly pursuing the goods of 
fortune; rejoicing like seculars over worldly advantages ; 
pusillanimously disturbed by any loss, however small, 
of earthly goods, and contentiously striving for the 
possession of the same ; shamelessly running hither and 
thither ; scandalously engaging themselves in purely 
temporal affairs, as if this were their sole concern, 
this their substance and portion. The peasant, who 
has no more extensive or more valuable possession than 
afew miserable acres, may excusably devote all his atten- 
tion to the cultivation of them. The beggar may conceal 
in his bosom a morsel of bread, because this is the only 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC igi 


kind of ware that would rust in his wallet. But as for 
thee, my brother, wherefore dost thou spend thyself 
on trifles so poor, criminally prodigal of thy labour ? 
For thou hast a richer inheritance than the things of 
this world. Perhaps thou thinkest that it is too far 
away? If so, thou errest. For nothing is nearer to 
us than that which is within us.* But perchance thou 
wilt answer, not that it is too far remote, but that it 
is of no value, and that therefore thou deemest it more 
prudent to strive after a sufficiency of earthly goods ? 
This also is a mistake. The sufficiency thou art seeking 
thou shalt more surely find in the service of God, nay, 
nowhere else can it be found at all. Possibly thou art 
under the impression that the spiritual vineyard requires 
no labour? Or that it wouldnot sufficiently reward thy 
labour? Or at least that it is perfectly safe and not 
needing to be watched by the keeper? If thou enter- 
tainest any such belief, know that thou art harbouring 
a grave delusion. In spiritual matters it is especially 
true that “what things a man shall sow, those also 
shall he reap,” and that “he who soweth sparingly 
shall also reap sparingly, and he who soweth in blessings 
shall also reap in blessings,” so that one man may have 
fruit thirty-fold, another sixty-fold, and a third a 
hundred-fold. ‘‘ But thou hast this treasure in earthen 
vessels,” that is, if thou still hast it. For it seems to 
me thou hast lost it ; it seems to me that it has 
been taken from thee; it seems to me that, in the 
words of the Prophet, ‘strangers have devoured thy 
strength and thou knowest it not’’; it seems to me 
that the reason why thou dost not keep thy heart in 
thy treasure is because that treasure itself has been 


* “For behold the kingdom of God is within you.”— 
Luke xvii. 21. 


192 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


lost. But if it is still in thy possession, then I beg of 
thee, who art so careful of thy other goods that thou 
despisest not the least, who showest so much solicitude 
for the preservation of thy chaff, I beg of thee to 
attend also to the keeping and guarding of thy corn. 
Nay, thou oughtest not to expose thy treasure to any 
risk at all seeing thou art so attentive to thy dunghill. 
For although the dunghill may excite the envy of a 
thousand, perhaps there will be ten thousand enemies 
to envy thee thy treasure, and enemies no less abound- 
ing in cruelty and craft than they are strong in numbers. 
‘A thousand shall fall at thy side,” says the Psalmist, 
“and ten thousand at thy right hand.” Turn hither 
the eyes of thy faith. Perhaps thou shalt discover that 
the robbers have already broken through, perhaps they 
are now engaged in plundering thy house and dividing 
the spoil. Wherefore dost thou keep such vigilant 
guard over thy left side, like a foolishly watchful sen- 
tinel, unless because worldly goods appear to thee to 
be no longer at thy side but before thy face, unless 
because it is these things rather than the Lord thou 
settest always in thy sight, so that he who touches 
them seems to wound, not thy side, but the very apple 
of thine eye? 

Oh, take care, whoever thou art, that thus, neglecting 
thy right side, attendest exclusively to thy left, take 
care lest at the judgment thou shouldst find thyself 
placed with the goats, on the left which thou hast 
chosen. This is a “ sharp word,” my brethren, and no 
wonder it makes you tremble. But it is not enough to 
fear it unless we also endeavour to avoid it. And I 
recall how my Lord Jesus, after all His other unspeak- 
able mercies to me, for my sake also suffered His right 
Side to be opened, because He wished to give me to 


SEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 193 


drink from that Side only, and there to prepare for me 
a place of refuge. Would to God I were worthy to dwell 
like the dove of the Canticle “ in the clefts of the Rock, 
in the hollow place ” of His right Side! But remember 
that Christ did not feel this wound at all. For He 
willed to receive it only after the slumber of death had 
deprived Him of sensibility. This was in order to 
admonish us that, so long as we live, we ought to 
keep wakeful watch over our right side ; and that the 
soul which is so perversely insensible as not to be 
conscious of a wound inflicted here, should consider 
herself dead. We are told that the human heart is 
placed in the left side: there is a special fittingness in 
that, because our affections are ever inclined to the 
things of earth. Nor was the Psalmist ignorant of this, 
since he exclaimed in piteous accents, “ My soul hath 
cleaved to the pavement: quicken Thou me according 
to Thy word.” Neither was the Prophet Jeremias, 
who, however, would not have us to sink under the 
burden of our human condition and heaviness of heart, 
but calls out to us encouragingly, saying, ‘‘ Let us lift 
up our hearts with our hands to the Lord in the 
heavens.” By these words, he is plainly exhorting us 
to transfer our heart from the left side to the right. 
Soldiers of this world, my brethren, are accustomed to 
carry their shields on their left side only. But let not 
us imitate them in this, that is, unless we wish to be 
numbered amongst those who manifestly fight for 
earthly interests and not for Christ. ‘‘ No one,” says 
the Apostle, ‘‘ being a soldier to God, entangleth him- 
self with secular businesses.” As if he should say, 
“ No soldier of God carries his shield more to his left 
side than to his right.” 

Nevertheless, both sides must be protected. For
