194 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


you have not forgotten the words of the Psalmist, 
“ His truth shall compass thee with a shield.” And 
the Apostle also says, “ In all things let us exhibit 
ourselves as the ministers of God . . . by the armour 
of justice on the right hand and on the left.” But 
perhaps it is not the same manner of defence that is 
prescribed for both sides. Let us therefore listen to 
Justice Itself, speaking to us in the Holy Scriptures. On 
the one hand it is said, ‘‘ Revenge not yourselves, my 
dearly beloved, but give place unto wrath,” also, “ In 
your patience you shall possess your souls ” ; and on 
the other, ‘‘ Give not place to the devil,” and, “‘ Resist 
the devil and he will fly from you.” Listen again to 
St. Paul telling you how you may guard both sides: 
“ Providing good things, not only in the sight of God, 
but also in the sight of all men.” “ For so is the will of 
God, that by doing well you may (not only cause the 
demons to pine away with envy, but also) put to silence 
the ignorance of foolish men.” But shall we always 
require this protection? Shall we be always exposed 
on our two sides to the attacks of our enemies ? No, 
my brethren. The time will come when they shall be 
unable to stand upright, not to speak of making an 
attack upon us. ‘‘ A thousand shall fall at thy side, 
and ten thousand at thy right hand.” For the malice 
of men shall then have no power to hurt us, and we 
shall be as little afraid of the legions of devils as of so 
many swarms of worms or flies. We shall gaze upon 
them as fearlessly as the children of Israel, after crossing 
the Red Sea, contemplated the drowning Egyptians 
with their horses and chariots submerged in the waters. 
And we also, but with more joy and security, shall 
“sing to the Lord, for He is gloriously magnified, the 
horse and the rider He hath thrown into the sea.” 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE RESURRECTION OF THE BODY, AND ON THE 
REASONS WHY THE JUST SHALL REJOICE AT THE 
SUFFERINGS OF THE WICKED. 


“ But thou shalt consider with thy eyes, and shalt see the reward 
of the wicked.” 


Sometimes, dearest brethren, my discourses to you 
would be shorter than they are, if the opportunity for 
making them were oftener permitted me. I think you 
must have occasionally noticed this for yourselves: 
that the sermons are brief in proportion to their fre- 
quency. But whenever I am so detained by the malice 
of the times * that—although much against my will— 
I have to desist, perhaps for several days together, 
from the duty of exhorting and consoling you : no one, 
as it seems to me, ought to wonder if I endeavour to 
make up for lost time by prolonging my lectures ac- 
cording asthe interval between them has been wider. 
Let this serve as an apology both for the prolixity of 
yesterday’s discourse and for the brevity of to-day’s, 
to which it is also the preface. For I am afraid some 
of you were dissatisfied with the last sermon as having 
been too long, and that some may be dissatisfied with 
the present as too short ; and that some more may be 
inclined to find fault, not so much with the length of 


* Cf. Matthew vi. 34. By “the malice of the times’’ the 
Saint means the distracting occupations which took up so much 
of his time and often obliged him to leave his monastery.— 
(Translator.) 


195 


196 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


the one or the shortness of the other, as with the suc- 
cession of short and long. But now to begin. 

“ His truth shall compass thee with a shield: thou 
shalt not be afraid of the terror of the night, of the 
arrow that flieth in the day, of the business that walketh 
about in the dark, of invasion, or of the noon-day devil. 
A thousand shall fall at thy side, and ten thousand at 
thy right hand: but it shall not come nigh to thee.” 
I have given you in the preceding discourses the thoughts 
which Truth has vouchsafed to inspire me with in con- 
nection with these verses, explaining how the same 
Truth shields the faithful soul from temptations during 
life, and from perils at the hour of death. The Royal 
Prophet, in another psalm, refers more briefly to these 
two benefits, saying to the Lord, “‘ By Thee I shall be 
delivered from temptation, and through my God I 
shall go over the wall,” because, namely, with God for 
his Guide, he could walk through life without any fear 
of stumbling, and should meet with no obstacle at his 
departure hence. In the first part of this verse, there- 
fore, the Psalmist promises himself a frequent deliver- 
ance out of the hands of his enemies, and in the second 
full and perfect security. As for the verse which comes 
next in the psalm I am expounding, viz., “ But thou 
shalt consider with thy eyes and shalt see the reward 
of the wicked,” I believe these words contain a promise — 
of no little happiness. ‘‘ A thousand shall fall at thy 
side, and ten thousand at thy right hand ; but it shall 
not come nigh to thee. But thou shalt consider with 
thy eyes, and shalt see the reward of the wicked.” 
Such is the promise. Let it be so, I pray Thee, O Lord, 
let it be so. “ Let them be confounded that persecute 
me, and let not me be confounded ; let them be afraid, 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 197 


and let not me be afraid ” ; yea, and let them fall also, 
but let me stand fast. 

It appears to me, my brethren, that here, in this 
short verse, the Prophet is manifestly proclaiming the 
immortality of the soul and confuming our faith in a 
bodily resurrection. For he clearly intimates that I 
shall survive to see the downfall of the demons, and 
shall contemplate with these eyes of flesh their final 
retribution. For it is not said simply, “‘ Thou shalt 
consider with eyes,” but, “ Thou shalt consider with 
thy eyes,” * with those very eyes which now “languish 
through poverty ” and “ fail whilst I hope in my God.” 
And in truth, brethren, our eyes do fail us whilst we 
gaze in hope. ‘‘ For what a man seeth, why doth he 
hope for?” as the Apostle says ; because “‘hope that 
is seen is not hope ” at all. Therefore with those same 
eyes which now thou dost not presume so much as to 
lift up to heaven, with those same eyes which so often 
at present overflow with tears and are worn away with 
multiplied sorrows, it is with those very eyes, I say, 
“thou shalt consider and shalt see the reward of the 
wicked.” Think not that new organs of sight shall be 
given thee ; no, but the former shall be restored. But 
wherefore do I speak of the eye, which, although the 
smallest is nevertheless the noblest and most useful of 
our bodily members? For “ this my hope is laid up 
in my bosom,” that, according to the promise of Truth 
Itself, not a single hair of our heads shall perish. 

But perhaps the reason why it is especially promised 
us that we shall see with our eyes is this, because here 

* “Non enim simpliciter oculis, sed oculis, ait, tuis con- 
siderabis.”” According to the Latin idiom, the explicit use of 


the possessive pronoun, where (as here) it is already implied 
in the context, expresses some degree of emphasis.— (Translator.) 


198 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


below it seems to be the sovereign desire of the human 
soul to see good things. Hence the Psalmist says, “I 
believe to see the good things of the Lord in the land 
of the living.” That is, he desires to have the upper 
windows of his body opened wide to the light of God, 
longing to walk by sight rather than by faith. “ Faith 
(as you know) cometh by hearing,” not from sight. 
According to St. Paul, it is ““ the substance of the things 
to be hoped for, the evidence of the things that appear 
not.” Consequently, in faith, just as in hope, the eye 
fails us, and it is the ear alone that can profit us. “‘ The 
Lord God hath opened my ear,” says the Prophet Isaias. 
But the day will come when He shall also open our eyes. 
The time will come when no longer shall it be said to 
the soul, ‘‘ Hearken, O daughter, and see, and incline 
thine ear ” ; but rather, “ Lift up thine eyes and be- 
hold.” Behold what? Surely the treasures of joy and 
exultation which the Lord God shall plentifully pour 
out upon her. What? Not alone those good things, 
which although they cannot be seen in this life, can 
at least be heard of and believed; but also those 
whereof the Apostle says, “‘ eye hath not seen, nor ear 
heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man 
(to conceive) what things God hath prepared for them 
that love Him.” For at the resurrection, the eye shall 
be able to take in more than the ear, or even the mind 
takes in at present. It is, as I think, on account of the 
soul’s most ardent desire to see what she already has 
heard of and believes, that another illustrious herald of 
the future resurrection has also made particular re- 
ference to the eyes: “ I shall be clothed again with 
my skin,” says holy Job, “and in my flesh I shall see 
my God; Whom I myself shall see and my eyes shall 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 199 


behold, and not another ; this my hope is laid up in my 
bosom.” 

Perhaps, however, there is in these words of the 
Patriarch, “ and my eyes shall behold,” as also in those 
of the Psalmist, “ Thou shalt consider with thy eyes,” 
a special significance, which requires to be examined 
more attentively. Do you suppose, my brethren, that 
the eyes with which I now see are truly mine? No, 
evidently they are not. Once, indeed, they appeared 
to be mine, because they also, as you know, belonged 
to the portion of the paternal inheritance which I, 
the younger son, received of my Father and so badly 
administered. For I soon wasted and squandered it 
all. The law of sin occupied my members, and death 
freely entered in through my windows, because I had 
now become the slave of sin and death. Yea, I was 
truly a miserable slave, not to a human master, but to 
foul and disgusting swine. For I did not even serve in 
the condition of a hired labourer, but absolutely as a 
thrall. How indeed can it be thought that I received 
wages, since even food was denied me, and such food 
as was worse than the hunger? For when I would 
fain have filled myself “with the husks the swine did 
eat, no man gave unto ” me; so that although I lived 
with the swine, I was not allowed to feast with the 
swine. Moreover, how could that eye have been truly 
mine which “hath wasted my soul?” ? Thus, I was in 
a manner compelled at last to return His gift to the 
Giver, in order that He might recover from the usurping 
tyrant what I had proved unable to defend. 

Consider more carefully, dearest brethren, and ponder 
well by whose power or by what power you have 
escaped the intolerable yoke of the spiritual Pharao, so 


200 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


that “ you no longer yield your members as instruments 
of iniquity unto sin,” and sin no longer “ reigns in your 
mortal body.” It certainly was not by your own. No, 
“the Right Hand of the Lord hath wrought strength.” 
It has been His doing, to Whom alone nothing is im- 
possible. Do not say, then, “ our mighty hand, and not 
the Lord, hath done all these things,” but make the 
true and salutary acknowledgment that all is due to 
the grace of God. And let no one doubt that he must 
be constantly on his guard, lest, whilst still the days 
are evil and there is no security for man, he should 
ever presume to entertain the thought of demanding 
back his ‘‘ portion of substance ” from the keeping of 
so kind and provident a Trustee, and of resuming a 
perilous, or rather a pernicious freedom of administra- 
tion. If the Father is jealous, it is of our interests He 
is jealous. Neither is it out of envy, but according to 
His loving providence, that He commands us to leave 
the whole inheritance in His own care ; for He knows 
well that we should lose it. But when we shall have 
arrived at that great and holy city whose borders ‘‘ He 
hath laid out in peace,” where no hostile incursions 
need be dreaded, then, my brethren, not only will He 
restore us to ourselves, but He will give us Himself in 
addition. In the meantime, let us turn away from 
our own wills, and let us never be so rash as to usurp 
dominion over the members we have consecrated to 
God, knowing that it is a very grave sin of sacrilege 
to use what has been solemnly devoted to piety for the 
purposes of vanity, curiosity, pleasure, or for any other 
such ungodly ends. “ Know you not,” says the Apostle, 
“that your members are the temple of the Holy Ghost, 
Who is in you, Whom you have from God, and you are 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 201 


not your own?” Again he says, “ The body is not for 
fornication.” Then for what isit? Thinkest thou, my 
brother, that it is for thee ? Let it be so, by all means, 
let thy body be at thine own free disposal, provided, 
however, that thou canst, by thine own strength, either 
deliver it from the yoke of passion, or at least preserve 
it after it has been delivered. But if thou canst not 
do this, nay, rather because thou canst not do this, 
St. Paul tells thee that thy ‘‘ body is not for fornica- 
tion,’ nor for thee, but for the Lord’’; and that it 
should now « serve justice unto sanctification,’ lest it 
become again the slave of corruption and its “last 
state be worse than the first.” “I speak a human 
thing,” says the same Apostle in another place, ““ be- 
cause of the infirmity of your flesh. For as you have 
yielded your members to serve uncleanness and iniquity 
unto iniquity, so now yield your members to serve 
justice unto sanctification.” But, as he tells us himself 
in this very passage, it is only on account of our present 
weakness that he gives us this injunction. For when 
that which “is sown in weakness shall rise in power,” 
there shall then be no need of serving. Why should we 
have any doubt that God will restore us to ourselves 
there where liberty can be enjoyed with security, and 
security can dispense with precaution? Why should 
we not believe that, since the great Father of the family 
will then place the faithful servant over all His goods, 
much more He will give him back his liberty ? Therefore 
“thou shalt (then) consider with thy eyes and shalt 
see the reward of the wicked,” provided that thou dost 
sincerely and practically acknowledge that those eyes 
belong to God during the present life rather than to thee. 
For even apart altogether from the inviolable obligation 


202 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


of thy vow (whereof I have been speaking) by which, 
renouncing thine own will, thou hast consecrated to 
the service of God that body thou couldst not of thy- 
self deliver from the slavery of sin: independently, I 
say, of that engagement, how canst thou consider as 
thine own those members in which the opposing law of 
sin still dwells at least, even if it does not reign ; and 
in which thy second enemy, the penalty of sin, not 
only dwells but even has the upper hand and rules 
with unchallenged sway? Dost thou regard as thine 
own that body which “is dead because of sin,” or as 
belonging to the soul which it “ weigheth down ” un- 
ceasingly ? But if anyone wishes to call it his own, there 
seems to be only one sense in which he can truly do so: 
that is to say, he may call it his own burden or his own 
prison. Otherwise, how, for instance, canst thou call the 
eyes wherewith thou seest thine own, during this life at 
any rate, since, whether thou willest or not, they are so 
often overpowered by sleep, dimmed by smoke, injured 
by dust, clouded by noxious humours, tortured by sharp 
pain, and finally extinguished by the death of the body ? 
But hereafter they shall indeed be thine, when none 
of these things shall exist any longer to trouble them. 
Then, at last,“ thou shalt consider with thy eyes,” 
and use them according to thy pleasure, with equal 
freedom and security. For then thou shalt have no 
need to say, ‘“‘ Turn away my eyes that they may not 
behold vanity,” because they shall contemplate Truth 
in all its purity. Much less needest thou fear lest death 
should enter in through thy windows, for death itself 
shall then, at last, have been destroyed. Perhaps, 
however, thou art afraid that thy eyes shall be 
dazzled by such an excess of light, where each of the 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 203 


just shall shine as the sun? Good reason in truth 
shouldst thou have for this fear, were not thy eyes, 
with thy other bodily members, destined to share in 
the glory of the resurrection. 

“Thou shalt consider with thy eyes, and shalt see 
the reward of the wicked.” That, my brethren, will 
no doubt be a bitter torment for the damned, the 
crown and consummation of all their evils. For we may 
suppose that it would be some consolation to them if, 
in their sufferings, they could escape the knowledge, or 
at least avoid the gaze of those whom in life they had 
maliciously persecuted. But, it may be asked, if our 
contemplation of their misery adds so materially to 
their punishment, what is its necessity to us, or what its 
use? What addition can it make to our happiness ? 
For surely there can be nothing more impious, more in- 
human, more abominable, than the wish to feast one’s 
eyes on the torments of another, no matter how 
inimical, no matter how depraved, and to take pleasure 
in the contemplation of his anguish ? Nevertheless, just 
as “the wicked shall see and shall be angry, he shall 
gnash his teeth and pine away ” (for the blessed shall 
be called up to heaven before the reprobate are cast 
down into the dungeons of everlasting fire, in order 
that the latter may be more fiercely tormented by the 
sight of the glory they have lost): so “ the just shall 
see and shall rejoice,” beholding the misery from which 
they have escaped. Consequently, in that terrible and 
final separation, on the one hand, the sight of the sheep 
shall fill the goats with most bitter jealousy ; and on 
the other, the elect shall discover in the doom of the 
reprobate the most powerful motives for thanksgiving 
and praise. For, plainly, the thanksgiving of the just 


204 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


would not be so magnificent, unless, along with the 
inconceivable happiness which they enjoy, they also 
beheld “the reward of the wicked,” from a partici- 
pation in which (as they faithfully and gratefully 
remember) it is only the pure mercy of the Redeemer 
that has saved them. Neither would the wicked be 
consumed with such envious rage, if they did not see 
the just ascending before their eyes to the kingdom of 
unutterable bliss, whilst they groan to hear themselves 
condemned to the stench, the horror, and the everlasting 
fires of the infernal pit, and to all the long agonies of a 
death that is immortal. ‘‘ There,” says the Saviour, 
“shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth ” : weeping, 
because of the fire that shall never be extinguished ; 
gnashing of teeth, because of the worm that dieth not ; 
weeping from profitless grief; gnashing of teeth from 
impotent fury: weeping from an excess of suffering ; 
gnashing of teeth from the violence of wasting envy, 
and the obstinancy of unrepented malice. To this end, 
therefore, “thou shalt see the reward of the wicked,” 
in order that thou mayest lose even the power to show 
thyself ungrateful to thy Redeemer, by the sight of 
the awful danger out of which He has delivered thee. 
Yet this is not the only benefit the just shall derive 
from seeing the reward of the wicked. The same sight 
shall give them at last perfect security. For they shall 
no longer have cause to fear anything from the malice 
either of wicked men or of apostate angels, after 
beholding them falling, a thousand at their side, and 
ten thousand at their right hand, and not alone falling, 
but falling into hell. Do you think, brethren, that 
they can be any sooner delivered from all apprehension, 
from all suspicion of that old serpent, “‘ more subtle 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 205 


than any of the beasts of the earth,” particularly 
when they remember how he seduced the first woman 
in paradise—do you think there can be perfect security 
for the just until they see him, now with his whole 
body, thrust down into the avenging flames, and 
separated from them by an impassably wide abyss ? 
There is yet another benefit which shall accrue to 
thee from the contemplation of the “reward of the 
wicked.” It is this, that thine own dignity and glory 
will appear ali the greater in thine eyes from the con- 
trast with the ignominy and ugliness of the reprobate. 
For contrary qualities when juxtaposed appear each to 
receive an access of intensity. Thus if I place side by 
side a black object and a white object, the former will 
seem to have become blacker than before, and the latter 
whiter. But I will offer you a more reliable testimony 
to this from the Psalmist : ‘‘ The just shall rejoice,” he 
says, “‘ when he shall see the revenge.” And wherefore ? 
Because “he shall wash his hands in the blood of the 
sinner.” Manifestly, then, he shall not stain but rather 
cleanse his hands in the sinner’s blood; so that what 
defiles the one purifies the other, and that which makes 
the one more loathsome renders the other more beautiful. 
Of the three reasons I have assigned why the just 
shall rejoice in the punishment of the wicked, there is 
none, I believe, which can appear repellent to our human 
instincts, even in our mortal condition. Yet it is not 
from any of these motives that Divine Wisdom will 
laugh at the sinner’s destruction, as most certainly 
He will. For He Who cannot lie has plainly predicted 
this, saying, ‘‘ Because I called and you refused; I 
stretched out My hand, and there was none that re- 
garded .. . I also will laugh in your destruction, and 


206 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


will mock when that shall come to you which you feared, 
when sudden calamity shall fall on you, and destruction, 
as a tempest, shall be at hand.” Now, what can there 
be pleasing to Wisdom in the ruin of the unwise, except 
His own most just disposition and most righteous 
ordinance? And evidently that which shall give 
pleasure to Wisdom, shall also of necessity please each 
of the wise. Therefore, think it not strange that it is 
promised thee, ‘‘ Thou shalt consider with thy eyes, 
and shalt see the reward of the wicked,” since thou 
shalt even laugh at their destruction. Not that, from a 
certain monstrous cruelty, thou shalt find pleasure in 
contemplating the mere torments of the damned ; but 
because the beautiful order established by divine pro- 
vidence will produce an inexpressible delight in him 
who has zeal for justice and the love of equity. For, 
where, under the light of uncreated Truth, thou shalt 
clearly and perfectly behold everything arranged in the 
most admirable order, his own place assigned to every- 
one, or rather everyone assigned to his own place, how 
canst thou help feeling a complacency in all things, 
and magnifying in all things the great Disposer of all? 
Very appropriately, therefore, did the Apostle Peter 
say of the “son of perdition ” that he went to “his own 
place,” because he “burst asunder in the midst ” in 
the air, a confederate of the aerial powers, as if heaven 
had refused to receive and earth to support the be- 
trayer of Him Who, as being true God and true man, 
had come down from heaven to “ work salvation in the 
midst of the earth.” 

Accordingly, ‘‘ thou shalt consider with thy eyes and 
shalt see the reward of the wicked.” And this spec- 
tacle shall enhance thy happiness ; firstly, by showing 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 207 


thee what thou hast escaped ; secondly, by confirming 
thee in absolute security ; thirdly, by the contrast with 
thine own blissful state; fourthly, by satisfying thy 
burning zeal for justice.* For then it will be no longer 
the time for showing mercy, but for exercising justice ; 
nor shall any compassion be felt for the impious then 
or ever after, since there shall be no longer any hope 
of their amendment. We shall have then laid aside 
that softness of our human infirmity which, during the 
present life, charity uses for the salvation of souls, 
gathering together in the ample folds of its wide- 
stretching net good fish and bad, that is to say, both 
the pleasant and the painful emotions of the mind. 
But this only happens whilst the net is in the sea. 
For as soon as it is drawn ashore shall be verified 
what is written, “they chose out the good, but 
the bad they cast forth.” By this is meant that 
after the present life, charity shall indeed still “rejoice 
with them that rejoice,” but certainly it can no more 
“weep with them that weep.” Otherwise how shall 
we judge this world (according to the Saviour’s promise) 
unless forgetting that affection of tender sympathy, we 
are “‘ brought into the cellar of wine’’? t} Hence the 
Psalmist says, “ I will enter into the powers of the 
Lord : O Lord, I will be mindful of Thy justice alone.” 
Even in this life a human judge is not permitted to 
make allowance for the poverty of the culprit, or to 
show compassion for him. All such feelings of sym- 
pathy have perforce to be suppressed, no matter what 


* The same reasons are assigned by St. Thomas, Sum. Theol., 
III. Supplem., q. xciv. a. 3. Cf. also a. 1 and 2.—(Translator.) 

tł That is to say, we shall be filled with holy zeal, of which 
wine is the symbol. See Sermon XLIX. on the Canticle of 
Canticles.—(Translator.) 


208 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


the effort may cost us, and the sentence must be pro- 
nounced in accordance with the requirements of strict 
justice. How much more, then, shall that be fulfilled 
which is written, ‘‘ Their judges, joined to the Rock, have 
been swallowed up,” there where no repugnance of mind 
shall be felt, no disrelish for duty experienced? Then 
indeed the judges shall be swallowed up altogether in 
a fiery zeal for justice, and shall imitate the solidity of 
the Rock (Christ) to Whom they are joined. “‘ Joined 
to the Rock” they truly are, since they “have left 
all things and followed ” Him alone. For this is what 
the Rock Himself said in reply to Peter’s question as 
to how He would reward His disciples, ‘‘ I say to you, 
that you who have followed Me, in the regeneration, 
when the Son of Man shall sit on the seat of His Majesty, 
you also shall sit on twelve seats, judging the twelve 
tribes of Israel.” And the same is predicted by the 
Prophet Isaias where he announces that “ The Lord will 
come to judgment with the ancients of His people.” 
Does it seem to you, my brethren, that anything soft 
or flexible shall be found in the judges when thus 
“joined to the Rock’? “He who is joined to the 
Lord,” says the Apostle, “is one spirit,” to which I 
will add, he who is joined to the Rock is one stone with 
Him. It is for this union with the Rock the Psalmist 
yearns whenhe cries out, “‘ It is good for me to adhere to 
my God.” In this way, therefore, “their judges, joined 
to the Rock, have been swallowed up.” Oh, what a 
grace of sweet familiarity! What an eminence of 
honour! What a precious ground of confidence! What 
a singular motive for perfect security ! 

But can there be conceived anything so terrible, 
anything so full of anxiety and killing agitation, as to 


EIGHTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 209 


stand for judgment before that awful tribunal and to 
await the still uncertain sentence from a Judge of such 
rigorous justice? St. Paul tells us that “ it is a fearful 
thing to fall into the hands of the Living God.” O my 
brethren, let us judge ourselves now in this life. Let 
us strive by an anticipated judgment to escape that 
dreadful suspense and uncertainty. God will not 
judge what has been judged already. And just as 
“some men’s sins are manifest, going before to judg- 
ment,” so too is it with the piety of other men. And 
therefore also, just as the former, without awaiting the 
Judge’s sentence, are drawn down into hell by the 
sheer weight of their own crimes: so the latter, with 
perfect liberty of spirit, unhesitatingly ascend the 
thrones prepared for them. Oh, how happy is the 
poverty of those who leave all voluntarily, in order to 
follow Thee, Lord Jesus! Happy and blessed it surely 
is, since it can render us so secure, yea, and so glorious 
amid the ruins of a crashing world, at that terror- 
inspiring scrutiny of merits, before that array of 
impartial judges ! 

But let us now hear what answer the faithful and 
loving soul makes to promises so magnificent, lest 
perhaps she should show herself either diffident or 
possessed of a confidence that is not expedient. She 
says, therefore: “ Because Thou, O Lord, art my 
hope.” What answer could be more modest or more 
pious? Nor could she receive any more fitting rejoinder 
than the words which immediately follow : “‘ Thou hast 
made the Most High thy refuge.” 

But I have to ask your forgiveness, brethren. For 
I think I have to-day also somewhat exceeded the 
bounds of my promised brevity.

NINTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE ADVANTAGE OF LIVING FOR GOD ALONE, 
AND PUTTING ALL OUR HOPE IN HIM. 


« Because Thou, O Lord, art my hope : thou hast made the Most 
High thy refuge.” 

On this occasion also, my brethren, you shall hear 
something concerning the promise of the Father, the 
expectation of the children, the end of our present 
exile, the reward of our labour, and the fruit of our 
captivity. Hard indeed is our captivity, not only that 
which we endure in common with all men, and which is 
the general condition of the human race ; but also that 
whereby, in order the more effectually to mortify our 
wills and to lose our lives in this world, we have bound 
ourselves with the fetters of so rigorous a rule and 
condemned ourselves to a prison of such severe ascetic- 
ism. Surely a most miserable bondage, yet only if 
suffered against our will, instead of by free election. 
As the case is, however, we have voluntarily sacrificed 
our liberty to God, and there is no other violence done 
to our will than that which comes from the will itself. 
But clearly there must be some cause for this. So 
there is, my brethren, and a cause than which none 
greater can be conceived. I mean the love of God. 
Does it seem to you that anything which is done or 
suffered for the love of God, however great, how- 
ever laborious it may be, ought to excite our com- 
passion? But if occasionally the magnitude of the 
tribulation awakens in our souls a feeling of pity, 


210 


NINTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 211 


that sentiment is soon changed to congratulation by 
the remembrance of the motive. Besides, you do not 
need to be told that all our good works are done not 
only for God, but also by God. “For it is God Who 
worketh in you both to will and to accomplish, according 
to His good will.” * He is, therefore, the Author of the 
good we do, just as He is its Rewarder and its whole 
Recompense. Hence that sovereign Good, although in 
Himself absolutely simple, appears to bein us a double 
Cause of virtuous actions, namely, final Cause and 
efficient. Rightly, then, does the Apostle say, not 
only that we persevere in our life of multiplied labours, 
but also that “in all these things we overcome be- 
cause of Him That hath loved us.” But is it not 
by Him also that we overcome? Undoubtedly. ‘‘ For 
as the sufferings of Christ abound in us, so also by 
Christ doth our comfort abound.”’ 

The expression, ‘‘ For God’s sake,” is common enough 
and familiar enough ; and, although often idly uttered, 
it is an expression full of profoundest meaning. The 
words are constantly on the lips of men, even of those 
whose hearts are strangers to the sentiment they 
express. For whenever anyone now solicits alms or 
any other kind of assistance, it is always for God’s 


* This, of course, must not be understood in a sense which 
would exclude the free co-operation of our own wills with the 


divine action. ‘‘It is usual with the inspired authors,” says 
St: Augustine, “‘ to represent God as doing what the creature 
does by His grace. .. . What thou dost by His grace, He is 


said to do, because without Him thou wouldst not do ít” 
(In Ps. lii. n. 5). And commenting on the passage before us, 
he says, “ God worketh in us both to will and to accomplish, 
not because we do not will ourselves and accomplish ourselves, 
but because without His help we should neither will nor ac- 
complish anything of good ”’ (De Gratia Christi, c. xxv.). That 
this is also St. Bernard’s doctrine is manifest from his work 
De Gratia et Libero Arbitrio, c. iii—(Translator.) 


212 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


sake. And some are even ready to ask things for God’s 
sake which are not according to God, which they do 
not desire for the glory of God, but rather for the pur- 
pose of offending God. Nevertheless, these words are 
truly “living and efficacious” (as is every word of 
God) when, instead of being uttered thoughtlessly, or 
conventionally, or from mere habit, or as a means of 
persuasion, they proceed (as they ought) from the 
fulness of devotion and from a pure intention of the 
mind. My brethren, ‘“‘ the world passeth away, and 
the concupiscence thereof ’’ ; and when the world shall 
fail, then we shall realise how unprofitable and insecure 
is everything that is done for its sake. For when the 
foundation itself lacks stability, how can it render 
stable any of the things which it appears to support ? 
Therefore the Apostle warns us that “he who soweth 
in his flesh, of his flesh also shall reap corruption ”’ ; 
because, namely, “all flesh is grass, and the glory 
thereof as the flower of the field.” But when the grass 
withers, its flower must necessarily fade and fall. He 
Who alone is, is alone an unfailing Cause. And it is 
not the “flower of the field,” but “the word of our 
Lord (that) endureth for ever.” ‘‘ Heaven and earth 
shall pass,” says the Lord Himself, “ but My words 
shall not pass.” 

Consequently, my dearest brethren, you have done 
what is very prudent and very profitable in choosing 
to “ keep hard ways for the sake of the words of (His) 
lips,” and to sow your seed in a field where the smallest 
portion of it cannot fail to fructify. Even “he who 
soweth sparingly ” shall not lose his seed, but ‘ shall 
also reap sparingly.” No one that sows at all remains 
without a harvest. For we know by Whom it has 


= 


NINTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 213 


been promised that whosoever shall give even a cup of 
cold water for His name’s sake “shall not lose his 
reward.” Nevertheless, is it not true that “ with what 
measure you mete it shall be measured to you again,” 
that he who gives but a cup of cold water shall not 
receive the same reward as another who gives not 
water but his very blood, and so drinks of the chalice 
offered him by Christ? This chalice of the Saviour 
- is no cup of cold water, but a “ goodly chalice which 
inebriateth,” “a cup of strong wine full of mixture.” 
For my Lord Jesus alone possesses pure wine, because 
He alone is Himself perfectly pure, and alone also “ can 
make him clean that is conceived of unclean seed.” 
Surely He alone has the pure wine, Who, according to 
His Divine Nature, is that Wisdom ‘“ which reacheth 
everywhere by reason of her purity . . . and no defiled 
thing cometh to her” ; and according to His Human 
Nature “did no sin, neither was guile found in His 
Mouth.” He alone has tasted death, not from the 
necessity of His condition, but by the free choice of 
His own will. Neither did He die for His own profit, 
because “‘ He hath no need of our goods ” ; nor yet in 
order to make us a return of “ grace for grace,” since He 
gave His life, not for the sake of those who were already 
His friends, but for the purpose of gaining friends, of 
making friends out of enemies. “For when we were 
enemies, we were reconciled to God by the death of 
His Son.” Or perhaps it were more true to say that 
He died for those who were His friends already, not 
yet indeed loving friends, nevertheless friends beloved. 
For “in this is (grace) : not as though we had loved 
God, but because He hath first loved us.” Do you 
wish to know by how much His love for us anticipated 


214 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


our love for Him? Then listen to St. Paul: “ Blessed 
be the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, Who 
hath blessed us with spiritual blessings in heavenly 
places in Christ: as He chose us in Him before the 
foundation of the world, that we should be holy and 
unspotted in His sight in charity ; Who hath predes- 
tined us unto the adoption of children through Jesus 
Christ unto Himself, according to the purpose of His 
will : unto the praise of the glory of His grace, in which 
He hath giaced us in His beloved Son.” How is it 
possible that we were not beloved in Him in Whom we 
were chosen? Or how is it possible that we were not 
pleasing in Him in Whom we were graced? Thus, 
therefore, ‘‘ did Christ . . . according to the time, die 
for the ungodly,” but according to predestination He 
died for His friends and brothers. 

In all these respects, therefore, His wine, and His 
alone, is found to be pure. Amongst other martyrs, 
there is not a single one who can presume to say that 
to him may not be applied the words of the Prophet 
Isaias, ‘‘ Thy wine is mingled with water.” * First, 
because, “no one is free from stain ” in this life, no — 
one can glory in the possession of a perfectly pure 
heart. Secondly, because all must, sooner or later, 
undergo the penalty of death. Thirdly, because by 
laying down their lives for Christ, they purchase at a 
cheap price a blissful immortality: but woe to them 
if they are ashamed to render Him testimony by their 
blood.f Fourthly, because at best they make but a 


* He means that in the case of none of the holy martyrs

t That is to say, their motive is not altogether pure, since

very poor and inadequate return for the immense and 
gratuitous love which He has already shown them. 
Nevertheless, He Who is Himself without “ mixture ” 
does not disdain even Such impure wine, so that the 
Apostle can say with confidence, ‘‘I fill up those things 
that are wanting of the sufferings of Christ in my 
flesh for His Body.” * Consequently, although one and 
the same “ penny ” of eternal;life is rendered equally 
to all the elect, nevertheless in it “ star differeth from 
star in glory,” and “one is the glory of the sun, 
another the glory of the moon, and another the glory of 
the stars.” So shall it bein the resurrection of the dead. 
For although the “ house ” is one, it nevertheless con- 
tains “ many mansions.” Hence,as regards the eternity 
and the sufficiency of the happiness, he who has little 
merit is nothing short, and he who has much has nothing 
over. But with respect to eminence and distinction of 
merit, every man shall receive what is proportionate 
to his labour, so that nothing may be lost of all that 
has been sown in Christ. 

These remarks have been made, my brethren, for the 
purpose of commending to your esteem the wonderful 
spiritual beauty of the words I am now discussing : 
“ Because Thou, O Lord, art my hope.” Whatever, 
therefore, is to be done, whatever is to be avoided, 
whatever to be suffered, whatever to be desired : ‘‘ Thou, 
O Lord, art my hope.” This is the cause of all the 
promises made to me: this is likewise the whole and 
sole reason of my expectation. Let others rely on their 
merits ; let them boast of having borne “‘ the burden of 
the day and the heat,” and of “ fasting twice in the 


* St. Paul thus seems to imply that his own sufferings were 
worthy of being united to and of supplementing Christ’s.— 
(Translator.) 


216 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


week,” and of not being “as the rest of men ” : “ but 
it is good for me to adhere to my God, and to put my 
hope in the Lord God.” Other men may hope in other 
things, this one in thé knowkdge of letters, that one 
in the wisdom of the world, a third in the nobility of 
his descent, a fourth in the loftiness of his dignity, a 
fifth in some similar vanity : but for Thy sake, O Lord, 
“ I count all things tosbe. but loss,” yea, “and count 
them but as dung thattI mayain Christ,” “ because 
Thou, O Lord, art my hope.” Let him who likes put 
his hope in uncertain riches; for my part, I will not 
look elsewhere but to Thee for the very necessaries of 
life, trusting to that saying of Thine, on account of 
which I have abjured the world: “‘ Seek ye first the 
kingdom of God, and His justice, and all these things 
shall be added unto you.” For “ to Thee is the poor 
man left: Thou wilt be a helper to the orphan.” If 
then, rewards are offered to me, it is through Thee I 
will hope to obtain them ; “if battles should rise up 
against me, in (Thee) will I be confident.” Let the 
world rage, let the devil burn with fury, let the flesh 
rebel against the spirit : spite of all, in Thee will I hope. 

My brethren, to entertain such sentiments is to live 
by faith. But no one can say from his heart, ‘‘ Because 
Thou, O Lord, art my hope,” except the man who is 
interiorly persuaded by the Holy Ghost to do what 
the Prophet recommends in the words, “ Cast thy care 
upon the Lord, and He shall sustain thee,” which the 
Apostle Peter also counsels, where he writes, “ Casting 
all your care upon Him, for He hath care of you.” For 
if these are really our sentiments, why should we, 
hesitate to renounce all other wretched, vain, unprofit-? 
able, delusive hopes, and with all the energy of our 


NINTH-SERMON ON PSALM XC 217 


minds and all the fervour of our spirits to cling to this 
one solid, perfect, blissful hope? If there is anything 
impossible to the Lord, if there is anything even difficult 
to Him, seek out some other in*whom you may place 
your trust. But He can do all things by His mere word. 
What is easier than to speak a word? But I would 
have you understand clearly what is meant by this 
speech of God. I say, then, that if He wills to save us 
we shall infallibly be delivered ; if it is His good pleasure 
to vivify us, there is “life in His good will ” ; if He 
decides to give us an eternal reward, it is lawful for 
Him to do what He pleases. Perhaps you are now 
satisfied concerning His power to assist you, but are 
yet in doubt with respect to His good will? However, 
the “‘ testimonies (of His good will) are become exceed- 
ingly credible.” Listen to the words of Christ Himself : 
“Greater love than this no man hath, that a man lay 
down his life for his friends.” When indeed will God 
be wanting to the man who puts his hope in Him, 
since this is what He has so earnestly admonished us 
_to do? Evidently, then, He will not abandon those 
who make Him their hope. As the Psalmist says, 
“The Lord will help them and deliver them: and He 
will rescue them from the wicked and save them.” 
Wherefore ? On account of what merits? The Prophet 
immediately proceeds to explain: “‘ Because they have 
hoped in Him.” A most sweet reason, truly, yet also 
most efficacious and irresistible. For this hope is real 
justice, not the justice of the law, but “ the justice that 
is of faith.’ “He shall cry to Me, and I will hear 
him: I am with him in tribulation. I will deliver 
him.” Come, my brethren, reckon up all your tribu- 
lations. Each one of you may say with the Prophet: 


218 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


“ According to the multitude of my sorrows in my 
heart, Thy comforts (shall) give joy to my soul.” That 
is, provided you do not seek for consolation elsewhere, 
provided you cry to Him, provided you hope in Him, 
provided you make not anything low and earthly, but 
only the Most High your refuge. “ Know ye that no one 
hath hoped in the Lord and hath been confounded ? ” 
It were easier for heaven and earth to pass away than 
for one word of His to come to naught. 

“Thou hast made the Most High thy refuge.” To 
this asylum the seducer has no access; to this height 
the calumniator cannot mount; to this retreat the 
wicked accuser of the brethren finds no admittance. 
For, as you remember from the beginning of the psalm, 
these words are addressed to him “that dwelleth in 
the aid of the Most High,” fleeing thither from the 
“ pusillanimity of spirit and the storm.” We have a 
double necessity for running to this refuge and dwelling 
in this aid, because as the Apostle says, there are 
“combats without (and) fears within.” There would 
be less need of having recourse to the Lord, if either 
our interior strength could support courageously the 
tumult outside, or if external peace allowed our pusill- 
animity to remain undisquieted. ‘‘ Thou hast made the 
Most High thy refuge.” My brethren, let us often 
run for safety to this refuge. It is a strongly fortified 
place, where we shall have nothing to fear from the 
enemy. Would to God we were allowed to remain 
there always! But that is not for this present time. 
What is now a refuge shall, however, become one day 
a dwelling-place, and an everlasting dwelling-place. 
Meanwhile, since we are not as yet permitted to make 
our abode there, let us at least visit it often. For against 


NINTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 219 


every temptation, against every tribulation, against 
every kind of necessity, this city of refuge is open to 
shelter us ; the mother’s arms ate extended to welcome 
us; the “clefts of the rock ”? are ready to receive us ; 
“the bowels of the mercy of our God ” invite us to 
enter. Little wonder, then, if he does not succeed in 
escaping from his enemies, whosoever despises such a 
refuge. 

It seems te me, my brethren, that the observations 
already offered would suffice as an exposition of my 
present text, had the Prophet said here what we find 
in another psalm, ‘‘ For in Thee I have put my trust.” 
But his words in this place, “‘ Because Thou, O Lord, 
art my hope,” may well appear to signify something 
greater and more sublime, inasmuch as they declare 
that the Lord is not only the foundation, but also the 
object of his hope. For the object of our hope has a 
better right to be called simply our hope than has the 
reason thereof. Doubtless there are some who hope 
to receive and long to receive from the Lord certain 
temporal or spiritual favours : but perfect charity desires 
nothing, thirsts for nothing save what is supreme, the 
Sovereign Good, and exclaims with all the eagerness of 
ardent love, ‘‘ For what have I in heaven, and besides 
Thee, what do I desire upon earth? For Thee my 
flesh and my heart hath fainted away: Thou art the 
God of my heart, and the God That is my portion for 
ever.” Both these species of desire are very beauti- 
fully comprehended in this short verse from the Prophet 
Jeremias, ‘‘ The Lord is good to them that hope in Him, 
to the soul that seeketh Him.” Observe with what 
prudence he varies the number, using the plural when 
speaking of them that hope in the Lord, because this 


220 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


appears common to many ; but referring in the singular 
to the seeker of the Lord, because it is a sign of singular 
purity, of singular grace, of singular perfection, not 
only to seek nothing save from the Lord, but to seek 
nothing even from Him save Himself. And if He is 
so good to the former, what shall He be to the latter ? 
Rightly, therefore, is it said to the soul that thus seeks 
the Lord: “ Thou hast made the Most High thy refuge.” 
For the soul possessed of such a thirst for God does not 
wish, like Peter, to buildfor Him a tabernacle on an 
earthly mountain, nor yet, like Mary Magdalen, to 
touch Him with the hand of flesh, but rather cries out 
to Him with the Spouse in the Canticle, “ Flee away, 
my Beloved : be like to a roe or a young hart upon the 
mountains of Bether.’”’ And that, because she has 
heard Him saying, “‘ If you loved Me, you would indeed 
be glad, because I go to the Father ; for the Father is 
greater than I.” She has also heard Him saying, “‘ Do 
not touch Me, for I am not yet ascended to My Father.” 
And so, being now enlightened as to the heavenly 
counsel, she exclaims with the Apostle, “ And though 
we have known Christ according to the flesh, now we 
know Him so no longer.” ‘‘ Upon the mountains of 
Bether,” says the Spouse. That is, she desires to reach 
Him where He is enthroned at the Right Hand of the 
Father, to Whom He is no longer inferior,* but Most 

* This of course does not mean either that Christ as God 
was inferior to the Father before His Ascension, or that after 
the Ascension His Human Nature was elevated to an equality 
with the Divine. The Saint is speaking according to what 
was apparent: during His life on earth the Word Incarnate 
appeared to be less than the Father. Or the meaning may 
be that, after the Ascension, Christ, even in His Human Nature, 
began to receive manifestly the same supreme worship as is 


paid to the Father. Cf. St. Thomas, Sum. Theol., III. q. xxv. 
a. 1-2; also A Lapide, Comment. in Joan. xiv. 28.—(Translator.) ~
