High as the Most High, above all Powers and Princi- 
palities, above Angels and Archangels, above Cherubim 
and Seraphim,—for these are the only mountains in 
Bether, which signifies “ the house of God.” ‘‘ Now 
this is eternal life,” my dearest brethren, “ that (we) 
may know the Father, the only true God, and Jesus 
Christ Whom He hath sent,” and Who with Him is 
one true God, over all things, and blessed for ever. 
Amen. 


TENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


On SIN AND ITS PUNISHMENT, AND ON THE DELIVER- 
ANCE FROM BOTH WHICH THE RESURRECTION SHALL 
BRING US. 


“ There shall no evil come to thee: nor shall the scourge come 
near thy tabernacle.” 


Brethren, it is no discovery of mine, nor is it any- 
thing novel to you, but something very familiar, the 
truth, namely, that as regards the principal objects of 
our faith it is more easy to know and more dangerous 
to be ignorant of what they are not than what they are. 
It seems to me that the same holds good of hope, 
and no less than of faith. For the human mind, 
from its experience of evils, so great and so many, 
understands far more easily what it is to be without 
suffering and misery than what it is to be in the 
enjoyment of bliss. There is an intimate relation 
between faith and hope. The happiness which the 
former believes shall be, the latter looks forward 
to enjoy with confident expectation. Justly, there- 
fore, does the Apostle define faith as “the sub- 
stance of things to be hoped for.” For surely no one 
can hope unless he first believes in the object of his 
hope, any more than one can paint a picture on the 
empty air. Faith, therefore, seems to say, ‘‘ God has 
prepared a treasure of inconceivable happiness for His 
faithful servants ” ; hope, “ It is for me that happiness 
is prepared ” ; and then charity, “ As for me, I am 


222° 


TENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 223 


already speeding to the enjoyment of that happiness.” 
But, as I observed before, it is extremely difficult, if 
not absolutely impossible, to form an adequate idea of 
the good things in store for us, except perhaps for one 
to whom the Lord Himself reveals by His Spirit ‘‘what 
things God hath prepared for them that love Him,” 
which as the Apostle says, ‘‘ eye hath not seen, nor ear 
heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man ” 
to conceive. There is undoubtedly a certain kind 
of imperfect perfection (if I may be allowed the ex- 
pression) possible to us even in this life, otherwise St. 
Paul would not have said, “ Let us, therefore, as many 
as are perfect, be thus minded,” which must evidently 
be interpreted in accordance with his previous words, 
“ Not as though I had already attained or were already 
perfect.” But to whatever perfection a man may attain 
whilst living in the flesh, he will always be obliged to 
confess with the same Apostle, “ Now I know (only) in 
part,” and, “ We see now through a glass, in a dark 
manner, but then face to face.’’* Consequently, that 
kind of happiness which is known to be more easily 
intelligible to man, is more frequently proposed to him 
in the Scriptures, out of a loving consideration for his 
weakness, For it is natural for the afflicted to repute 
as highest happiness deliverance from their miseries, 
and to esteem a life without suffering or sorrow as the 
most blissful of all lives. Hence the Royal Prophet 
sings in one of the psalms, “ Turn, O my soul, into 
thy rest, for the Lord hath been bountiful to thee ’’; 

* As to the question whether any mere viator has ever 
enjoyed the Beatific Vision, even momentarily, cf. St. Thomas, 
Sum. Theol., II. ii. q. clxxv. a. 111; also St. Bernard’s Ser- 


mons on the Canticle of Canticles, vol. ii. pp. 151-2 (note).— 
(Translator.) 


224 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


and without making any mention of receiving other 
benefactions appertaining to happiness, he goes on, 
“ For He hath delivered my soul from death, my eyes 
from tears, my feet from falling.” By these words he 
plainly indicates how great a rest he deems it, and how 
great a benefit from God, to be delivered from tribula- 
tions and dangers. 

Similiar to this in meaning shall be found the verse 
of the ninetieth psalm, which I have to examine 
to-day, namely, “‘ There shall no evil come to thee : nor 
shall the scourge come near thy tabernacle.” The sig- 
nification of these words, appears to me to be manifest 
enough, and some of yourselves, probably, have already 
divined it. For you are not so uninstructed or so wanting 
in spiritual enlightenment, as not to be able to distin- 
guish with ease between yourselves and your tabernacles 
on the one hand, and between the “evil” and the 
“scourge’’ on the other. You have even heard the 
Apostle saying, when he had “‘ fought the good fight,” 
that the time was at hand for the dissolution of his 
earthly tabernacle. But wherefore do I cite for you 
the words of St. Paul? As if, forsooth, a soldier could 
be ignorant of his own tabernacle and requiring to have 
it pointed out to him by means of a foreign example ! 
Some, indeed, we behold who have changed their taber- 
nacles into permanent dwellings of most shameful cap- 
tivity, no longer living in them like soldiers in war- 
time, but rather as most wretched bondslaves. Nay, 
what is still more strangely perverse, some wander so 
far out of the right path, and arrive at such a degree 
of forgetfulness, such a pitch of spiritual madness, that 
they appear to identify themselves with their outward 
earthly tabernacle. For how can they be otherwise 


iP Sara oe 


TENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 225 


than entirely forgetful, not only of God, but of them- 
selves also, they, who, “as dead from the heart,” 
devote all their attention to the body, and are so 
solicitous for their tabernacles as if they expected them 
to. stand for ever? Nevertheless, these tabernacles 
are doomed to fall, and that in a very short time. Or, 
do you not consider those to be ignorant of themselves 
who are so occupied and engrossed in ministering to 
flesh and blood as if they judged themselves to be 
nothing distinct from these material elements ; who so 
“receive their souls in vain,” as if they were utterly 
unconscious that they have souls at all? “Tf thou wilt 
separate the precious from the vile,” saith the Lord 
by His Prophet Jeremias, “ thou shalt be as My Mouth.” 
That is to say, “ If thou wilt prudently distinguish 
between the external and material part of thy nature 
and the interior, spiritual part, in such a manner that 
thou wilt be more solicitous to defend the latter from 
evil than the former from the scourge, then thou shalt 
be as My Mouth.” For the evil in question here is that 
whereof the Psalmist says in another place, ‘“‘ Decline 
from evil and do good.” This is the evil which deprives 
thy soul of her Soul,* in that it “ maketh a division 
between thee and God.” And whilst it reigns in thee, 
as a body without a soul so is thy soul without God, 
utterly dead in herself, like one of those whom the 
Apostle declared to be « living without God in this 
world.” 

* God may be said to be the Soul of our souls in the sense 
that He is the Principle of their supernatural life just as they 
are the principle of natural life to our bodies. But beyond 
this the analogy does not hold. God and the soul do not com- 
plete each other essentially, as do soul and body, nor do 


they stand to each other in the relation of form and matter.— 
(Translator.) 


I, P 


226 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


However, I would not advise you to have a hatred 
even for your flesh. On the contrary, I would have 
you to love it, as given you to be your helper in this 
life, and the associate of your immortal felicity in the 
next. But let the soul love the body in such a manner 
that the spirit shall not seem to have been turned 
into flesh, and thus deserve to hear from the Lord 
the words of reproach, ‘‘ My Spirit shall not remain in 
man for ever, because he is flesh.” Yes, my brethren, 
let the soul love her body: but let her love her own 
Soul much more dearly. Let Adam by all means love 
his Eve, yet not in such a way as to be more willing 
to obey her voice than the voice of God. For to be 
so loved is not expedient even for the flesh : I mean, if 
your love of it leads you to shelter it in this life from 
the scourge of paternal correction, whilst treasuring up 
against it the wrath of eternal damnation in the life 
to come. “Ye brood of vipers,” said the Baptist to 
the Pharisees and Sadducees, “ who hath showed you 
to flee from the wrath to come? Bring forth, therefore, 
fruit worthy of penance.” As if he should say in the 
words of the Psalmist, ‘‘ Embrace discipline lest at any 
time the Lord be angry,” that is: “submit to the rod 
of correction lest you should feel the bludgeon of ven- 
geance.”’ Why do carnal men say to us, “This manner 
of life is cruel : you have no compassion for your flesh ?” 
I grant, we do not spare the flesh any more than the 
farmer spares the seed. Yet what kindness to the 
seed could be greater than such apparent cruelty? Is 
it not happier in being allowed to renew and multiply 
itself in the ground than if it were permitted to rot in 
the granary ? Alas! “the beasts have rotted in their 
dung,” as the Prophet Joel speaks. Is it thus you 


TENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 227 


spare your flesh ? We indeed may seem to be cruel in 
condemning our bodies to penance during this life, but 
you are much more cruel in sparing them. For even 
now our flesh “‘ resteth in hope.” : but consider to what 
dishonour your flesh is subjected here and what misery 
awaits it hereafter. ‘‘ There shall no evil come to 
thee: nor shall the scourge come near thy tabernacle.” 
Here, my brethren, we have a double robe,* and 
a twofold immortality is commended to our desire. 
What causes death but the separation of soul and 
body ? Hence we speak of a dead body as soulless 
(exanime). But what causes this separation if not the 
scourges of the present life, the violence of pains of 
every description, and the corruption of the body itself, 
which are all the penalty of sin ? Justly, therefore, does 
our flesh fear and hate the scourge, which compels it 
to separate, with indescribable bitterness, from the 
sweet and honourable companionship of the soul. 
Nevertheless, it has to be endured, however un- 
willingly, until the hour when the soul returns to 
reanimate the body. And is it not better for thee to 
submit to pain in this life in such a manner that thou 
shalt escape it altogether in the next, and “ the scourge 
shall (no more) come near thy tabernacle ” ? 

My brethren, I have already reminded you of what 
it is expedient for you never to forget, namely, that 
God is the true Life of your souls. But the soul too, 
may be separated from her Life, not by the scourge, as 
the body, but by the evil, the evil proper to the soul, 


* Allusion to Apocalypse vi. 11: “ And white robes were 
given, to every one of them one ; and it was said to them that 
they should rest for a little while, till their fellow-servants and 
their brethren, who were to be slain even as they, should be 
filled up ’’—when they should receive the second robe in the 
glorification of the flesh.—(Translator.) 


228 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


which is nothing other than sin. Alas, dearest brethren! 
How can we take delight in frivolous occupations, how 
can we freely indulge our propensity to sloth, in the 
immediate neighbourhood of these two serpents, that 
are watching to take from us our double life, the life 
of our soul and the life of our body ? How can we go 
to sleep in security? Is not negligence in presence of 
danger rather a sign of despair than of real security ? 
We ought to desire indeed deliverance from both the 
scourge and the evil ; yet sin is more to be dreaded than 
the penalty of sin. And we must be the more careful 
to avoid the evil than the scourge, in proportion as it 
is a greater misfortune and a more utter ruin for the 
soul to be separated from God than for the body to be 
separated from the soul. For when all sin has been 
entirely removed, it must needs be that the effect shall 
be taken away with the cause. Consequently, just as 
no evil can then ‘‘ come to thee,” so neither ‘‘ shall the 
scourge come near thy tabernacle.” That is to say, 
suffering of every kind shall be as far removed from the 
body as sin from the soul. For, as you may notice, 
the Psalmist does not say, “ There shall be no evil in 
thee,” or “ The scourge shall not be im thy tabernacle,” 
but ‘‘ There shall no evil come to thee, nor shall the 
scourge come near thy tabernacle.”’ 

There are some men, my brethren, in whom sin 
appears not only to dwell but even to dominate: so 
much so that it seems to have come as near to them as 
is possible, at least until it shall begin to reign in them 
in such a manner that never more can it cease to reign. 
You may meet with others in whom sin is still dwelling, 
yet no longer reigns or dominates. It has been plucked 
up, but not yet cast forth ; it has been overthrown, but 


TENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 229 


not yet driven out. “ But from the beginning it was 
not so,”’ as is sufficiently evident. Before that primeval 
transgression of the divine command, not only did sin 
not reign in our first parents, but it did not even dwell 
in them. Nevertheless, it must be supposed to have 
come near them, even at that time, since it so easily 
obtained their consent and so speedily gained admission. 
Yea, and it was then nigh when God gave them solemn 
warning, in the words addressed to Adam, “ In what 
day soever thou shalt eat of it (the tree of knowledge) 
thou shalt die the death.” Happy, therefore, is our 
expectation and blessed our hope, since the promised 
resurrection shall make us so much more glorious than 
was our primitive condition.* For after the resurrec- 
tion there shall be neither sin nor punishment, neither 
the evil nor the scourge. They shall never again, 
rather let me say, they never again can either dominate 
or dwell in our souls or bodies. “ There shall no evil 
come to thee,” says the Psalmist, “nor shall the 
scourge come near thy tabernacle.” And what can be 
farther away from thee than that can never come nigh 
thee ? 

But what are we doing, brethren? I fear we have 
been discovered in the act of transgressing our Rule. 
For, as you know, our common Abbot, the great St. 
Benedict, has appointed this hour, not for spiritual 
instruction, but for manual labour. However, I think 
he will easily pardon us, especially when he remembers 

` that pious fraud whereby the monk Romanus ministered 
so faithfully to his wants of old, during the three years 


* Hence the Church sings in the “ Exultet on Easter 
Saturday, “O certe necessarium Adae peccatum quod Christi 
morte deletum est! O felix culpa quae talem et tantum meruit 
habere Redemptorem ! ”—(Translator.) 


230 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


he lived in the cavern. This holy man, so we read,* 
was wont on certain days to steal an hour from the 
vigilance of his superior, in order to bring surreptitiously 
to the Saint a portion of the bread given him for his 
own use. As for me, dearest brethren, I make no doubt 
that many amongst you possess in themselves a greater 
abundance of spiritual refreshment than I can com- 
municate : but the little I have is not lost to me by being 
shared with you. Nay, rather I shall enjoy whatever 
the Lord may be pleased to bestow, with all the more 
sweetness and security, when I enjoy it in common 
with you. This kind of treasure is not lessened by com- 
munication : it rather increases according as it is dis- 
pensed. Yet if I preach to you more frequently than 
the custom of our Order sanctions, I do so, not by my 
own presumption, but at the bidding of my venerable 
brother-abbots, who have enjoined on me what, never- 
theless, they consider unlawful for themselves; because 
they know that there is special reason and special 
necessity for me to make myself useful in this way. 
For were I able to join in your manual labour, I should 
not be addressing you now. Participation in your toil 
would indeed be a more efficacious manner of preaching 
and more acceptable to my own conscience. But since 
that is denied me through the merit of my sins, and 
the manifold infirmities (so well known to you) of this 
burdensome body, as well as by the necessities of the 
present times, God grant that, saying and not doing, 
I may deserve to be found even the least in the kingdom 
of heaven. Amen. 


* Dialogues of St. Gregory the Great, lib. ii. c. i. 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE WAYS OF MEN, THE WAYS OF THE DEMONS, 
THE* WAYS OF THE HoLy ANGELS, AND THE 
WAYS OF THE LORD. 


“ For He hath given His angels charge over thee, to keep thee in 
all thy ways.” 


My brethren, it is written in the Prophet Jeremias, 
and written truly, that we owe it to “ the mercies of 
the Lord that we are not consumed,” because He has 
not delivered us into the hands of our enemies. His 
Eye of incomparable love ever watches over us, without 
interruption, as without weariness. Hence the Psalmist 
says, “Behold, He shall neither slumber nor sleep, 
That keepeth Israel.” That is very necessary for us. 
For he also shall neither slumber nor sleep, that fighteth 
against Israel. And just as the Lord is solicitous for 
us and hath care of us; so too is Satan solicitous how 
he may injure and destroy us, and his only care is to 
prevent the soul that has abandoned her God from ever 
returning to Him. But we either pay no attention 
at all, or we pay too little attention, to the presence 
of that Majesty in Whose sight we are always standing ; 
we do not sufficiently consider the constant protection 
afforded us by our Divine Defender, nor the manifold 
' gifts of His munificence. Thus, we show ourselves to 
have but little gratitude for the grace, rather for the 
multiplied graces, with which He forestalls and sup- 
ports us. At one time, by His own direct action, He 
fills our souls with heavenly illumination ; at another 


231 


232 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


He visits us by His angels ; now He employs our fellow- 
men toinstruct us ; and now He consoles and enlightens 
us by means of the Sacred Scriptures. ‘‘ For what things 
soever were written, were written for our learning ; that 
through patience and the comfort of the Scriptures we. 
might have hope.” Well does the Apostle say, “ for 
our learning, that through patience we might have 
hope,” because, as we read in Proverbs, “ The learning 
of a man is known by patience.” And St. Paul himself 
tells us in another place that “ patience (worketh) trial, 
and trial hope.” How is it that we alone are wanting 
to ourselves? How is it that we alone neglect our- 
selves? Perhaps the reason why we are so careless 
with regard to our own interests is because we behold 
assistance coming to us freely from all sides? But this is 
the very reason which should make us the more zealous 
and vigilant. For undoubtedly there would not be so 
much solicitude regarding us.in heaven and on earth, 
unless our necessities seemed to be correspondingly 
great ; and unless we were surrounded by numerous 
enemies, we should not have about us such manifold 
protection. 

How happy, therefore, are those amongst us who 
have already, in a manner, been delivered from the 
“snare of the hunters,” and have gone forth from the 
tabernacles of war-time into the dwellings of peace, 
secure now from all danger of evil, and “ singularly 
settled in hope’’! It is to one of these, rather I should 
say, it is to the whole assembly of these that the words 
are addressed, ‘“‘ There shall no evil come to thee ; nor 
shall the scourge come near thy tabernacle.” Remember, 
my brethren, that this promise is made, not to such as 
live according to the flesh, but to those who, although 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 233 


living in the flesh, yet walk according to the spirit. 
For with respect to the carnal man, there is no distinc- 
tion between himself and his tabernacle. All things 
are confounded in him, because he is a son of Babylon.* 
Such a one is entirely flesh, and the Spirit of the Lord 
cannot rest upon him. But how can evil be wanting 
there where the good Spirit abideth not? And where- 
soever evil is found, there of necessity shall the scourge 
appear also. For punishment is the inseparable com- 
panion of sin. ‘‘ There shall no evil come to thee ; nor 
shall the scourge come near thy tabernacle.” This 
doubtless is a grand promise. But on what grounds 
may I hope to see it accomplished in myself? How 
shall I avoid both the evil and the scourge? Whither 
shall I flee, to what remote land shall I withdraw so 
that they may not come nigh me? By what merit, by 
what wisdom, by what virtue can I hope to escape? 
Listen, brethren : ‘‘ For he hath given His angels charge 
over thee, to keep thee in all thy ways.” In all which 
ways? In all the ways by which thou declinest from 
evil and by which thou fleest from the wrath to come. 
There are many ways and also many varieties of ways. 
Hence the traveller is exposed to much perplexity and 
peril. How easy it is to take the wrong road where 
many roads diverge, especially when one is not ac- 
quainted with the landmarks! And observe that God 
hath not given His angels charge over us to keep us 
in all ways without distinction, but only in all our 
ways. For there are other ways from which, rather than 
in which, we have need to be guarded. 

Therefore, brethren, “let us search our ways,” as 
the Prophet Jeremias counsels. Let us also examine 


* “ Babylon ” signifies “ confusion.’’—(Translator.) 


234 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


the ways of the demons, the ways of the holy angels, 
and the ways of the Lord. But I am conscious that I 
am undertaking a task which is beyond my capacity. 
You must, therefore, assist me by your prayers, in 
order that God may open to me the treasure of His 
wisdom and “‘ make pleasing to Himself the voluntary 
offerings of my mouth.” Well now to begin. The 
ways of the children of Adam lie in necessity and 
cupidity. For we are drawn by both and are driven by 
both. There is, however, this difference, that weseem 
to be more driven by necessity and more drawn by 
cupidity. Besides, necessity, so it seems, should be 
attributed to the body in particular. Nor is this way 
of necessity a straight and simple way, but very round- 
about, with many twists and turns, and admitting of 
few short cuts, if any at all. What man is not aware 
how manifold and multiform are our human necessi- 
ties? Who shall be able to enumerate them all? 
Experience is our best instructor in this matter, be- 
cause, as the Prophet Isaias bears witness, “ vexation 
alone shall give understanding.” By this each of us 
is taught how much need he has to cry to the Lord, 
not, “ Deliver me from my necessity,” but with the 
Psalmist, “ Deliver me from my necessities.” Nor is 
it alone out of the way of necessity we desire to be led, 
but also out of the way of cupidity, as many of us, I 
mean, as do not turn a deaf ear to the admonitions 
of the Wise Man. What does he say? “Turm away 
from thy own will” ; and again, “Go not after thy . 
lusts.” Indeed of the two evils, it were better to walk 
in the way of necessity than in that of cupidity. The 
former way has been described as manifold ; but more 
manifold still is the latter, yea, it is manifold beyond 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 235 


all measure. For cupidity appertains to the heart. 
Therefore its multiplicity exceeds that of necessity in 
proportion as the soul is greater than the body. Such, 
my brethren, “are the ways which seem good to 
men, but the end thereof ” is without end, because 
they lead down into hell’s abyss. If we have now dis- 
covered the ways of men, let us consider this also, 
whether it be not of them that the Psalmist sings, 
“Destruction and unhappiness are in their ways.” 
How can it be questioned that there is unhappiness 
in the way of cupidity, and not the happiness which 
men seek in it? For what though some appear 
to find all the happiness they wish for in. the 
prosperity of this world? The misery of such is 
only made the more miserable by the eagerness with 
which they embrace unhappiness for happiness, or 
rather lose and submerge themselves in unhappiness. 
And woe to the sons of men by reason of this false and 
delusive felicity! Woe to him who says in his heart, 
“I am rich and made wealthy, and have need of 
nothing,” whereas he is “‘ wretched and miserable, and 
poor, and blind, and naked’’! Necessity, as I have 
remarked, proceeds from the weakness of the flesh, and 
cupidity from the hunger and forgetfulness of the heart. 
For therefore does the soul hunger after foreign 
bread because she has forgotten to eat her own: 
that is to say, the reason why she covets earthly 
delights is because she neglects to consider the 
heavenly. 

Let us now examine the ways of the demons. Let 
us exarnine them in order that we may avoid them ; 
let us study them with the object of shunning them. 
For their ways are presumption and obstinacy. Do 


236 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


you ask me how I can know this? Well, consider 
him who is the prince of devils, for as he is, so are they 
of his household. And look at the commencement of 
Lucifer’s ways. Did he not evidently begin his career 
by an act of most monstrous presumption, saying, “ I 
will exalt my throne above the stars of God, I will sit 
on the mountains of the covenant, in the sides of the 
north . . . I will be like the Most High.” Oh, how 
daring was such presumption, and how horrible! It 
was this that caused the ruin of both him and his 
followers. As the Psalmist says, ‘‘ There the workers of 
iniquity are fallen, they are cast out and could not 
stand.” It was on account of their presumption they 
could not stand, and because of their obstinacy they 
cannot now rise again after falling. Presumption has 
made each of them “a spirit that goeth,” and ob- 
stinacy “a spirit that returneth no more.” Astounding 
indeed wasthe demons’ presumption, but not less astound- 
ing is their obstinacy, because their pride ascendeth 
always: “ Therefore there is no change with them.” 
For by their unwillingness to turn from the way of 
presumption, they have fallen into the way of obstinacy. 
Oh, how perverted and corrupted in heart are the sons 
of men, as many of them as follow in the footsteps of 
Satan and begin to walk in the ways of the demons ! 
For the one object of the “ wrestling ”’ ofthe “ spirits 
of wickedness ”’ with us is to induce us to enter their 
ways, so that they may lead us along in them, and 
bring us at last to the doom prepared for themselves. 
Therefore, flee, O man, flee from presumption and 
obstinacy, lest they should “ make thee a joy to thy 
enemies.” For these are the vices in which thy enemies 
long particularly to behold thee entangled, having 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 237 


experienced in themselves how difficult it is to escape 
out of such a whirlpool. 

But I would not have you to be ignorant, my 
brethren, of the manner in which we may descend 
into, or perhaps I should say, fall into these ways of 
the demons. As it appears to me, the first step 
in this descent consists in shutting one’s eyes to one’s 
own weakness, to one’s own wickedness, to one’s own 
worthlessness. For then one begins to spare oneself, 
to flatter oneself, and to form a high esteem of one- 
self, forgetting the words of St. Paul, that, “if any 
man think himself to be something, whereas he is 
nothing, he deceiveth himself.” The second down- 
ward step is ignorance of oneself. For when in the 
first step, imitating our first parents, we have by our 
dissimulation ‘“‘ sewed together fig-leaves (so to speak) 
and made ourselves aprons’”’ of concealment, what 
wonder is it that we cannot now see our wounds, especi- 
ally as the sole reason for covering them was to prevent 
their being seen? * After this, we go so far as to 
deny our wounds whenever they happen to be revealed 
by another, inclining our “hearts to evil words, to 
make excuses in sins.” We have now taken the third 
step, which has carried us very near, yea, to the very 
borders of presumption. For what evil shall he be 


* Cf. Genesis iii. 7. The Saint uses similar language in De 
Consideratione, lib. ii. c. ix., where he says, addressing Pope 
Eugenius III: “ And not only oughtest thou to consider what 
thou wert born, but also in what manner thou didst enter the 
world. Otherwise thou shalt lose the profit and fruit of thy 
meditation. Take off, then, those hereditary aprons (perizo- 
mata) which have been cursed by God from the beginning. 
Tear away that covering of sewed fig-leaves, which hides thy 
shame but heals not thy wounds.” By the aprons and covering 
he means, of course, dissimulation ; and it is described as heredi- 
tary, as having been practised by Adam and Eve.—(Translator.) 


238 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


afraid to commit, who even dares to excuse his sins ? 
But he will find it difficult to maintain himself here 
on this slippery incline and surrounded with darkness, 
especially as there shall not be wanting an evil angel 
of the Lord to push and pursue him.* Consequently 
he takes the fourth step, or rather he plunges down 
the fourth precipice, which is contempt. For as we 
read in Proverbs, “ The wicked man, when he is come 
into the depth of sins, contemneth.”’ Then and thence- 
forward “ the pit shutteth her mouth upon him ” ever 
more and more, contempt delivering him over to im- 
penitence, whilst impenitence fixes his will in unchange- 
able obstinacy. Here at last is the sin which “ shall not 
be forgiven him neither in this world nor in the world 
tocome’’; because the heart that is thus rendered hard 
and obdurate “neither feareth God nor regardeth 
man.” Now, he who so cleaves to the devil in all his 
ways, has evidently become one spirit with him. It 
is with reference to the ways of men (which have been 
already pointed out to you) that the Apostle says, in 
warning his disciples, ‘‘ Let no temptation take hold 
on you, but such as is human.’ For it is proper to 
man to sin. But who does not know that the ways 
of the demons are entirely foreign to the nature of 
man? Although perhaps there are some men who 
from the habit of walking in the demon’s ways have 
put on the demon’s nature. Nevertheless, whatever 


* “ Let their way become dark and slippery ; and let the 
angel of the Lord pursue them ” (Ps. xxxiv. 6). We have here 
a most terrifying picture of our spiritual situation : walking on 
ice in the dark, with enemies pursuing and pushing us. How 
can we hope to escape a fall? “ And when they heard this, 
the disciples wondered very much, saying : Who then can be 
saved ? And Jesus beholding, said to them : With men this is 
impossible : but with God all things are possible ’’ (Matthew 
xix, 25-56),.—(Translator.) 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 239 


may be thought of particular men, it is the property, 
not of man, but of the devil, to persevere in sin. 

We have next to inquire what are the ways of the 
holy angels. Doubtless they are those which the Only- 
Begotten Himself hath revealed, where He says, ‘‘ You 
shall see the heavens opened, and the angels of God 
ascending and descending upon the Son of man.” 
Therefore the ways of the angels are ascents and de- 
scents. They ascend for their own sakes ; they descend 
or rather condescend to us and for us. For these 
blessed spirits mount up to God in blissful contempla- 
tion, and out of compassion for us they come down to 
our lowliness to keep us in all our ways. They ascend 
to rejoice in God’s presence ; they descend to execute 
His will, “ for He hath given His angels charge over 
thee.” Nevertheless, not even when they descend to 
us are they deprived of the vision of glory, because 
they “always see the Face of the Father.” 

I suppose you want to hear something now concern- 
ing the ways of the Lord? Well, if I undertook to 
show you them, I should consider myself to be very 
presumptuous. It is written of Him, however, that 
“He will teach us of His ways” Himself. For how 
could we have confidence in any other teacher? Yea, 
He has already taught us His ways, since He has opened 
the lips of the Psalmist and inspired him to say, “ All 
the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth.” It is by 
.these ways, my brethren, that He comes to all souls 
in general and to each in particular, viz., by the ways 
of mercy and truth. For we cannot suppose that God 
has yet come to him, who, whilst presuming much on 
His mercy, seems to have forgotten His justice or truth. 
But neither is He found in the heart which is filled 


240 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


with terror by the remembrance of His truth, without 
deriving any consolation from the thought of His mercy. 
He cannot be said to hold the truth, who fails to re- 
cognise mercy where it truly exists : on the other hand 
mercy, to be true, must be accompanied by truth, that 
is to say, by justice. Accordingly, where (as the Psalmist 
sings) “ Mercy and truth have met each other, (there) 
justice and peace have kissed.” Nor can the Lord 
Himself be far away since “‘ His place is in peace.” Oh, 
my brethren, “ what great things have we heard and 
known, and our fathers have told us ”’ concerning this 
happy union of mercy and truth! “ Thy mercy and 
Thy truth have always upheld me,” exclaims the Royal 
Prophet ; and in another place, “ Thy mercy is before 
my eyes, and I am well pleased with Thy truth.” And 
regarding the same Prophet the Lord made this pro- 
mise, “‘ My truth and My mercy shall be with him.” 
But let us consider also the visible and manifest 
comings of the Lord. By that which has already been 
accomplished, have we not received the Saviour in 
mercy? And by that which is yet to be, which is 
promised at the end of the world, do we not expect 
One Who shall judge us in truth? Perhaps it was 
this which inspired the Psalmist to sing, “ God loveth 
mercy and truth ; the Lord will give grace and glory.” 
Yet even at His first coming, He was mindful not only 
of His mercy, but also of His truth “ to the house of 
Israel ’’ ; and at His second, not only shall He “ judge 
the ard with justice and the a with His truth,” 
but even then His justice shall be accompanied by 
mercy, except in the case of those who shall not have 
done mercy. For these are the ways of His eternity, 
whereof you may read in the Prophet Habacuc, “ The 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 241 


hills of the world are bent down from the ways of His 
eternity ;’’ * and this can be easily proved by Scrip- 
tural testimonies which I have in mind. Thus, it is 
written in the Psalms, “ The mercy of the Lord is from 
eternity and unto eternity ” ; and also, “ The truth of 
the Lord remaineth for ever.” It is from these two 
ways that “ the hills of the world have bent themselves 
down,” which hills symbolise the proud demons, the 
princes of this world, the rulers of this darkness, who 
have not known the ways of truth and mercy, but 
are utterly unmindful of their paths. What can there 
be in common between truth and him who, according 
to the clear testimony of the Saviour, “is a liar and 
the father thereof ” and “stood not in the truth ” ? 
And with regard to mercy, the very misery he has 
inflicted upon us sufficiently proves how little he pos- 
sesses of that. When indeed was he ever merciful, who 
“was a murderer from the beginning”? For “he 
that is evil to himself, to whom will he be good ? ” 
And who could be more evil to himself than he who 
never repents of his own iniquity, is never brought to 
compunction even by his own sufferings? It was a 
false presumption that forced him originally from the 
way of truth, and now it is a cruel obstinacy that keeps 
the way of mercy closed against him. Hence it is that 
he can never either find mercy in himself or obtain 
mercy from the Lord. In this manner, therefore, have 
all those swelling hills been ‘‘ bent down from the ways 
of His eternity,” inasmuch as they have descended by 


* “ Incurvati sunt colles mundi ab itineribus aeternitatis 
Ejus ” (Hab. iii. 6), which the Douay Translators have rendered, 
« The hills of the world are bowed down by the journeys of His 
eternity.” This, however, does not suit the present context. 
—(Translator.) 


T Q 


242 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


their own tortuous and winding ways, or rather by 
their precipitous plunges, from ways of the Lord 
which are straight and right. But how much more 
prudently and profitably have not other spiritual hills 
been bowed and humbled unto salvation by these same 
ways of His eternity! For they have not, like the 
former, been bent from these ways, so as to diverge 
from their straightness : but the ways of God’s eternity 
have bowed and conformed them to their own recti- 
tude. Do we not even now behold the hills of the 
world bending down, whilst the high and the mighty 
prostrate before the Lord with devoted humility and 
do homage at His feet? Do they not bend, when from 
the perilous heights of their pride and cruelty they 
stoop to the lowly ways of mercy and truth ? 
According to these ways of the Lerd’s eternity are 
directed not only the ways of the holy angels but also 
the ways of the elect amongst men. And in truth the 
first forward step to be taken by the man who has 
just emerged from the slough of vice is to show mercy, 
to have compassion on the only son of his mother, to 
please God by having pity on his own soul, as the Wise 
Man counsels. He who acts in this way, imitates his 
Redeemer in the great work of divine compassion, not 
sparing himself, but crucifying himself with Him Who 
was first crucified for us, and in a manner dying like 
Him for his own salvation. This salutary self-pity is 
the first feeling awakened in his mind after he has 
returned to himself, and it dwells and operates in the 
very centre of his soul. It now remains for him to 
proceed along the royal road of penance and to advance 
therein until he reaches truth. But, as I have often 
reminded you, brethren, compunction of the heart 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 243 


ought not to be separated from confession of the lips. 
“ For with the heart we believe unto justice ; but with 
the mouth confession is made unto salvation.” He 
that is truly converted in heart will of necessity be 
little in his own eyes. For Truth has said, ‘‘ Unless 
you be converted and become as little children, you 
shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven.” Such a 
one, therefore, must not desire to conceal what he 
cannot but know, namely, how completely he has 
been “ brought to nothing.” Let him not be ashamed 
to expose to the light of truth that which he beholds 
(not without an affection of commendable self-com- 
passion) in the secret recesses of his heart. Thus, my 
brethren, does man enter upon the ways of mercy and 
truth, the ways of the Lord, the ways of life. And 
the fruit of these ways is the salvation of the wayfarer. 

It is manifest that the ways of the blessed spirits 
also are directed to the ways of the Lord. For when 
they ascend by divine contemplation, they gaze upon 
the subsisting Truth, Which they satiate their desires 
with, yet still desire though satiated ; and when they 
descend, they exercise mercy in our regard, by keeping 
us in all our ways. As the Apostle says, they “ are all 
ministering spirits sent to minister for them who shall 
receive the inheritance of salvation.” They are our 
ministers, therefore, not the Lord’s. And in this they 
are but imitating the example of the Only-Begotten, 
Who did “not come to be ministered unto but to 
minister,” Who stood amongst His disciples, not as 
their Lord, but as their servant. The fruit of the ways of 
the angels, as regards themselves, is their own beatitude 
and the sweet obedience of charity ; as regards us, it 
is, on the one hand, the communication of heavenly 


244 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


graces, and on the other, the keeping of our ways. 
“For He hath given His angels charge over thee, to 
keep thee in all thy ways,” that is to say, in all thy 
necessities and in all thy desires. Otherwise it would 
be very easy for thee to fall into the ways of death, 
because from necessity thou mightest proceed to ob- 
stinacy, or from cupidity to presumption. And these, 
viz., obstinacy and presumption, are not the ways of 
men, but of demons. For in what are men accus- 
tomed to grow obstinate so easily as in that which 
they look upon, or pretend to look upon, as belonging 
to necessity ? It is to this fact the poet was referring 
when he sang : 


“< Admonish as thou please,’ he makes reply. 
‘ All that is possible to me have I 
The power to do, but nothing more ; and thou, 
If in my place, wouldst say what I say now.’ ” * 


And what is it that impels us to presumption, but the 
violence of a passionate desire ? 

In the meantime, therefore, God “hath given His 
angels charge over thee,” not to withdraw thee from 
thy ways, but rather to keep thee in them, and by 
their own ways to direct thy ways into His ways. 
Dost thou inquire, in what manner? By bringing it 
to pass that what they themselves do from the pure 
and sole motive of charity, thou shalt do at least 
by the persuasion and compulsion of thine own ne- 
cessity : that thou also shalt feel constrained to descend, 


* “ Quidquid moneas, ait ille, ego quod possum, possum, 
et non 


Ultra possum, Tu si hic es, aliter sentias ? ” 
—Terence, Andr., a. 2, S. I. 


ELEVENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 245 


or better, to condescend, by treating thy neighbour with 
compassion ; and again, with the angels, lifting thyself 
aloft on the wings of holy desires, shalt endeavour 
with all the force of thy affection to attain to the 
sovereign and everlasting Truth. Hence the Prophet 
Jeremias admonishes us, saying, “ Let us lift up our 
hearts with our hands to the Lord in the heavens.” 
Hence in the Mass also it is said to us daily, “ Lift up 
your hearts.” And hence the Psalmist reproves us for 
our negligence with the words, “O ye sons of men, 
how long will you be heavy of heart? Why do you 
love vanity and seek after lying ? ” He says “ heavy 
of heart,’ because when the heart is burdened and 
oppressed it cannot be so easily raised up to seek and 
to love the truth. And be not surprised that the holy 
angels do not disdain to admit us with themselves, 
yea, and to draw us into the ways of the Lord, since 
they even condescend to keep us in our own. But 
how much more securely do they walk in these ways, 
and how much more happily! Nevertheless even their 
way of walking in mercy and truth is infinitely inferior 
to His Who is Himself Mercy and Truth. 

How wisely has God disposed these various orders, 
putting each in the place most suitable for it! He 
stands Himself at the apex of the universe, because 
He is supreme, having nothing above Him, nor any- 
thing beyond. His angels He has placed, not indeed 
on the summit, but still in security : they dwell in the 
neighbourhood of Him Who is the Highest, and are 
therefore ‘“‘endued with virtue from above.” The 
place assigned to man is neither on the summit, nor in 
security, but rather in uncertainty. Having the solid 
earth under his feet, he occupies a very low place 


246 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


indeed, yet not the very lowest, so that he has both 
the power and the necessity to walk with caution. The 
demons, finally, live at large in this hanging mass of 
windy, wandering air. For they are unworthy to 
ascend to heaven, and too proud to descend to earth. 
I have now said enough for to-day. And would to 
God we were able by His grace to return sufficient 
thanks to Him from Whom is all our sufficiency! For 
we are not “sufficient to think anything of ourselves, 
as of ourselves, but all our sufficiency is from ” Him, 
“Who giveth to all men abundantly,” and Who is 
over all things, God blessed for ever. Amen. 


TWELFTH SERMON ON THE PSALM XC. 


On GoD’s GOODNESS TO US, AND ON THE RESPECT, 
LOVE, GRATITUDE AND CONFIDENCE DUE TO OUR 
GUARDIAN ANGELS. 


“ For He hath given His angels charge over thee : to keep thee in 
all thy ways.” 


In yesterday’s sermon, as you will remember, I told 
you that the ways of the demons are presumption and 
obstinacy ; and I gave you my reasons for saying so. 
But there is another point of view from which, if you 
judge it necessary, their ways may be examined. For 
although they endeavour by every means to hide them, 
the Holy Spirit constantly frustrates their efforts, and 
in several passages of the Scriptures points out to us 
clearly the paths of the wicked. Thus of all the demons it 
is written, “ The wicked walk round about.” And of 
their leader in particular, we read, “ The devil, as a 
roaring lion, goeth about seeking whom he may devour.” 
He was once even compelled to acknowledge this him- 
self, in the presence of the Divine Majesty. For so it is 
written in Job: “ Now on a certain day when the sons 
of God came to stand before the Lord, Satan also was 
present amongst them. And the Lord said to him: 
whence comest thou? And he answered and said: I 
have gone round about the earth, and walked through 
it.” Let us say, therefore, that the ways of Satan are 
gyrations and circumventions. The latter of these he 
uses with us ; the former he practises in himself. For 
he is always lifting himself up, and is always flung 


247 


248 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


down again: his pride is ever soaring aloft, and ever 
humbled even to the ground. What is this but going 
round in a circle ? And he who thus walks round about, 
moves indeed, but never advances. Woe to the man 
that imitates this gyration by refusing to renounce his 
own will! Should I attempt to draw him away from 
it, he appears to follow me for a little, but in manifest 
pain. He is evidently moving in a circle. He is be- 
ginning already to return—but by another road—to 
the point whence he started, namely, to self-will, from 
which he refuses to be completely detached. He struggles 
to break loose on this side and on that, he endeavours 
to escape, now by one way, now by another, but is 
always held fast by the peg of self-will. 

But, my brethren, if it is an evil thing to be gyrating 
oneself in this manner, it is much worse to be the cir- 
cumventor of another. In truth, it is this particularly 
which makes the devil to be a devil. Yet you may 
ask how can that proudest of creatures descend from 
the loftiness of his pride in order to circumvent miser- 
able mortals ? But observe even here how the wicked 
one walks round about. “He beholdeth every high 
thing,” as holy Job says, yet he also carefully considers 
what is lowest, but only that he may be lifted up the 
more, that he may be the more inflated. For he seems 
to himself to have mounted higher when he has placed 
the humble under his feet. Hence we read in the psalm, 
“Whilst the wicked one acts proudly, the poor is set 
on fire.”’* How perversely do the evil angels thus 
imitate the good in ascending and descending! They 

* « Dum superbit impius, incenditur pauper,” ps. ix, 2. St. 
Bernard takes this to mean that it is a source of pride to the devil 


to have succeeded in kindling in our souls the fire of the 
passions. —(Translator.) 


TWELFTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 249 


ascend from the love of vainglory, and descend through 
the malice of jealousy. As their descent is a cruelty, 
so their ascent is a lie. For, as I told you yesterday, 
the demons are strangers to mercy and truth. But if 
the malignant angels come down for the purpose of 
circumventing us, let us give thanks to Him by Whose 
command the good angels also descend to assist us 
and to keep us in all our ways. Nor is that enough. 
For “in their hands they shall bear thee up: lest thou 
dash thy foot against a stone.” 

Oh, how much instruction, and admonition, and con- 
solation is given us, dearest brethren, in the different 
verses of this psalm! Is there another psalm in the 
entire psalter which so admirably consoles the faint- 
hearted, admonishes the remiss, instructs the ignorant ? 
Therefore it is that divine providence has brought it 
to pass that the words of this should be very frequently 
on the lips of the faithful, especially during the holy 
season of Lent. And the Church seems to have caught 
the suggestion to make so much use of the same psalm 
in her times of fasting, from the use which the devil 
made of it to tempt the Lord after His long fast in the 
desert. Thus, in this matter also, that most wicked 
servant has unwillingly and unwittingly benefited the 
children of God. For what can grieve him more, or 
what can rejoice us more, than the fact that even his 
malice is made to co-operate unto good for us? “ He 
hath given His angels charge over thee: to keep thee 
in all thy ways.” “Let the mercies of the Lord give 
glory to Him, and His wonderful works to the children 
of men.” Let them confess and “say among the 
gentiles : The Lord hath done great things for us: we 
are become joyful.” ‘‘O Lord, what is man that 


250 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


Thou art made known to him ? or the son of man that 
Thou makest account of him?” “Or why dost Thou 
set Thy Heart upon him?” For in truth, Thou dost 
set Thy Heart upon us and “ hast care of us,” and art 
ever “solicitous for us.” Thou hast sent us Thine 
only-begotten Son, Thou hast given us Thy Holy 
Spirit, Thou hast promised us the vision of Thy Face. 
And lest there should be any one at all in the kingdom 
of heaven unoccupied in the care of us, Thou hast 
appointed the blessed angels “ to minister for us,” Thou 
hast charged them with our keeping, and ordered them 
to act as our guides. It is not enough that Thou 
“makest (these) spirits Thy angels,” * but Thou must 
also make them the angels of Thy little ones. For the 
Lord has said, “ Their (the little ones’) angels in heaven 
always see the Face of My Father Who is in heaven.” 
So Thou hast made these blissful spirits Thy mes- 
sengers to us and also our messengers to Thee. 

“ He hath given His angels charge over thee.” t Oh, 
marvellous condescension ! Oh, truly divine affection of 
charity ! For Who is He that hath given charge? And

after SS. Augustine and Gregory the Great, interprets this verse 
in such a manner as to make “angelos tuos’”’ a predicate, so 
that the meaning will be : “ Who employest the heavenly spirits 
as Thy angels or messengers.” St. Paul appears to understand 
it in the same sense (Heb. i.) But according to Theodoret and 
St. Thomas (In c. i. ad Heb.) the predicate is “ spiritus,” and 
the sense, consequently : ‘‘ Who makest Thy angels (messengers) 
as swift as spirits,” that is to say, as the winds, of which there 
is mention in the preceding verse of Psalm ciii., ‘‘ Who walkest 
upon the wings of the winds.” This latter interpretation is
