TWELFTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 251 


to whom hath the charge been given? And concerning 
whom? Brethren, let us diligently study and carefully 
commit to memory the words of this most consoling 
charge. Who is He That hath given charge ? Whose are 
the angels ? Whose commands do they obey ? To Whose 
will are they submissive? Attend to the Psalmist ; 
“ He (the Almighty) hath given His angels charge over 
thee : to keep thee in all thy ways.” Nor do the angels 
show any reluctance to execute the order, since they 
even “bear thee up in their hands.” The Divine 
Majesty, therefore, has given charge to angels, has 
given charge to His own angels, namely, to those sub- 
lime and blissful spirits, who dwell so near Himself, 
who are so intimately united to Him, and are truly 
the “ domestics of God.” And He has given them 
charge over thee. Who art thou? Lord, “ what is 
man that Thou art mindful of him ? or the son of man 
that Thou visitest him ? ” As if indeed man were 
not rottenness, and the son of man but a worm! But 
what, thinkest thou, is the charge He has given con- 
cerning thee? Perhaps, as holy Job feared, He has 
“written bitter things against (thee) and will con- 
sume (thee) for the sins of (thy) youth’? Perhaps 
He has charged His angels “against a leaf that is 
carried away with the wind” to “show their power, 
and to pursue a dry straw ” ? Perhaps He has charged 
them to “take away the impious one that he may 
not see the glory of God” ? No, that charge has not 
been given yet, but the day will come when it shall 
be given. And lest it be given against thee, take good 
care never to depart from “ the aid of the Most High ” 
and always to “ dwell under the protection of the God 
of heaven.” For that terrible charge shall be given, 


252 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


not against, but in behalf of those whom the God of 
heaven protects. And if it is not given now, it is for 
their sake it is deferred, because “all things are for 
the sake of the elect.” For when the servants were 
preparing to go out with the intention of plucking up 
at once the cockle sown amidst the wheat, “ the good 
man of the house coming said to them: suffer both 
to grow until the harvest, lest perhaps, gathering up 
the cockle, you root up the wheat also together with 
it.” But how shall the wheat be preserved until the 
harvest time? This, my brethren, is the task here 
enjoined the angels: this is the work they are busy 
with now. 

And so, “He hath given His angels charge over 
thee to keep thee.” O Word incarnate! O Wheat 
amongst the cockle! O Grain amidst the chaff! O 
“ Lily amongst the thorns”! Let us return thanks 
to Him, my brethren, let us give thanks to Him 
both for you and for me. For He has entrusted you 
to my care as a most precious deposit, the fruit of 
His cross, and the price of His Blood. But He is not 
content with such poor protection for you, so unsafe, 
so useless, so weak, so inadequate; and He therefore 
proclaims by His Prophet, ‘Over thy wall, O 
Jerusalem, I have appointed watchmen.” For they 
who appear to be the walls of the spiritual Jerusalem, 
or the towers of the walls, require themselves, and 
indeed more than others, the vigilance of these 
watchmen. 

“ He hath given His angels charge over thee : to keep 
thee in all thy ways.” O my brother, with how much 
reverence, with how much gratitude, with how much 
confidence ought not these words to inspire thee! 


TWELFTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 253 


With how much reverence for a presence so august ! 
With how much gratitude for benevolence so great ! 
With how much confidence in a keeping so secure ! 
Walk therefore with caution, remembering that the 
angels of God accompany thee in all thy ways, as the 
Lord has commanded them. In every place, whether 
public or private, show respect for thy angel. Surely 
thou wouldst not dare to do in his presence what thou 
wouldst fear to do in mine. But perhaps thou dost 
doubt the presence of him whom thou canst not see ? 
What if thou wert to hear him ? or to touch him ? or 
to scent him? Understand, therefore, that it is not 
by sight alone that we can have certainty of the 
presence of objects. For not even all things belonging 
to the material order are perceptible to sight. Much 
less, then, can it perceive purely spiritual objects, which 
lie beyond the range of every corporeal faculty and 
require to be “ spiritually examined.” But if thou wilt 
consult thy faith, it shall give thee evidence of the 
presence of the angels. I have said advisedly, “ thy 
faith shall give thee evidence,” because the Apostle 
himself defines faith to be “the evidence (argumentum) 
of things that appear not.” The angels are present, 
therefore, and they are present to thee: not only are 
they with thee, but they are with thee to defend thee. 
Yes, my brother, the angels of God stand nigh to assist 
and to protect thee. ‘‘ What (shalt thou) render to the 
Lord for all the things that He hath rendered to 
(thee)? ” For to Him alone is due all the honour 
and glory. Why do I say, “ to Him alone ” ? Because 
it is He Who “ hath given charge.” And “ every best 
gift and every perfect gift is from above,coming down 
from the Father of lights.” 


254 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


Nevertheless, although it is the Lord Who “ hath 
given His angels charge over thee,” we must also show 
ourselves grateful to these celestial spirits, who obey 
the divine injunction with so much charity, and assist 
us in our so great necessity. Let us, therefore, make a 
return of gratitude, yea, and of affection to such 
glorious guardians. Let us give them love for love. 
Let us honour them as much as we are bound to, as 
much as we can. Yet all our reverence and all our 
love must be directed ultimately to Him from Whom 
both angels and men derive their whole capacity for 
honouring and loving, and their whole claim to be 
honoured and loved. But we ought not to interpret 
the Apostle’s words, “To God alone be honour and 
glory ”* in such a manner as to make him contradict 
what the Psalmist says, “ But to me Thy friends, O 
God, are made exceedingly honourable.” In my 
opinion, this passage of St. Paul ought to be under- 
stood in the same sense as what he writes in another 
place, ““ Owe no man anything, but to love one another.” 
For surely he does not here counsel the faithful to 
refuse to acknowledge their other obligations. Especi- 
ally as he has told them in the very last preceding 
verse to “ render to all men their dues : tribute to whom 
tribute is due ; custom to whom custom, fear to whom 
fear, honour to whom honour.” Therefore, in order 
that you may understand more fully what he intends 
to teach or counsel in both these passages, take an 
illustration from the lights of the firmament. Con- 
sider how when the sun is shining the lesser luminaries 

* “ Soli Deo honor et gloria ” (1 Tim. i. 17). The English 
Version has, “ To the only God be honour and glory,” which 


manifestly does not accord with St. Bernard’s context.— 
(Translator. ) 


TWELFTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 255 


are nowhere to be seen. Do you suppose they have © 
been removed from the sky ? Or that they have been 
extinguished ? No, my brethren, that is not the reason. 
They are still in their places, but are so covered and 
surrounded by the more brilliant radiance of the sun 
that they are no longer visible to our eyes. In the 
same way, the law of love ought to overcome all other 
obligations and reign alone in our hearts, appropriating 
to itself whatever is due to other laws, so that we 
may do all things solely from love. Thus also the 
honour due to God must prevail over and in a sense 
exclude the honour claimed by others, so that He 
alone is not only honoured more than any other, but 
is honoured in all others. And this is equally true 
with respect to the charity we should have for God, 
as compared with that which we owe to His creatures. 
For when we have given the Lord our God the love of 
our whole heart, and of our whole soul, of our whole 
mind, and of our whole strength, what love is there left 
to give others? Consequently, let us in Him love His 
angels, and love them tenderly, since we are destined as 
“joint-heirs ’? with them to share in their glory. For 
now indeed we are the sons of God, but “it hath not 
yet appeared what we shall be,” because we are still 
as children “ under tutors and governors, until the 
time appointed by the Father”; and seem in the 
meanwhile to ‘‘ differ nothing from servants.” 

Yet, my brethren, although we are but little ones, 
and although there is still a long road before us, aye, 
and a road beset with dangers innumerable, why should 
we be afraid under the protection of such powerful 
guardians? They who keep us in all our ways cannot 
be conquered by any hostile force; and they can 


256 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


neither be led astray themselves nor lead us astray. 
They are faithful, they are prudent, they are invincible: 
wherefore, then, should we fear? Only let us follow 
them, only let us cling to them, and we shall “ dwell 
in the aid of the Most High’ and “ abide under the 
protection of the God of heaven.” And consider how 
necessary for thee is this protection, this keeping of 
thee in all thy ways. “In their hands,” sings the 
Psalmist, “ they shall bear thee up: lest thou dash 
thy foot against a stone.” Does it seem to thee a 
matter of small account that there is “a stone of 
stumbling in the way ?” ? Attend to the words which 
follow: “Thou shalt walk upon the asp and the 
basilisk ; and thou shalt trample under foot the lion 
and the dragon.” Oh, how needful is a guide, or rather 
a carrier, especially for a little one entering upon this 
danger-strewn road! “In their hands they shall bear 
thee up.” They will keep thee in all thy own ways, 
and will conduct thee wherever, as a child, thou art 
able to walk. But they will not suffer thee to be 
tempted beyond what thou canst endure; and when 
the ‘“‘ stone of stumbling ” appears in the way they 
will take thee up in their arms, and lift thee over. 
Oh, how easily does ke surmount obstacles who is 
borne in the hands of the holy angels! How easy one 
finds it to swim, when (according to the vulgar 
saying) another holds one’s head over water ! 
Therefore, my brethren, I say to each one of you: 
as often as thou feelest the pressure of violent tempta- 
tion, as often as bitter tribulation threatens to engulf 
thee: invoke thy guardian, call upon thy guide, cry 
to thy “helper in due times in tribulation.” Call 
out to him and say, “ Lord, save me, I perish.” “He 


TWELFTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 257 


neither slumbereth nor sleepeth that keepeth ” thee 
(although he sometimes hides himself from thee for a 
space) lest perchance thou, not knowing that he is 
supporting thee, should to thy great peril throw thyself 
out of his arms. These arms of his are to be under- 
stood spiritually. They are, in fact, spiritual suc- 
cours, which are variously and invisibly ministered to 
each of the elect according to the measure of the par- 
ticular difficulty or danger to be encountered, or, as 
it were, according to the magnitude of the “stone of 
stumbling ” ; and they are ministered by angels ap- 
pointed to that office. I shall now make mention of 
some of those trials which I regard as the more common, 
and of which, I am sure, most of you have had ex- 
perience. Suppose you see a person under violent 
temptation. He is severely tried either by bodily in- 
firmity, or by temporal misfortune ; or is assailed by 
spiritual sadness, or suffering from a fainting of soul. 
Already he is beginning to be tempted above what he 
can bear; already he has reached the “stone of 
stumbling” ; and he shall certainly dash his foot against 
it, unless someone comes to help him. But, my brethren, 
what is that stone? I take it to be none other than 
that ‘‘ Stone of stumbling,” that Rock of scandal, upon 
Which “ whosoever shall fall he shall be broken, but 
on whomsoever It shall fall, It shall grind him to 
powder”; the “chief Corner-Stone, elect, precious,” 
that is to say, our Lord Jesus Christ. To fall upon 
this Stone is to murmur against Him, to be scandal- 
ised because of “ pusillanimity of spirit and the storm.” 
And so he who is now on the point of falling, who is 
ready to dash his foot against the Stone, has urgent 
need of angelic consolation, and of angelic hands to 


I. R 


258 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


bear him up. It is evidently true that the murmurer 
or the blasphemer falls upon the Stone of stumbling, 
and that he hurts only himself, not also Him against 
Whom he so blindly dashes his foot. 

It appears to me that persons subject to such tempta- 
‘tions are oftentimes upheld by the spiritual hands of 
angels, in so powerful a manner that they pass over, 
almost unconsciously, the obstacles which had occa- 
sioned them the utmost alarm. And afterwards they 
feel as much surprised at the facility they have 
experienced as they had previously felt dismayed at 
the difficulty which they anticipated. Now do you 
wish me to tell you what is meant in particular (in 
my opinion) by the two hands of the angels? It 
seems to me that they signify a twofold admonition : 
we are borne up in angelic hands whenever we are 
reminded of the shortness of our present tribulation, 
and of the eternity of the future reward: I should 
rather have said, whenever these truths are so engraved 
and imprinted on our hearts that we perceive in a 
most intimate and lively way how “ that which is at 
present momentary and light of our tribulation, worketh 
for us above measure exceedingly an eternal weight of 
glory.” Now who can question that these inspirations 
are the work of the good angels, since it is certain 
that evil suggestions come from the bad angels ? Dearest 
brethren, make the angels of God your familiar friends ; 
frequent their society by constant remembrance and 


fervent prayer ; for they are always nigh to you with 
eomfort and protection. 


THIRTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE FOUR VICES OF OBSTINACY, ENVY, ANGER, 
AND PUSILLANIMITY. 


“In their hands they shall bear thee up : lest thou dash thy feet 
against a stone. Thou shalt walk upon the asp and the basilisk : 
and thou shalt trample under foot the lion and the dragon.” 


“Tn their hands they shall bear thee up; lest thou 
dash thy foot against a stone.” It seems to me, my 
brethren, that these words (which, as you will remem- 
ber, were discussed in yesterday’s sermon also) may 
be understood not only of present consolation, but of 
future comfort as well. I mean to say that the holy 
angels who during life keep us in all our ways, will 
bear us up in their hands when life’s journey is ended. 
To this there are not wanting trustworthy witnesses. 
It is not long since you heard it read to you of our 
holy father St. Benedict (blessed in everything else 
as well as in name) how that, whilst his gaze appeared 
to be riveted on the splendour of some dazzling light, 
he beheld the soul of Germanus, Bishop of Capua, 
being borne to heaven by angels in the form of a fiery 
globe. But wherefore do I offer you human testimony ? 
For Truth Itself tells us in the Gospel concerning the 
poor ulcerous mendicant that “he was carried by 
angels into Abraham’s bosom.” How indeed could we 
walk without their assistance in ways so new to us, 
and in a land so entirely unknown, especially since 
there is a “ stone of stumbling” in our path? What 
is this stone? The stone here is the wicked one who 


259 


260 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


was wont in times past to be worshipped in stones, and 
who offered stones to the Lord, saying, “ If Thou be the 
Son of God, command that these stones be made bread.” 
Moreover, thy foot is thy affection*: her affection is 
the foot of thy soul which the angels bear up in their 
hands lest she should dash it against the stone. Oh, 
how could any soul be otherwise than extremely alarmed 
if she had to make alone that last long journey, if she 
had to enter upon those perilous paths without conso- 
lation, if she had to walk with her own feet along that 
stone-bestrewn way ! ; 

But I will offer thee yet stronger proof of the need 
thou hast to be borne in other hands, and in none 
other than those of the angels. ‘‘ Thou shalt walk 
upon the asp and the basilisk : and thou shalt trample 
under foot the lion and the dragon.” How should the 
human foot fare amongst such stones? What affec- 
tion of the soul could pass without hurt through the 
midst of such horrible monsters; which are nothing 
else than the “spirits of wickedness,” here designated 
by most appropriate symbolic names? For it is of 
them the Psalmist has told thee what I do not believe 
thou hast forgotten, namely, that “a thousand shall 
fall at thy side, and ten thousand at thy right hand.” 
But perhaps we may suppose that the powers of hell 
have so divided amongst them the works of malice, 
the functions of iniquity, that, deriving their names 
from their particular offices, they are called—one an 
asp, another a basilisk, a third a lion, and a fourth 
a dragon: In other words, they are named asps, or 
basilisks, or lions, or dragons, according to their special 


* “ Pes tuus affectio tua.” St. Augustine (In Ps. xciv.) has: 
“ Pedes nostri in hoc itinere affectus ; quasi duo pedes amor 
et timor.’’—(Translator.) 


THIRTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 261 


invisible manner of injuring souls, whether by their bite, 
or by their glance, or by their roar and violence, or 
even by their breath. I remember to have read in the 
Gospel of a certain kind of evil spirit that it “is not 
cast out but by prayer and fasting.” The words of 
apostolic reproof had no power to dislodge it. Have 
we not here the asp which the Psalmist represents as 
deaf, where he says, “ Like a deaf asp that stoppeth 
her ears, which will not hear the voice of the charmer ” ? 
Dost thou desire to be delivered from the fear of so 
terrible a monster? Dost thou desire to be able after 
death to walk upon this formidable asp ? If so, be 
careful not to walk after him in life. Take care not to 
imitate him now and he shall have no power to frighten 
thee hereafter. 

There is one vice, my brethren, over which, as it 
seems to me, this kind of devil has full dominion. And 
if you wish to know what it is, it is that spiritual cir- 
cuitousness, against which I warned you in yesterday’s 
discourse : it is that obstinacy which I admonished you 
to beware of in the sermon last preceding. .For I 
never weary of warning you (whenever the opportunity 
offers) to avoid with all diligence so deadly a pest. 
Indeed this vice is the subversion of all religion ; and 
according to the testimony of the Hebrew Legislator, 
“the venom of asps is incurable.” It is said to be the 
custom of the asp to press one of his ears as strongly 
as he can against the ground, and to stop the other 
with the end of his tail, so as to prevent himself from 
hearing. How then can he be influenced by the voice 
of the charmer, by the voice of the preacher? I can 
only pray for him; I can only humble my soul in 
penance for him ; I can only baptise myself for his 


262 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


dead soul in an abundant flood of compassionate tears: 
for I see that such a one is spiritually dead, and that 
no wisdom of human charmer, no labour of human ad- 
monisher can overcome his insensibility. However, let 
the obstinate soul remember that it is not in heaven 
but in the earth the asp is wont to hide his head. For 
the wisdom which is from above is, according to St. 
James, not only “modest,” but also “ peaceable,” 
that is to say, docile ; whilst the stubborn wisdom of 
the asp cannot but be “ of the earth, earthly.” But 
the pressing of his ear to the earth would not be enough 
to cause complete deafness, unless his tail were also 
employed to stop his hearing. Now what does this tail 
signify ? It signifies the evil end which the soul pro- 
poses to herself in her intentions. This it is which 
makes her deafness desperate. For now she is on the 
one hand bowed to the earth by her attachment to her 
own will, and on the other hand, by bending back her 
tail (if I may so speak), she assiduously contemplates 
and holds steadily before her mind the evil object 
which she wishes to obtain. Do not, my brethren, do 
not, I beg of you, ever stop the ear of your conscience, 
ever harden your hearts. For the reason why the 
mouth of the obstinate man is always full of sharp 
and bitter words is this, because no benevolent ad- 
monition can penetrate to his soul. Hence the poison 
of the asp is always felt in the sting of his tongue, 
because he has hardened himself with so much dili- 
gence against the tongue of the charmer. 

The basilisk is the most baneful and abominable 
of all irrational creatures. It is said to carry its 
venom in its eye. Do you wish to know what this 
venomous eye, this evil eye, this fascinating eye, signifies 


THIRTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 263 


spiritually ? Believe me, it is the vice of envy. For what 
is it to be envious but to look with an evil eye? Were 
the devil that seduced our first parents not a basilisk, 
death would never have entered the world by his envy. 
Alas for miserable man, that he does not take due pre- 
cautions against this envious one! My brethren, let 
us overcome this vice whilst we are still in the body, 
if we wish to have no fear after death of a demon 
of such monstrous iniquity. Let no one look with an 
envious eye upon his brother’s spiritual prosperity. 
For by doing so he infects it with his poison, so to 
speak, and in a manner kills it—as far as it depends 
upon him. “ Whosoever hateth his brother is a mur- 
derer,” according to the testimony of Truth. And 
what of him who hates the spiritual good of his brother ? 
Does he not likewise deserve to be called a murderer ? 
In the former case, thy brother still lives, yet thou art 
guilty of his death ; in the latter the fire of charity, 
which the Lord Jesus came to cast upon the earth, still 
burns in the breast of thy brother, and nevertheless 
thou art already condemned as having by thy envy 
extinguished the flame. 

But woe to us because of the dragon! For the 
dragon is a monster of prodigious size, which kills what- 
ever it touches with its fiery breath, as well the birds 
of heaven as the beasts of the earth. In my opinion, 
by this dragon is to be understood the spirit of anger. 
How many, even of those who appeared to be living a 
life of exalted virtue, have we not lamented to see 
miserably blasted by the dragon’s breath and falling 
shamefully into his mouth! How much better had it 
been for them to have been angry with themselves, as 
the Psalmist counsels, so that they might not sin! 


264 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


The human passion of anger is a good natural affec- 
tion : but the abuse of the gifts of nature brings heavy 
loss and lamentable ruin. Therefore, my brethren, let 
us exercise this passion in its legitimate sphere, lest 
otherwise it should begin to occupy itself with things 
illicit or unprofitable. It is thus that one love is wont 
to destroy another; and thus one fear is accustomed 
to be expelled by another. “Be not afraid of them 
who kill the body,” says the Lord, “and after that 
have no more than they can do.” And He added 
immediately, “ But I will shew you Whom you shall 
fear : fear Him Who, after He hath killed, hath power 
to cast into hell. Yea, I say to you, fear Him.” That 
is to say, we are to fear God in order to be delivered 
from the fear of man: we are to be filled with the 
spirit of the fear of the Lord, and then there shall be 
no room in us for any other fear. And I counsel you, 
my dearest brethren, yet not I but the Lord, not I 
but Truth: be not angry with them who take from 
you transitory goods, or who heap insult upon you, 
or who even perchance inflict upon you physical suffer- 
ing, “and after that have no more that they can do.” 
But I will show you what you ought to be angry with. 
Be angry with that which alone has power to hurt you, 
which alone can prevent all temporal evils from bene- 
fiting rather than injuring you. Shall I tell you what 
it is? It is your own personal iniquity. ‘‘ Yea, I say 
to you,” be angry with that. For no adversity shall 
have power to harm you if no iniquity reigns in you.* 
He who is angry as he ought to be, with this evil, shall 


* “Nulla nocebit adversitas, si nulla dominetur iniquitas ” 
—from the Collect of Vespers of the Friday preceding Quad- 
ragesima Sunday.—(Translator.) 


THIRTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 265 


be found indifferent to all others, or he will even wel- 
come them. “I am ready for stripes,” he will say 
with the Psalmist ; “‘ come reverses, come reproaches, 
come bodily pain : ‘ I am ready and am not disturbed,’ 
because ‘my sorrow (for my sins) is always in my 
sight.” Why should we not esteem as nothing all 
external troubles in comparison with this domestic 
affliction ? Why should we not, each of us, say with 
King David, “‘ Behold my son who came forth from 
my bowels persecuteth me, and shall I be angry with 
an impudent servant? ” “ My heart is troubled, my 
strength hath left me, and the light of my eyes itself 
is not with me ” : and shall I bewail temporal losses ? 
shall I allow myself to be troubled by bodily 
discomforts ? 

It is thus, my brethren, that we acquire not only 
the spirit of meekness which the dragon’s breath can- 
not injure, but also a magnanimity which the roaring of 
the lion cannot terrify, “Your adversary, the devil, 
as a roaring lion goeth about,” says the Apostle Peter. 
Yes, he can roar, but thanks to the great “ Lion of the 
tribe of Juda,” he cannot hurt us. Let him roar as 
much as he pleases: but let not the flock of Christ flee 
from him. How he threatens! How he rages! How 
he strives to overwhelm us with fear! But let us 
stand firm. Let us not show ourselves such helpless 
beasts as to be overcome by his mere roaring. We 
are told by writers who have made a careful study of 
these matters, that no animal can stand before the 
roařing of the lion, not even such as will repel his actual 
onslaught with the greatest courage ; he puts to rout by 
his roaring the enemy that would resist his attack. 
He, therefore, is truly a beast, truly devoid of reason, 


266 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


whosoever is so pusillanimous as to succumb to pure 
fear, who is conquered before the battle by the mere 
anticipation of labour, who is slain not by the sword 
but by the trumpet. “You have not yet resisted 
unto blood,” said that strenuous leader, who knew so 
well how harmless is the roaring of the infernal lion. 
And another Apostle tells us, “ Resist the devil and 
he will fly from you.” 


FOURTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON GOD’S FOURFOLD CLAIM TO OUR GRATITUDE; 
ON THE DEVIL’S FOURFOLD TEMPTATION, AND ON 
THE FOURFOLD VIRTUE WHEREWITH THIS MUST 
BE ENCOUNTERED. 


“Thou shalt walk upon the asp and the basilisk : and thou shalt 
trample under foot the lion and the dragon.” 


My brethren, let us give thanks to our Creator, to 
our Benefactor, to our Redeemer, to Him in Whom 
we have placed all our hope, or I should rather say, 
to Him Who is Himself all ourhope. For He Who is our 
Redeemer already, is likewise to be our Reward : nor do 
we now seek from Him any other reward than Himself.* 
We are ourselves the first gift He has bestowed upon 
us. For, as the Psalmist sings, “‘ He made us and not 
we ourselves.” Dost thou, O man, esteem it a small 
favour that He has made thee? But consider what 
He has made thee: a noble creature, even according 
to thy material part, yet nobler far in thy soul, which 
is stamped with the Creator’s likeness, endowed with 
the faculty of reason, and with the capacity for ever- 
lasting bliss. Moreover, according to both soul and 
body, thou hast been made admirable above all other 
earthly creatures, thy two elements of spirit and flesh 
being united together in an incomprehensible manner 


* We read of St. Thomas that once, whilst praying before a 
crucifix at Naples, he heard a voice saying to him : “ Thou hast 
written well of Me, Thomas; what reward wilt thou have ? ” 
And the holy Doctor made answer: ‘‘ None other, Lord, than 
Thyself.” —(Translator.) 


267 


268 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


by the unsearchable wisdom of the Creator. And in 
proportion to the greatness of man is the greatness of 
the favour which God has done thee by creating thee. 
What is more, that favour was entirely gratuitous. Can 
it be doubted? Before his creation, clearly, man had 
no merit, since as yet he had no being at all. But 
perhaps it was expected that after receiving existence 
he would make an adequate return to his Maker? “I 
have said to the Lord,” sings the Psalmist, “ Thou art 
my God, for Thou hast no need of my goods.” There- 
fore there was no expectation that man would render 
requital to Him Who is absolutely self-sufficing, but 
he was expected to render humble thanks to Him Who 
has so many claims to his gratitude. Why should he 
not return thanks? Were another to restore to thee 
the light of thy eyes, or the power of hearing, or the 
sense of smell, or the free control of hand or foot, or 
the use of reason, by some cause suspended : who would 
not be violently angry with thee if he found thee at 
any time either forgetful of the benefit, or ungrateful 
to the benefactor? But the Lord thy God has given 
thee not only the use and exercise of thy members 
and faculties, but the members and faculties them- 
selves, all of which He created for thee out of nothing. 
Yes, it was He Who made them, Who formed and 
fashioned them, and appointed its proper function for 
each. How then does He not deserve by every right 
and title all the gratitude thou art capable of for benefits 
so great ? 

But not content with creating thee, great though that 
benefit was, He Who caused thee to be when before 
thou wert not, gave thee also the means of preserving 
thy existence. And He showed Himself as generous 


FOURTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 269 


in this as He was admirable in the work of thy creation. 
Regarding the first benefit He said, ‘‘Let Us make 
man to Our image and likeness,” but with respect to 
the second, “ Let him have dominion over the fishes 
of the sea, and the fowls of the air, and the beasts, 
and the whole earth, and every creeping creature that 
moveth upon the earth.” He had already announced 
that the heavenly bodies were created for thy use ; for 
they are represented as having been made in order to 
be “for signs and for seasons, for days and years.” 
For whose benefit, thinkest thou ? Evidently for thine 
alone ; because other creatures either have no need of 
such things, or have no understanding thereof. How 
bountiful He has been in this second benefit, how in- 
comparably generous! How liberally He has pro- 
vided for thy support, for thy instruction, for thy con- 
solation, for thy correction also, if ever thou shouldst 
need it, yea, and even for thy innocent pleasure! And 
both these benefits were doubly gratuitous. Why do 
I say they were doubly gratuitous? Because they 
neither cost thee any merit to obtain, nor the Lord 
any labour to bestow. For ‘‘ He spoke and they were 
made, He commanded and they were created.” But 
oughtest thou to be the less devoted to Him, the less 
indebted to Him, the less grateful to Him, for the 
reason that the benefits obtained cost Him no more 
to give than they cost thee to acquire? It is one of the 
characteristics of a perverted heart to seek out occa- 
sions of ingratitude. No one acts in this way but he 
who is disposed to be ungrateful even without occasion 
or excuse. I suppose thou wilt acknowledge that neither 
of these two benefits of God is the less useful to thee 
because it cost thy Benefactor no labour? But if, 


270 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


perchance, thou shouldst esteem gifts bestowed upon 
thee the more highly in proportion to the trouble and 
expense to the donor, thou hast this judgment from 
thyself, and (as I think) hast learned it from no other 
teacher than thine own heart. Thou dost not even 
allow it to be used as a standard by thine own bene- 
ficiaries. For thou art more willing to oblige thy 
brother whenever thou canst do so without inconveni- 
ence to thyself. And nevertheless, although the act 
of kindness has cost thee nothing, thou shouldst be 
very displeased if that were alleged as an excuse for 
ingratitude. 

But consider now the third benefit of God, I mean 
the work of thy redemption. With regard to this, at 
any rate thou hast not even the shadow of an excuse 
for ingratitude, since it certainly cost thy Benefactor 
labour. It was indeed a gratuitous favour, yet only 
free of cost so far as concerned thee, not also on the 
side of the Redeemer. Thou hast been saved without 
merit on thy part, but not without suffering on His. 
Wherefore, then, does thy affection still slumber ? Nay, 
it must be rather dead than slumbering, if it 
does not respond to a benefit so great, if it does 
not pour itself out unreservedly in “ thanksgiving 
and the voice of praise.” For this third benefit power- 
fully commends the two preceding, by most evidently 
proving them to have been the genuine effects of love, 
which were bestowed without labour on the part of 
God, not because He was unwilling to bestow them 
otherwise, but because it was not expedient that He 
should otherwise bestow them. Therefore the Lord 
thy God made thee; He made many other creatures 
for thee; aye, and He even made Himself for thee. 


FOURTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 271 


For what says the Evangelist? “The Word was made 
flesh and dwelt among us.” What more remains for 
Him to do? One thing more. He has made Himself 
one flesh with thee : He designs now to make thee one 
spirit with Himself. O my brother, never allow any 
of these four favours to leave thy heart, or thy lips, 
or thy memory, or thy affection. Keep the thought 
of them ever in thy mind, and the joy of them ever 
in thy heart. Employ each one of them as a stimulant 
to spur on thy soul; make use of them as torches to 
set thy heart on fire. Thus shalt thou make Him a 
return of love, Who has proved His love for thee in so 
many ways. But remember His words to the dis- 
ciples, “ If you love Me, keep My commandments.” 
Therefore, keep the commandments of thy Creator, 
keep the commandments of thy Benefactor, of thy 
Redeemer, of thy Rewarder. 

The benefits, then, are four: how many are the 
commandments? We all know that they are ten in 
number: now multiply ten by four, the tenfold ob- 
servance of the law by the fourfold thanksgiving, and 
thou hast forty, the mystic quadragesimal number. 
Only be sure now to “stand in justice and fear, and 
prepare thy soul for temptation.” Beware of the 
serpent’s craft; be on thy guard against the enemy’s 
ambush. For he will endeavour to prevent by a 
fourfold temptation the fourfold thanksgiving thou 
art required to make. The Lord Jesus Himself was 
tempted in all these ways, as St. Paul truly testifies, 
where he says of Him that “He was tempted in all 
things like as we are, without sin.” Perhaps there may 
be someone who is surprised at this and saying to 
himself, “I do not remember to have read of any 


272 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


fourth temptation of the Lord.” But I think such a one 
will be convinced of what I say if he recalls the testimony 
of holy Job, ‘‘ The life of man upon earth is a warfare.’ 
For bearing this in mind, he will understand that the 
temptations to intemperance in the desert, to vainglory 
on the pinnacle, and to ambition on the mountain-top, 
were not the only ones endured by Christ.* Each of 
these was a manifest temptation. But more violent 
than any of them, although also more secret, was the 
temptation which from that time never left Him until 
His death on the cross. Nor is the hiddenness of this 
long-continued trial out of keeping with the correspon- 
dence I have asserted to exist between the four benefits 
and the four temptations. For with regard to the 
benefits also, the three preceding, which have been 
already conferred, are, as thou knowest, very apparent 
and visible; whilst the fourth, which is to fulfil our 
longing for eternal life, has not yet been bestowed, not 
yet experienced. What wonder, therefore, if the fourth 
temptation is concealed, since the benefit which occa- 
sions it is also concealed ? But it is the most powerful 
and persistent of them all, because in it the wicked 
one makes use of all the resources of his malice to 
frustrate our hope. 

In the first place, therefore, he strives to render thee 
ungrateful to the Author of thy nature by inspiring 
thee with an excessive solicitude for that nature : just as 
he had the impudence to say to Christ Himself during 
His fast in the desert, “‘ Command that these stones be 
made bread.” As if He Who fashioned us did not know 


* So we read in St. Luke that, when Christ had repelled the 
third temptation, “‘ the devil departed from Him for a time.” — 
(Translator.) 


FOURTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 273 


our weakness and our many corporal necessities : as if 
He had no care for men Who feeds the very birds of the 
air! Oh,what ingratitude art thou guilty of towards Him 
Who, for thy sake, created the whole material universe, 
if ever thou shouldst dare to fall down and adore the 
demon, in order to obtain from him the worldly wealth 
thou dost covet! ‘‘ All these will I give thee,” he 
says, “if falling down thou wilt adore me.” O most 
miserable one! Art thou, then, the creator of the 
things thou dost promise ? Or how canst thou bestow 
what the Lord has created? Or how are those goods 
which He has made and retains in His own possession 
to be hoped for from thee, and to be purchased by 
paying thee homage? But as to the diabolical sug- 
gestion, “ Cast thyself down,” be on thy guard, who- 
soever thou art that hast been placed on the pinnacle 
of the temple ; be careful of thyself, thou who standest 
in the watch-tower of the house of God ; beware thou 
who appearest to hold the position of honour and emin- 
ence in the Church of Christ. How ungrateful shouldst 
thou prove thyself, nay rather how unjust to Him Who 
has entrusted to thy stewardship that “ great mystery 
of piety,” if thou wert to administer the same for thine 
own profit, ‘supposing gain to be godliness’?! How 
unfaithful wouldst thou be to Him Who has conse- 
crated this ministry with His own most precious Blood, 
if in it thou soughtest only thine own glory, which is 
nothing, if thou soughtest the things that are thine 
own, “not the things that are Jesus Christ’s ” ! How 
unworthily wouldst thou correspond to His incredible 
condescension, Who has made thee so honourable in 
the dispensation of mysteries where He appears Him- 
self so humble, Who has committed to thy charge His 


I, 8 


274 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


holy sacraments, and invested thee with heavenly 
powers, with powers perhaps greater than He has com- 
municated even to the supernal spirits: how unworthily 
wouldst thou correspond to all this goodness, wert thou 
to “ cast thyself down ” by “ minding (not) the things 
that are above,” but “the things that are upon the 
earth” |! Yea, everyone that descends or casts himself 
down from the sublimity of virtue to the quest after 
vainglory, undoubtedly requites with insult the good- 
ness of the “ Lord of virtues,” Who suffered so much 
on earth in order to stamp our souls with the seal of 
His own divine sanctity. 

Let us consider more attentively the first tempta- 
tion, which takes occasion from corporal necessity to 
attack the soul; and let us see whether it ought not 
to be likened to the asp. This reptile wounds by 
fiercely biting, and stops its ears so that it may not 
hear the voice of the charmer. Now, what does the 
tempter strive to do in such temptations? He en- 
deavours to stop and deafen the ear of the heart against 
the consolations of faith. But “the enemy had no 
advantage over Him” Who said, “ not in bread alone 
doth man live, but in every word that proceedeth from 
the mouth of God,” for he could not stop the spiritual 
ear of Christ. As to the temptation wherein it is said, 
“ All these will I give thee, if falling down Thou wilt 
adore me,” do you not discern in it the very hiss of 
the infernal dragon insidiously inciting to sin? * This

draconis.” This is quite a remarkable instance of onomatopoeia,

monster, we are told, lying concealed in the sand, can 
draw down to him by the power of his poisonous breath 
even the birds as they fly in the air.. And what deadly 
poison was contained in that breath which he breathed 
upon the Lord, when he spoke the words, ‘“ All these 
will I give Thee, if falling down Thou wilt adore me ” ! 
But Christ was not like other birds, and the dragon’s 
breath had no power to do Him harm. 

What shall I say of the basilisk? The most ter- 
rible of all beasts is he, and the very sight of him is 
sufficient (so we are assured) to poison and kill human 
beings. Unless I mistake, this monstrous creature is 
a type of vainglory.* “Take heed,” said the Saviour, 
“that you do not your justice before men, to be seen 
by them.” As if He should say to us, ‘‘ Beware of the 
eyes of the basilisk.” But whom are we told the 
basilisk has power to hurt? Only the man who does 
not see the basilisk. For if thou seest him before he 
has seen thee, he can do thee no harm, but (so they 
say) dies himself. At any rate, such is the case with 
the spiritual basilisk. Vainglory can kill only such as 
do not see it, the blind and the negligent, who expose 
themselves to it and present themselves before it : who

do not rather fix their eyes upon it, observe it, study 
it, and perceive how silly a thing it is, how transitory, 
how vain, how worthless. For thou slayest the basilisk 
by scrutinising him in this manner: thou art not con- 
quered by vainglory, but art rather its conqueror and 
destroyer, having reduced it to smoke (so to speak), 
yea, brought it even to nothingness. There is no need, 
I think, to explain why I have understood as referring 
to vainglory the temptation contained in the words, 
“ If Thou be the Son of God, cast Thyself down.” For 
wherefore would He consent to do such a thing but in 
order to be seen and applauded by the crowd ? 

But notice, my brethren, how the basilisk concealed 
himself, so that he might not be observed before he 
was an observer. “For it is written,” he said, “ that 
He hath given His angels charge over thee, and in 
their hands shall they bear thee up.” What is written, 
O malignant one? What, I ask, is written? “ He 
hath given His angels charge over thee.” Yes, but 
what is the charge He hath given? Consider, my 
brethren, and take notice how this wicked and de- 
ceitful basilisk suppresses that which would expose 
his imposture. For what is the charge given by God 
to His angels? That which follows in the psalm, 
“To keep thee in all thy ways.” But does He charge 
them to keep me even if I should throw myself down 
a precipice? O miserable one, what manner of way 
is this which thou art suggesting, this headlong plunge 
from the pinnacle of the temple? Surely it would be 
more proper to speak of it as a ruinous fall than as a 
simple way. Or if a way it be, it is thy way, but not 
mine. Vainly hast thou employed as a temptation 
against the Head what was written for the comfort of 


FOURTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 277 


the body. For it is the body that requires to be 
guarded, and that occasions the fear lest it dash its 
foot against a stone. But the Head, Who has nothing 
to be afraid of, has consequently no need of protection.* 
Why dost thou not quote the passage which follows : 
“Thou shalt walk upon the asp and the basilisk, and 
thou shalt trample under foot the lion and the 
dragon”? Because no doubt these words appertain 
to thyself. Monstrous malignity is here designated by 
monstrous appellatives, and represented as doomed to 
be trampled on, not only by the Head, but by the 
whole body as well. For after having been thrice put 
to confusion, the demon no longer attacked the Lord 
with the craft of the serpent,f but exhibited against 
Him thenceforward the cruelty of the lion, unto con- 
tumely, and stripes, and blows, and death, yea, unto 
the death of the cross. However, the “Lion of the 
tribe of Juda hath conquered” and manifestly “ trampled 
under foot the lion”’ of hell. So also against ourselves, 
my brethren, when he finds his other temptations 
unavailing, the infernal enemy gives full vent to his 
ferocious anger, and stirs up a persecution “such as 
hath not been from the beginning,” in order that at 
least the bitterness of the tribulation might shut 
against our hopes the city of God. Happy the soul 
that, mighty in her virtue, tramples even the lion 
under foot, and prevails so far as to bear away by 
violence the kingdom of heaven ! 

Therefore, my dearest brethren, let us proceed in 
future with the utmost wariness and circumspection,

like persons walking on asps and basilisks. Let us 
be always on our guard against every “‘ root of bitter- 
ness,” so that none of us may be found fractious, or 
arrogant, or impatient, or unforgiving, or disobedient. 
Let us not cast ourselves down, but rather let us rise 
above and leap over the deadly fascination of tem- 
poral glory; so that, according to what is written— 
“ A net is spread in vain before the eyes of them that 
have wings ’’—we may also “trample under foot the 
lion and the dragon,” undismayed by the roaring of 
the one, and untouched by the poison of the other. 
These four monsters, so it seems to me, lie in wait 
for the four cardinal passions of our nature, one for 
each. What passion does the dragon study to sur- 
prise? Doubtless, cupidity, because he knows that it 
is the root of all evils, and how readily. it corrupts the 
heart. Hence he said to the Lord with such urgency, 
“ All these will I give Thee, if falling down Thou wilt 
adore me.” As for the lion, there is no need to inform 
you that his terrific roaring is only heard at the gates 
of fear. The asp is known to haunt the threshold of 
sadness and he hopes by his biting to gnaw his way 
through the door. Hence he did not dare to approach 
Christ until he observed Him to be hungry. Joy, on 
the other hand, has need to beware of the basilisk, 
because it is by the avenue of this affection that he is 
wont to dart into the soul his poisonous glances: that 
is to say, vainglory can only hurt us by means of the 
vain joy it engenders.* 

* According to St. Augustine, the verse under discussion 
here contains a prediction of the four kinds of persecutors from 
whom the Church was destined to suffer: the asp, typifying 
the Jews ; the basilisk, the heretics ; the lion, the pagans ; and 


the dragon, Antichrist. For other interpretations see Bellar- 
min’s Commentary.—(Translator.) 


FOURTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 279 


But consider also, my brethren, if we cannot oppose 
to these four temptations the four cardinal virtues. 
Where is the man that does not tremble when the lion 
begins to roar? If there be any such, look upon him 
as a man of fortitude. But when the lion has been 
baffled, the dragon conceals himself in the sand, in 
order to attract the soul by his poisonous breath, 
blowing into her (if I may so speak) the desire for 
earthly things. Who shall be able to avoid this hidden 
danger? No one certainly but the man of prudence. 
But perhaps whilst thou art carefully guarding thyself 
against both the lion and the dragon, thou meetest 
with some disappointment : and behold the asp makes 
his appearance at once, because this he regards as a 
favourable opportunity. Who shall avoid impatience 
under the sting of this asp? Only he that is tem- 
perate and modest, knowing, like the Apostle, how 
“to abound and to suffer need.” It is now, I think, 
after such victories, that the evil eye (of the basilisk) 
will try to fascinate thee with its treacherous charms. 
And shalt thou be able to turn away thy face? Most 
assuredly, if yet thou art a just man, who not only 
does not take to himself the glory that belongs to 
God, but even declines to accept it when offered to 
him by another; who not merely does what is just, 
but does it justly; who does not his justice before 
men to be seen by them; who, lastly, does not make 
his justice a reason for holding his head high. For 
the virtue of justice is closely related to humility *; it is 
the virtue which purifies the intention ; and it acquires 
merit the more truly and abundantly in proportion as 
it arrogates nothing to itself. 


* Cf. St. Bernard’s Sermons on the Canticle of Cazriticles, 
ol. ii. p. 41, note.—(Translator.) 


FIFTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON THE INSUFFICIENCY OF HUMAN AND ANGELIC 
PROTECTION, WITHOUT THE DIVINE; AND ON WHAT 
IS MEANT BY KNOWING THE NAME OF GOD. 


“ Because he hoped in Me, I will deliver him: I will protect 
him because he hath known My name.” 


“ Come to Me all you that labour and are burdened,” 
says the Lord Jesus,“ and I will refresh you. Take up 
My yoke upon you . . . and you shall find rest to your 
souls. For My yoke is sweet and My burden light.” 
He calls to refreshment them that labour, He invites 
to rest them that are burdened. Nevertheless, He 
does not promise to relieve them at once of every 
labour and burden, but only to exchange the labour 
and the burden which oppresses them for another, for a 
sweet yoke and a light burden, wherein, although it is 
not apparent how, they shall yet find rest and refresh- 
ment. Heavy, no doubt, is the burden of iniquity, 
sitting upon the talent of lead.* Under this weight 
was the Psalmist groaning when he exclaimed, “ My 
iniquities are gone over my head, and as a heavy burden 
are become heavy upon me.” What, then, is the 
burden of Christ? What is this light burden which 
He offers us? As I believe, it is the burden of His 
benefits. A light and sweet burden, surely, yet only 
for him who feels it, who has experience of it ; because 


* This seems to beanallusion to Zacharias v. 7-8, of which 


the Saint’s reading differed apparently from the Vulgate. Cf. 
Sermons on the Canticle of Canticles, vol. i. p. 268.—(Translator). 


280 


FIFTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 28r 


if thou feelest it not or dost not make account of it, 
it becomes very heavy indeed, yea, and very dangerous. 
Man, my brethren, is a beast of burden during the 
whole time of his mortality. If he yet bears the weight 
of his sins, his burden is an oppressive one. If the 
load of guilt has perchance been removed from his 
shoulders, he is now indeed less painfully laden ; but 
if he is truly wise, he shall discover this very unbur- 
dening to be itself no less sensible a burden than the 
burden of which it relieves him. For God burdens 
us by unburdening us. He burdens us with His 
goodness when He unburdens us of our sins. Listen 
to the voice of a benefit-burdened soul: “‘ What shall 
I render to the Lord for all the things that He hath 
rendered to me ?”’ Listen to another, “ Depart from 
me, for I am a sinful man, O Lord.” And to another, 
“ I have always feared God as waves swelling over me, 
and His weight I was not able to bear.” “I have 
always feared,” says holy Job. That is, not only 
before, but also after obtaining the benefit of grace 
and pardon. Happy he who is thus always fearful, 
and is as much afraid of being oppressed by the benefits 
of God as by the weight of his own sins! * 

My brethren, the reason why, in the Holy Scrip- 
tures, the divine beneficence, so constant and so 
bountiful towards us, is kept continually before our 
eyes, is in order to excite us to gratitude and to 
inflame us with love. “He hath given His angels 
charge over thee: to keep thee in all thy ways.” 
“What more could (He) have done for thee and 
hath not done it? ” But I know what thou art 


* The Saint is here insinuating that the benefits of God 
oppress the soul when they are not requited with thanksgiving. 
—-(Translator.) 


282 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


now thinking of, high-born creature that thou art. 
Thou art thankful indeed to the angels of the Lord for 
their kindness in keeping thee: but thy soul is yearn- 
ing for the Lord of the angels. Thou art wishing and 
praying that He would not be content with sending 
servants, but would come and speak to thee Himself ; 
that He would kiss thee, not through any intermediary, 
but with the kiss of His own Mouth. Thou hast heard 
Him promise that “ thou shalt walk upon the asp and 
the basilisk, and shalt trample under foot the lion and 
the dragon’’; nor art thou unaware of the victory 
won over the dragon by Michael and his angels. Never- 
theless, not to Michael, but to Michael’s Lord do thy 
desires cry aloud, ‘‘ Deliver me, O Lord, and set me 
beside Thee, and let any man’s hand fight against 
me.” The soul that has such dispositions may be said 
to seek a refuge not only higher than what is high, 
but higher than all except the highest. Therefore, 
inasmuch as she says with sincerity, “ Because Thou, 
O Lord, art my hope,” she deserves to receive the 
answer, ‘‘ Thou hast made the Most High thy refuge.” 

For the Lord, Who is “ compassionate and merciful,” 
does not disdain to be the hope of the miserable ; He 
does not refuse to show Himself the Deliverer and 
the Protector of them that trust in Him. “ Because 
he hoped in Me, I will deliver Him: I will protect 
him, because he hath known My name.” Very neces- 
sary is this protection; for “unless the Lord build 
the house, they labour in vain that build it: unless 
the Lord keep the city, he watcheth in vain that 
keepeth it,” whether he be an angel or a man. ‘‘ Mount- 
tains are round about” Jerusalem, but that would 
not be sufficient for its protection, nay, it would be no 


FIFTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 283 


protection at all, unless also ‘‘ the Lord is round about 
His people.” With reason, therefore, is it said of the 
Spouse in the Canticle, that after finding “ the watch- 
men who kept the city ”—I should rather have said, 
after she had been found by the watchmen, since 
‘she was not seeking them but the Bridegroom—she 
did not pause, but having questioned them briefly 
concerning her Beloved, sped forward more swiftly 
in pursuit of Him. For her heart had put its hope, 
not in the watchmen, but in the Lord. And 
had anyone tried to induce her to seek a refuge 
outside of Him, she was ready to answer with 
the Psalmist, “ In the Lord I put my trust: how 
do you say to my soul: Get thee away from hence to 
the mountain like a sparrow ? ” The Corinthians were 
not careful to follow this example of the Spouse. For 
on meeting the watchmen, they turned aside to tarry 
with them instead of passing on, and so began to say, 
“ I indeed am of Paul, and I am of Apollo, and I am 
of Cephas.” But what was the conduct of these sober- 
minded sentinels, these vigilant watchmen ?—for 
they could not keep the Spouse to themselves, being 
virtuously jealous of her, jealous of her “with the 
jealousy of God,” and having “espoused (her) to one 
Husband, that they might present (her) as a chaste 
virgin to Christ.” ‘‘ They struck me and wounded 
me,” she complains. Wherefore did they treat her 
thus? Unless I mistake, they were trying to make 
her pass on in order that she might find her Beloved. 
And she adds, “The keepers of the walls took away 
my mantle from me,” doubtless to enable her to run 
more freely. It is worth while noticing with how 
much force the Apostle strikes and with what kind of 


284 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


arrows he wounds those whom he considered to be 
tarrying with the watchmen, when they should be 
passing on. ‘“‘ Was Paul then crucified for you? ” he 
asks, “ or were you baptised in the name of Paul?” 
And again, “ For while one saith: I indeed am of 
Paul ; and another, I am of Apollo; are you not men? 
What then is Apollo, and what is Paul? The ministers 
of Him Whom you have believed.” ‘‘ Because he hoped 
in Me, I will deliver him.” This may be paraphrased 
thus: “Not in the keepers, whether men or angels, 
hath he hoped, but in Me, expecting nothing of good 
even at their hands, except from Me. For he knows that 
“every best gift and every perfect gift is from above, 
coming down from the Father of lights.’ From Me is 
the vigilance which men keep over him in his external 
conduct, because I have entrusted this to the care of 
human keepers. From Me likewise is the watchful guar- 
dianship of the holy angels, who observe the more hidden 
affections of his soul, powerfully excite him to virtue, 
and repel the suggestions of the wicked one. But the 
intimate surveillance over the most secret thoughts and 
intentions of his heart is not only from Me, but must 
also be exercised by Me in person, because no created eye, 
human or angelic, can penetrate to this sanctuary.” * 

* “In two ways can the thought of the heart be cognised. 
It can be cognised in its effect, and that not only by the angels 
but even by men. Yet the more interior is this effect, the 
greater is the penetration required for perceiving it. Some- 
times the thought may be discovered from external actions, 
sometimes also from alterations in the body. And physicians 
can even discern certain affections of the soul by examining 
the pulse. Much more, then, can the angels and the demons 
read our thoughts in this way, in proportion as their keener 
intellectual acumen takes cognisance of more subtle bodily 
changes. . . . Otherwise the thoughts can be perceived directly 


as they exist in the intellect, and the affections (intentions)

My brethren, let us acknowledge these three orders 
of keepers ; and with respect to each, let us discharge 
the duties which are incumbent upon us. Let us 
imitate the Apostle by “‘ providing good things not only 
in the sight of God but also in the sight of all men,” 


‘and in the sight of the angels. Let us endeavour to 


please all in all, but especially Him Who is supreme

spirits may be fulfilled what is written, “ They that

that I have not much cause for anxiety with regard to 
your obedience, all thanks to God to Whose grace it 
is entirely due. For what is my joy and my glory if 
it be not your ready obedience, and your irreproach- 
able lives? And if I could have the certain assurance 
that the angels also can discover nothing unworthy in 
you; that nothing of the anathema of Jericho* is 


the thoughts of the heart or the affections (intentions) of the 
will. The reason is: because the will of a rational creature is 
subject to God alone, and He alone can work on it, as being its 
principal Object and ultimate End. And hence those things 
which depend upon or exist in the will alone can be known 
(directly) only to Him. Now it is manifest that it depends 
on the will whether the intellect actually considers such or 
such objects, because when one has the habit of science, or the

concealed in any of you; that none of you is guilty of 
murmuring or of secret detraction; that none of you 
is insincere or remiss in religious observance ; that none 
of you entertains in his mind illicit images, which, alas! 
are wont to produce evil motions even in the body: 
had I full certainty on all these points, my joy and 
my glory would no doubt be greatly increased ; although 
they would not as yet be perfect. For we are not so 
great as to be able to esteem it “a small thing ” that 
we cannot be judged “ by man’s day,” much less are 
we so great as to count it “a small thing” that we 
“are not conscious to ourselves of anything.” But 
if even the truly great dread the secret Judge, how 
much more does it become us to think of His 
scrutiny with fear and trembling? Oh, if it were 
only given me to know for certain that there is 
nothing in any one of us displeasing to the eyes 
of Him Who alone has perfect knowledge of what 
is in man, and Who sees in him even that which he 
does not perceive himself! Dearest brethren, let us 
reflect with all possible diligence on that divine judg- 
ment. Let us fearfully, tremblingly, consider it in our 
meditations, and the more so, the less we are able to 
penetrate the unfathomable abyss of God’s judgments, 
or His irrevocable decrees. Accompanied with this 
fear, our hope shall have merit: when we are thus 
solicitous, our expectation shall not be in vain. 
Moreover, if you consider the matter carefully, you 
shall find that this very fear is itself the most solid 
and secure ground of our hope. For this fear is one 
of the greatest of God’s gifts. Now the bestowal of 
present benefits confirms our expectation of favours 
still in store. Besides we are assured by the Psalmist 


FIFTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 287 


that “the Lord taketh pleasure in them that fear 
Him.” But if there is “life in His good will,” as the 
same Prophet tells us surely in His “ pleasure” is ever- 
lasting salvation. ‘‘ Because he hoped in Me, I will 
deliver him.” With what sweetness and generosity 
does God here promise never to fail them that put 
their trust in Him! For this is the whole merit of 
man, the placing of all his hope in Him Who can save 
him, body and soul. ‘In Thee,” sings the Psalmist, 
“have our fathers hoped ; they have hoped, and Thou 
hast delivered them. They cried to Thee, and they 
were saved ; they trusted in Thee, and were not con- 
founded.” Who ever “hath hoped in the Lord and 
hath been confounded’? Therefore, “trust in Him, 
all ye congregation of people.” For “every place 
that your foot shall tread upon, shall be yours.” Your 
foot is your hope.* Consequently, hope has a promise 
to obtain whatever it shall extend itself to, if yet it 
is entirely grounded on the divine goodness, so that it 
may be firm and stagger not. Why should it fear the 
asp or the basilisk? Why should it dread the lion’s 
roar or the hiss of the dragon ? 

“Because he hoped in Me, I will deliver him.” 
“ Besides, after delivering him, ‘I will protect him’ 
and watch over him, lest he should be attacked again, 
and again be in need of deliverance ; provided, how- 
ever, that ‘he hath known My name’ ; provided only 
he hath not attributed his deliverance to his own 
virtue, but hath given glory to My name.” “I will 
protect him because he hath known My name.” The 
unveiling of His Countenance shall be our glorification. 
But meantime we must seek for our salvation in the

knowledge of His name. His name gives us hope, 
His presence shall give us, not hope, but happiness. 
“ For what aman seeth, why doth he hope for? ” So 
speaks St. Paul, who tells us elsewhere that “faith 
cometh by hearing.” And in another place he defines 
faith as “the substance of things to be hoped for.” * 
“I will protect him, because he hath known My 
name.” But fe hath not known the name of the Lord 
whosoever takes it in vain, whosoever says to Him, 
“ Lord, Lord,” and doth not the things which He says. 
And he hath not known His name who neither honours 
Him as a Father nor fears Him as a Lord. He also 
hath not known His name who attends to “ vanities 
and lying follies.” 

‘“ Blessed is the man whose trust is in the name of 
the Lord,” says the Royal Prophet, “and who hath 
not regard to vanities and lying follies.” But the 
name of the Lord was known to him who said, “ For 
there is no other name under heaven given to men, 
whereby we must be saved.” And we, my brethren, 
if we have indeed known that holy name which is 
“invoked upon us,” we must desire to see it always 
hallowed in us; and we must pray for this, saying, 
according to the formula prescribed by the Lord, 
“Our Father, Who art in heaven, hallowed be Thy 
name.” Listen to what follows in the psalm I am 
expounding, “ He hath cried to Me and I will hear 
him.” This, my brethren, is the fruit of the knowledge 
of the Lord’s name, the strong cry of fervent prayer. 
And further, the fruit of the cry is the hearing of the

Saviour. For how can anyone be heard by the Lord if

SIXTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC. 


ON HOLY DESIRES, AND ON THE THREE GREAT BENEFITS 
WE MUST PRAY AND HOPE FOR. 


“ He hath cried to Me, and I will hear him: I am with him in 
tribulation. I will deliver him, and I will glorify him.” 


“ He hath cried to Me, and I will hear him.” Here 
manifestly we have a “ testament of peace,” a pledge 
of piety, a compact of mercy and compassion. “‘ He 
hoped in Me,” saith the Lord, “and I will deliver him ; 
he hath known My name and I will protect him ; he 
hath cried to Me, and I will hear him.” He does not 
say, “he is deserving, he is just, he is righteous, he 
is ‘innocent in hands and clean of heart,’ therefore 
will I deliver him, and protect him, and hear him.”’ 
Had He used this or similar language, who would not 
despair? For who will venture to boast that his heart 
is perfectly pure? But now “ with Thee there is mer- 
ciful forgiveness, and by reason of (this) Thy law I have 
waited for (sustinui—hoped in Thee), O Lord.” A most 
sweet law this, which places the right to be heard in the 
very cry of petition. “He shall cry to me,” saith the 
Lord, “and I will hear him.” And justly is a hearing 
refused to the soul that neglects to cry, either not 
praying at all, or praying with tepidity and indevotion. 
A loud cry in the ears of God is the ardour of strong 
desire.* On the other hand, a weak desire resembles 
the subdued sound of a whisper. How shall this 


ce 


* “A loud cry in the ears of God is the ardent affection of 
the soul" (Imitation of Christ, Bk. III. ch. 5). 


290 


SIXTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 291r 


penetratetheclouds? Howshallit beheardinheaven ? 
Therefore at the very beginning of the Lord’s Prayer 
it is mentioned that the Father to Whom we are about 
to address ourselves has His home in heaven, in order 
to remind us that we must cry aloud and that our 
petition must be, as it were, shot up to Him by the 
impulsive force of spiritual fervour. For “‘ God is a 
Spirit,” and whosoever wishes that his cry shall come 
to Him, must cry in the spirit. Just as He does not, 
like man, look upon the face of man, but “ regardeth 
the heart ” ; so His ears are open rather to the desire 
of the heart than to the voice of the lips. Hence it 
was that once, when Moses was silent exteriorly, the 
Lord said to him, ‘‘ Why criest thou to Me?” For 
the affection of the Lawgiver’s heart had sounded in 
His ears as a strong cry. Rightly therefore does the 
Royal Prophet call him “the God of my heart.” 

“ He hath cried to Me, and I will hear him.” It is 
not without reason that the faithful one cried thus to 
God. The greatness of his necessity extorted a loud 
cry from him. For what has he sought by his cries 
but consolation, deliverance, and glorification ? This is 
evident from the answer he receives. For why should 
he obtain the promise of these favours had he prayed 
for something different? ‘‘ He hath cried to Me and 
I will hear him.” In what way wilt Thou hear him, 
O Lord? ‘I am with him in tribulation. I will deliver 
him, and I will glorify him.” It seems to me, my 
brethren, that the three benefits promised in this place 
are to be especially referred to the great triduum which 
we shall soon be commemorating.* For Christ, because 
of us, “ found tribulation and sorrow,” when “ having 


* He refers to the three last days of Holy Week.—(Translator.) 


292 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


joy set before Him, He endured the cross, despising the 
shame.” Nevertheless, ‘‘the things concerning (Him) 
had an end,” as He predicted before His passion. And 
therefore at the moment of His death He exclaimed, 
“ It is consummated ” ; and after that He rested awhile. 
But He had not to wait long for the glory of His 
resurrection. Early in the morning of the third day, 
the Divine Sun of justice arose unto us from the 
sepulchre. Thus, therefore, both the fruit of His 
tribulation and the truth of His deliverance were 
made manifest in the visible fact of His glorification. 
But for ourselves also, as I think, a similar triduum can 
be discovered. ‘‘ I am with him in tribulation,” saith 
the Lord. Does not this seem to refer to the day of 
our tribulation, to the day of our crucifixion, when 
that is fulfilled in us which Christ has foretold, saying, 
“ In the world you shall have distress’”’ ; and likewise 
His Apostle, “ All that will live godly in Christ Jesus, 
shall suffer persecution ° ? From this tribulation there 
can be no full and final deliverance before the hour of 
our death, because, in the words of the Wise Man, ‘‘a 
heavy yoke is upon the children of Adam from the 
day of their coming out of their mother’s womb, until 
the day of their burial into the mother of all.” This, 
then, is the day in which the Lord promises to deliver 
us, when the world shall no longer have power to 
injure us, either in body or in soul. As for our glori- 
fication, that is reserved for the last day of all, the 
day of the general resurrection, when that which “‘is 
sown in corruption shall rise in incorruption,’” when 
that which “is sown in dishonour shall rise in glory.” 

But, my brethren, how do we know that the Lord 
is with us in tribulation? By the very fact that we 


SIXTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 293 


ourselves are in tribulation. For which of us could 
face it, which of us could endure it, which of us could 
persevere under it, without His support? Oh, then 
dearest brethren, “ count it all joy when you fall into 
divers temptations,” not only because “through many 
tribulations we must enter into the kingdom of God,” 
but also because “the Lord is nigh unto them that 
are of a contrite heart.” Hence the Psalmist sings, 
“ For though I should walk in the midst of the shadow 
of death, I will fear no evils, for Thou art with me.” 
In this manner, therefore, according to what He has 
promised, He is with us “all days, even to the con- 
summation of the world.” But when will he deliver 
us and take us to Himself? This shall be when “ we 
shall be taken up in the clouds to meet Christ, into 
the air, and so shall be always with the Lord.” When 
shall we appear with Him in glory? ‘‘ When Christ 
shall appear, Who is our Life.” But meanwhile it is 
necessary for us that He should conceal Himself from 
our view, in order that tribulation may precede deliver- 
ance, and deliverance glorification. Listen to the voice 
of one that has been delivered: ‘‘ Turn, O my soul, 
into thy rest: for the Lord hath been bountiful to 
thee. For He hath delivered my soul from death, my 
eyes from tears, my feet from falling.” “ I will deliver 
him, and I will glorify him.” Happy the man who, 
during his earthly pilgrimage, has Thee, O Lord, for 
his Consoler and for his “Helper in due times in 
tribulation ” ! But how much happier he whom Thou 
hast delivered and saved from such terrible evils! How 
much happier he who has been already delivered from 
the “snare of the hunters,” who has been “taken 
away lest wickedness should alter his understanding, 


294 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


or deceit beguile his soul”! But happiest of all and 
beyond compare shall be he whom Thou shalt have 
taken to Thyself, whom Thou shalt have “ filled with 
the good things of Thy house,” and conformed to 
the brightness of Thy glory ! * 

“And now, my little children, “let us cry to 
heaven, and the Lord our God will have mercy on 
us.” “Let us cry to heaven,” I say, because under 
heaven there is nothing but labour, and sorrow, and 
vanity, and vexation of spirit. For “the heart of 
man is perverse above all things, and unsearchable,” 
and his “imagination and thought are prone to evil 
from his youth.” “I know that there dwelleth not in 
me, that is to say, in my flesh, that which is good.” 
I have the “law of sin” in my members, “fighting 
against the law of my mind.” Moreover, “ my heart 
hath forsaken me,” and my “ body is dead because of 
sin.” Truly “ sufficient for the day is the evil thereof,” 
when “ the whole world is seated in wickedness.” How 
wicked is the world now in all its ways! How wickedly 
are worldly desires known to war against the soul ! 
Then there are “the rulers of the world of this dark- 
ness,” “the spirits of wickedness in high places,” the 
aerial powers, and amongst them “the old serpent, 
more subtle than any of the beasts of the earth.” 
These, my brethren, are all the things, which are under 
the sun: they are all that can be found under heaven. 


* The three degrees of blessedness mentioned here correspond 
to the three promises of the text : ‘‘(1) I am with him in tribu- 
lation, (2) I will deliver him, (3) and I will glorify him.” The 
first is enjoyed by every man who lives under the divine pro- 
tection ; the second belongs to them that die in the grace of 
God, and whose salvation is thus secure ; the third is reserved 
for the general resurrection, when the elect shall be glorified 
in body as in soul. See also note at page 323.—(Translator.) 


SIXTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 295 


To which of them all shalt thou run for refuge? 
From which of them all canst thou hope to obtain 
any assistance or consolation? If thou lookest 
within thyself, thou shalt find thy heart withered and 
thyself “ forgotten as one dead from the heart.” If 
‘thou lookest below thee, thou shalt see that “ the cor- 
ruptible body is a load upon the soul.” And shouldst 
thou gaze about thee, thou shalt perceive how “the 
earthly habitation presseth down the mind that museth 
upon many things.” Seek, therefore, above thee. But 
be careful to pass over the aerial powers. For these, 
being robbers, and knowing that “ every best gift and 
every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the 
Father of lights,” lie in ambush between heaven and 
earth. Pass them by, therefore, pass by these wicked 
watchmen, who so tirelessly observe and guard the way, 
lest anyone from earth should enter the city of God. 
Should they strike thee, should they wound thee, as 
happened to the Spouse in the Canticle, leave them 
thy outer garment, as Joseph did of old to the 
temptress ; leave them also thy inner garment, so 
that, like the young man in the Gospel, thou mayest 
flee from them naked. Did not the Lord deliver 
into the hands of the wicked one not only the outer, 
but also the inner garment of holy Job, when He gave 
Satan power over his substance and his flesh, with 
the condition, however, that his life should be saved ? 
' Therefore, my brother, lift up thy heart: lift up thy 
voice as well, and thy desire, and thy hope, and thy 
intention, and thy whole life. Cry to heaven, and 
cry aloud that thou mayest be heard, and that the 
Father Who is in heaven “ may send thee help from 
the sanctuary, and defend thee out of Sion,” according 


296 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


to the prayer of the Psalmist: that He may now 
“sive thee help from trouble,” afterwards deliver 
thee from tribulation, and glorify thee at last in the 
general resurrection. These in truth are great things 
to ask, but Thou, the great Lord, hast promised them. 
Thy promise is the ground and motive of our hope, 
and it is from the same we have the courage to sing, 


“When our prayers from love proceed, 
Thou art promise-bound to heed.”’ * 


* “Si corde clamemus pio 
Certe debes ex promisso.’ 


From the Matins hymn for Lent.—(Translator.)
