SEVENTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC, 


ON THE VALUE OF TRIBULATION AND ON THE REWARD 
OF THE JUST. 


“ I will fill him with length of days ; and I will show him My 
salvation.” 

It is a happy coincidence for us, my brethren, that 
this verse of the psalm applies so well to the present 
season. Very soon we shall be celebrating the resur- 
rection of the Lord, and now his own resurrection is 
promised to each one of us: so that the members may 
more joyously commemorate as accomplished in their 
Head what they look forward to see sometime accom- 
plished in themselves also. Happily, therefore, ends the 
song with the promise of so happy an end to the singer. 
Joyous is its consummation which sings of a consum- 
mation so joyous. “I will fill him with length of days ; 
and I will shew him My salvation.” I have often 
reminded you, dearest brethren, that, according to the 
teaching of St. Paul, “godliness is profitable to all 
things, having promise of the life that now is and 
of that whiċh is to come.” Hence the same Apostle 
writes in another place, “‘ But now being made free 
from sin, and become servants to God, you have your 
fruit unto sanctification, and the end life everlasting.” 
This is the fulness, this is the length of days, promised 
in the verse which I am now discussing. For what is 
so long as eternity? What is so long as that which 
is confined within no limits? Truly a happy end is 
life everlasting, a happy end which knows no ending. 


297 


298 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


And evidently that whereof the end is so good, must 
likewise be good itself. Let us therefore embrace 
sanctification, because it is good, because it leads to 
a life which endureth for ever. Let us “ follow peace 
and holiness ; without which no man shall see God.” 
“ I will fill him,” saith the Lord, “‘ with length of days, 
and I will shew him My salvation.” This is the promise 
of the Right Hand of God: it is the gift of His Right 
Hand, which holy Job prayed or rather predicted of 
old that He would extend to him, saying, “ To the 
work of Thy Hands Thou shalt reach out Thy Right 
Hand.” In this Hand “ are delights even to the end,” 
as the Psalmist tells us. He also desired to have this 
Right Hand reached out to him, and obtained his 
request, the same of whom the Royal Prophet says 
in another place, “ He asked life of Thee: and Thou 
hast given him length of days for ever and ever.” 
But still more clearly spoke the Wise Man, “‘ Length 
of days is in His Right Hand, and in His Left Hand 
riches and glory.” ‘“‘Who is the man that desireth 
life, who loveth to see good days?” For this life 
by which we live upon earth is rather a death than 
a life. It is not a pure life, but a dying life. We 
say that a man is dying when he is manifestly 
approaching death. But what have we been doing 
from the very first moment in which we began to 
live, but approaching death and beginning to die? 
And moreover, the days of our earthly life, such as it 
is, are “few and evil,” according to the testimony of 
the holy Patriarch Jacob. But there we shall truly live 
where life is pure and vivid. There we shall enjoy good 
days, where the length of the days is interminable. 
Thanks to Him Who disposeth all things, not alone 


oS ae ee oF 


SEVENTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 299. 


with power, but also with sweetness: thanks, I say, 
to Him, Whose providence has so arranged that the 
days full of evil should be few and quick in passing, 
whilst the good days in store are destined to endure 
for ever. 

“I will fill him with length of days.” By these 
words, my brethren, the Lord expresses more clearly 
what He has said in the verse preceding, namely, “ I 
will glorify him.” Where is the man that would not 
be content to be glorified by Him Whose works are 
all perfect ? For the Divine Immensity cannot glorify 
anyone otherwise than immensely. That glorification 
must undoubtedly be great which is derived from the 
glory of God, “coming down from the magnificent 
glory,” to borrow the language of St. Peter. And 
rightly is it called a magnificent glory, since it glorifies 
the creature so magnificently, with an eternity, an 
abundance, a plenitude of glory. Here, on earth, 
glory is deceitful and brightness is vain, and the 
days of our life are quickly ended. The man of 
wisdom will not desire such things. He will rather 
say from his heart to Him Who reads the heart, 
“T have not desired the day of man, Thou know- 
est.” But I am not content with not desiring the 
day of man: I desire that it be not given me. For 
I know Who it is That said, “‘I receive not glory 
from men.” Oh, how miserable are you “ who receive 
glory from one another, and the glory which is 
from God alone, you do not seek’’! The only glory 
which we neglect to seek is that which alone is 
lasting unto length of days, which alone has fulness. 
“The days of man are short,” says holy Job. 
And the Psalmist, ‘‘Man’s days are as grass: as 


300 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


the flower of the field, so shall he flourish’’; to 
which the Prophet Isaias adds, “The grass is withered 
and the flower is fallen: but the Word of the Lord 
endureth for ever.” He is the true Day That “ knoweth 
no setting,” the eternal Truth, and the true Eternity, 
and by consequence the true and eternal Fulness. 
How could that glory fill us which is itself empty and 
vain, which is therefore often described as empty, in 
order to indicate that it is more capable of emptying 
our hearts than of filling them? During the present 
life, therefore, it is better for us to be humbled than 
exalted, it is better to experience want than pleasure; 
because, whereas all these things alike shall quickly 
pass away, the momentary enjoyment brings eternal 
pain and the momentary suffering an immortal crown. 

Very profitable, my brethren, is that tribulation 
which “‘ worketh trial ’’ and leads to glory everlasting. 
“I am with him in tribulation,” saith the Lord, “I 
will deliver him, and I will glorify him.” Let us give 
thanks to the ‘“‘ Father of mercies,” Who condescends 
to be with us in our tribulation, “‘ Who comforteth 
us in all our tribulation.” Very profitable, as I have 
said, yea, and very necessary for us ought we to 
esteem the short-lived tribulation which merits ever- 
lasting glory, and the short-lived sadness which shall 
be changed into joy, into unending joy which no 
one can take from us, into joy full and abounding. 
Truly necessary is that necessity which obtains for us 
an unfading crown. Therefore let us not undervalue our 
present tribulation: it is only a small seed, but nothing 
small is the fruit which grows from it. Perhaps it is in- 
sipid, perhaps it is unpalatable, perhaps it is a ‘‘ grain of 
mustard-seed.’’ Whatever it be, let us not regard that 


SEVENTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 301 


which is apparent in it, but only that which lies con- 
cealed. “For the things which are seen are temporal ; 
but the things which are not seen are eternal.” Let 
us enjoy the foretaste of the promised glory ; let us, 
like the Apostle, “ glory in the hope of the glory of 
the sons of God.” Yea, and to speak more plainly 
with the same Apostle, let us “glory also in our tribu- 
-lations,’’ because therein consists our hope of glory. 
Consider if this be not the truth which the Doctor of 
nations intended to teach when he added, “ Tribulation 
worketh patience, and patience trial, and trial hope.” 
These words should make it manifest to you that in 
saying, “ And not only so, but we glory also in our 
tribulations,” he did not mean to assign a new motive 
for glorying, but simply to explain the one already 
given where he declared that he gloried in hope. His 
purpose was not to commend to us a second source of 
glory, but to point out where we ought to look for 
the hope of glory and the glory of hope. For it is 
only in tribulation that we can find the hope of glory: 
I should rather have said that glory itself is contained 
in tribulation, just as the hope of the fruit, yea, just 
as the fruit itself is contained in the seed. In this way 
the kingdom of God is even now within us, as an im- 
mense treasure in “earthy vessels,” or buried in an 
unprofitable field. It is truly there, I say, but as hidden. 
Happy the man that finds it! And who is he ? The 
man who thinks more of the harvest than of the sowing. 
It is the eye of faith that discovers this hidden treasure, 
because it does not judge according to the face, but 
looks to that which appears not, and contemplates the 
things which are not seen. The Apostle reveals to us 
how truly he had discovered this treasure for himself, 


(E 


302 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


and how he desired it should be found by others, when 
he says, ‘‘ That which is at present momentary and 
light of our tribulation worketh for us above measure 
exceedingly an eternal weight of glory.” Observe how 
he doesnot say, “‘ that which is momentary and light of 
our tribulation shall be rewarded with,” no, but, “‘ worketh 
for us an eternal weight of glory.” The glory, my 
brethren, is not manifest ; it is laid up for us in our 
tribulation ; the momentariness of our humiliation has 
hidden in it an eternity of glory ; the lightness of our 
suffering contains a weight of happiness above measure 
exceedingly. In the meantime, therefore, let us make 
haste to purchase that field and to possess ourselves of 
the treasure which is hidden in the field. Let us 
“count it all joy when (we) shall fall into divers 
temptations.” Let us say from our heart, let us say 
with conviction, “ It is better to go to the house of 
mourning than to the house of feasting.” 

“I am with him in tribulation,“ saith the Lord. 
This being so, shall I wish for anything but tribulation 
during my sojourn here on earth? ‘‘It is good for me 
to adhere to my God and (not only so, but likewise) 
to put my hope in the Lord God,” and in Him alone, 
because He has said, “ I will deliver him and I will 
glorify him.” ‘I am with him in tribulation.” Else- 
where He tells us, “ My delights are to be with the 
children of men.” How well He deserves to be called 
“Emmanuel, which, being interpreted, is God with us’’! 
“ Hail, full of grace, the Lord is with thee,” said the 
Archangel to Mary. He is with us now, my brethren, 
in the fulness of grace; He will be with us hereafter in 
the fulness of glory. He came down from heaven that 
He might be “ nigh unto them that are of a contrite 


oe a he eee eens 


SEVENTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 303 


heart,” that He might be with us in our tribulation. 
But the time will come when “we shall be taken up 
in the clouds to meet Christ, into the air, and so shall 
we be always with the Lord ” ; if yet we are in the mean- 
time careful to keep Him with us as the Companion of 
our way, Who alone can bring us safely to our heavenly 
home: or rather, He is Himself our Way now, as He 
shall be our Home hereafter. Lord, it is good for me to 
be in tribulation, provided that I have Thee as my Com- 
panion. Yea, it is better to be afflicted with Thee than 
without Thee to reign, or to feast without Thee, or with- 
out Thee to be glorified. It is better for me, O Lord, 
to embrace Thee in tribulation, better to endure with 
Thee the furnace of affliction, than without Thee to 
enjoy even the bliss of paradise. ‘‘ For what have I 
in heaven, and besides Thee what do I desire upon 
earth? ” ‘‘ The furnace trieth the gold, and the trial 
of affliction just men.” There, Lord Jesus, it is there 
Thou art with us: it is there that Thou standest in the 
midst of those who are gathered together in Thy name ; 
as of old Thou didst deign to appear with the three 
holy youths in the Babylonian furnace, so that even 
the pagan monarch saw Thee and wondering said, “I 
see four men loose and walking in the midst of the fire, 
and there is no hurt in them, and the form of the fourth 
is like the Son of God.” Why should we tremble? 
Why should we hesitate? Why should we shrink from 
this salutary furnace? Fiercely indeed the cruel fire 
rages: but courage! The Lord shall be with us in 
tribulation. ‘‘ If God be for us, who is against us? ” 
And if He wills to deliver us, who shall snatch us out 
of His Hand? Who shall be able to wrest us from 
His grasp, if it is His good pleasure to save us? 


304 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


Finally, if He wills to glorify us, who shall dishonour 
us? If He raises us up, who shall cast us down ? 
Hear now, my brethren, with what manner of glory 
He will glorify us. “I will fill him,” He says, “ with 
length of days.” Observe in the first place that He 
speaks of days in the plural, not to signify succession, 
but to indicate abundance. However, lest you should 
suppose succession to be implied, remember what the 
Psalmist says in another place, speaking to the Lord, 
“Better is (the) one day in Thy courts above 
thousands.” We read in the sacred Scriptures of 
many holy and perfect men, that they migrated full 
of days from these changing days of ours, and we 
take the meaning to be that they died full of grace 
and merit. And to this fulness they attained through 
their having been day by day “ transformed from glory 
to glory (not by their own spirit, but) as by the Spirit 
of the Lord.” If, then, we give the name of day to 
grace and merit; if (as I have remarked before) we 
call “ the day of man,” the splendour of honour which 
comes from man, and that balefully brilliant glory 
which we seek from one another: why should not the 
plenitude of true glory be called the perfect day, or 
rather the full meridian? And if we can speak of the 
“divisions of graces ” as so many mystical days, why 
may we not also represent as a multitude of days the 
varieties of heavenly glory? Listen lastly to the 
Prophet Isaias speaking evidently of such a multitude 
of days as involves no vicissitude or succession: ‘‘ And 
the light of the moon,” he says, “ shall be as the light 
of the sun, and the light of the sun shall be sevenfold, 
as the light of seven days.” Unless I am deceived, 
these are the days which the faithful King Ezechias 


SEVENTEENTH SERMON ON PSALM XC 305 


had in mind when he prayed that he might sing 
psalms all the days of his life in the house of the 
Lord: because by the gratitude which they shall feel 
for every gift of glory, so great and so manifold, and 
by the thanks which they shall render always and in 
all things, the blessed shall sing psalms, as it were, all 
the days of their lives to the name of the Lord. 

“I will fill him with length of days,” saith the Lord. 
These words may be thus paraphrased : “ I know what 
he desires, I know what he thirsts for, I know what 
it is that shall give him satisfaction. Neither gold nor 
silver has any value in his eyes. Pleasures cannot 
content him, or worldly knowledge, or any earthly 
honour. All such things he regards as loss, he despises 
them, and ‘counts them but as dung.’ He has entirely 
emptied himself of them. For he will not allow him- 
self to be occupied by earthly goods, wherewith, as he 
knows, he could never be filled. He does not forget 
to Whose image and likeness he has been made, and 
of Whose glory he is rendered capable. Neither is he 
willing to enjoy such material prosperity as would be 
detrimental to his eternal interests. Therefore ‘I will 
fill him with length of days,’ since nothing but the true 
light can refresh him, nor anything fill him but the 
eternal light. For the length of days which I promise 
him shall have no limit, the glory shall never wane, 
the fulness never weary. The eternity of his happi- 
ness shall make him secure, its truth shall render him 
glorious, and its plenitude jubilant.” “And I will 
shew him My salvation.” It is then he shall deserve 
to see what he has so long desired, when the King of 
glory will “ present to Himself a glorious Church, not 
having spot’ on account of the brightness of the day, 

E U 


306 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


“or wrinkle” owing to the absolute fulness. For 
the splendour of that brightness can no more endure 
disquiet or agitation in any soul than it can tolerate 
uncleanness. Hence it is that even now we are com- 
manded to “ follow peace and holiness, without which 
no man shall see God.” When, therefore the Lord 
shall have “‘ satisfied thy desire with good things,” so 
completely that thou shalt want for nothing more, thy 
soul being fully satiated and tranquillised by this 
plenitude, then shalt thou be able to gaze upon the 
tranquillity and the plenitude of the Divine Majesty, 
and shalt be made like to God because thou shalt see 
Him as He is. Or perhaps the words, “And I will 
shew him My salvation,” ought to be understood in 
this sense: The beatified one, already full of all glory 
in himself and dwelling in a world of delights, shall 


also contemplate outside himself the salvation wrought © 


by God, and the whole universe filled with His Majesty.* 

Or again, we may, if we prefer, adopt the following 
exposition: In what He says, “I will shew him My 
salvation,” the Lord is only explaining what manner of 
days He has promised. ‘I will fill him with length of 
days,” He has said. Now someone might ask, how 
can there be any days in that supernal city, of which 
we read that no sun shall shine in it, and no night shall 
enshroud it? But we also read that “the glory of God 
hath enlighteneth it and the Lamb is the Lamp thereof.”’ 
Here, then, is the answer : “ I will shew him My salva- 
tion, so that, as the Evangelist says, the Lamb shall

SERMONS FOR CHRISTMAS EVE. 


FIRST SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS EVE. 


ON THE THREE BENEFITS WHICH WE OWE TO THE 
BIRTH OF CHRIST. 


« Jesus Christ, the Son of God, is born in Bethlehem of Juda.’’* 


The voice of joy has been heard in our land, “ the 
voice of rejoicing and salvation in the tabernacles ”’ not 
“ of the just,” however, but of sinners. We have heard 
the “good word” the “consoling word,” the word 
full of sweetness and “worthy of all acceptation.” 
“Ye mountains, give praise with jubilation,” and let 
“all the trees of the forest clap their hands ” “ before 
the face of the Lord because He cometh.” ‘‘ Hear, O 
yè heavens, and give ear, O earth.” Let every creature, 
but especially thou, O man, be astonished and give 
praise to the Lord, because “ Jesus Christ, the Son of 
God, is born in Bethlehem of Juda.” Oh, what heart 
can be so flinty, what soul so insensible, as not to melt 
with love at hearing this announcement? What 
sweeter tidings could be published? What happier 
event could be proclaimed? Has the world ever 
before heard anything like this, or has news like this 
been ever told? “ Jesus Christ, the Son of God, is 
born in Bethlehem of Juda.” O short word of the 
“shortened Word,” but full of heavenly sweetness ! 
Truly love is here in labour, eager to pour out more 

* These words are taken from the announcement of Christmas 
in the Cistercian Martyrology.—(Translator.) 

309 


310 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


plentifully the abundance of its mellifluous tenderness, 
and unable to find more adequate expression. For 
so perfect is the beauty and charm of this short verse 
that you cannot change a single iota without spoiling 
it. “ Jesus Christ, the Son of God, is born in Bethlehem 
of Juda.” O nativity spotless in sanctity, glory of 
the world, dear to man by reason of the greatness of 
the benefit it confers, inscrutable even to the angels 
because of the depth of its divine mystery, admirable 
to all in its novelty and unparalleled excellence! 
Nativity, which, to borrow the words of the Poet: 


“ Had no precursor in the years before, 
And peerless shall remain for evermore.” * 


O birth of Christ, alone unaccompanied by pain, alone 
pure and immaculate, not violating but consecrating 
the Mother’s integrity! O nativity, beyond nature, yet 
designed for the good of nature ; transcending nature | 
by thy miraculous excellence, restoring nature by the 
power of thy mystery! My brethren, “‘ who shall declare 
(this) generation?’’ The Angel announces it, the 
. power of the Most High overshadows it, the Holy 
Ghost descends upon it. A virgin believes, a virgin 
conceives by her faith, she brings forth as a virgin, 
and remains ever a virgin. Who can help wondering 
at all this? The Son of the Most High is born: God 
of God, begotten before the ages, is born to-day as a 
speechless Babe! Who can now wonder sufficiently ? 
And this nativity has not been in vain, not in vain 
has been the condescension of the Divine Majesty. 
* « Nec primam similem visa est, nec habere sequentem. ”— 


The Irish poet Sedulius, Carmen Paschale, iii. 39, who is here 
speaking of the Blessed Virgin. —(Translator.) 


FIRST SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS EVE 311 


“ Jesus Christ, the Son of God, is born in Bethlehem 
of Juda.” “ Awake and give praise, ye that dwell 
in the dust.” Behold the Lord cometh with salvation, 
He cometh with ointments, He cometh with glory. 
For Jesus cometh not without salvation, nor Christ 
without unction, nor without glory the Son of God, 
Who is Himself Salvation and Unction, Who is Him- 
self Glory, according to what is written, “ A wise son 
is the glory of his father.” Happy the soul, which 
after tasting the fruit of His salvation, runs to the 
odour of His ointments, in order that she may see 
His glory, “ the glory as it were of the Only-Begotten 
of the Father.” Take courage all ye who have wandered 
away, because Jesus comes to seek and to save that 
which was lost. Ye that are sick be of good cheer, 
for Christ has come to anoint the contrite of heart 
with the unction of His mercy. Exult and be glad, 
ye who are ambitious of glory, since the Son of God 
has descended to you to make you co-heirs of His 
kingdom. Thus, O Lord, thus, I pray Thee, “ heal 
me and I shall be healed; save me and I shall be 
saved,” glorify me and I shall be glorious. Then shall 
I say with the Psalmist, “ Bless the Lord, O my soul, 
and let all that is within me bless His holy name. 
Bless the Lord, O my soul, and never forget all He 
hath done for thee, Who forgiveth all thy iniquities, 
Who healeth all thy infirmities, Who satisfieth thy desire 
with good things.” As for me, my dearest brethren, 
I experience from these three things a threefold savour 
of sweetness in the announcement that “ Jesus Christ, 
the Son of God, is born.” For why do we call His 
name Jesus unless because “‘ He shall save His people 
from their sins?” And why has He willed to be 


312 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


called Christ, unless because He “shall make the yoke 
to putrify at the presence of the oil?” And wherefore 
has the Son of God made Himself the Son of man if 
it be not-to make men the sons of God? Who shall 
oppose His will? It is Jesus “That justifieth, who 
is he that shall condemn?” It is Christ That healeth, 
who is he that shall hurt? It is the Son of God That 
raiseth us up, who is he that shall cast us down ? 

Jesus is born: oh, rejoice all ye whomsoever the con- 
sciousness of sin foredooms to everlasting damnation ! 
For the mercy of Jesus is greater than any degree of 
malice, greater than any number of crimes. Christ 
is born: let gladness arise in every soul which is 
struggling with long-established habits of evil. For 
no spiritual malady, no matter how inveterate, shall be 
able to stand before the face of the unction of Christ. 
The Son of God is born: exult thou who art wont to 
aspire to greatness, because the Giver of great rewards is 
at hand. My brethren, “this is the Heir,” let us lovingly 
receive Him, “ that the inheritance may be ours.” For 
He Who hath given us His own Son, ‘‘ how hath He 
not also with Him given us all things? ” Let no one 
disbelieve it, let no one doubt it, for we are assured 
by a most trustworthy witness that “ the Word was 
made flesh and dwelt amongst us.” The only-begotten 
Son of God willed to share His sonship with us, “ that 
He might be the First-Born among many brethren.” 
And in order that weak and pusillanimous mortals 
might be left no excuse for doubting this, He first made 
Himself the Brother of man, and the Son of man, by 
truly taking upon Him our human nature. 

“ Jesus Christ, the Son of God, is born in Bethlehem 
of Juda.” Notice the humility exhibited in the choice 


ee A a a a a 


FIRST SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS EVE 313. 


of place. Not in Jerusalem, the city of the kings, is 
He born, but in Bethlehem, which is but “ a little one 
among the thousands of Juda.” O Bethlehem, here- 
tofore little indeed, but now magnified by the Lord! 
Thou hast been made great by Him Who in thee has 
made Himself little from great. Exult, O Bethlehem, 
and through all thy streets let joyous alleluias be sung 
to-day! What city that ever hears of it will not 
envy thee that most venerable stable and the glory 
of that poor manger! Thy name is now famous 
throughout the whole world, and “all generations 
shall call (thee) blessed.” Everywhere, ‘glorious 
things are said of thee, O city of God.” Everywhere 
resounds the song of joy: ‘“‘A Man is born in her and 
the Same hath founded her, the Most High.”* Every- 
where, I say, it is preached and proclaimed that “ Jesus 
Christ, the Son of God, is born in Bethlehem of Juda.” 
And not without purpose is the addition of the words, 
“of Juda,” by which we are reminded of the promise 
made to the fathers by the mouth of the Patriarch

Jacob, “ The sceptre shall not be taken away from 
Juda nor a ruler from his thigh till He come That is 
to be sent, and He shall be the expectation of nations.” 
For though “ salvation is of the Jews,” yet it reaches 
“to the farthest parts of the earth.” And the same 
Patriarch also prophesied, “Juda, thee shall thy 
brethren praise; thy hands shall be upon the necks 
of thy enemies.” These and other predictions which 
are nowhere recorded to have been verified of the 
typical Juda, we now see fulfilled in Christ. For He 
is ‘‘ the Lion of the tribe of Juda,” of Whom it is 
added, “ Juda is a lion’s whelp : to the prey, my Son, 
Thou art gone up.” A mighty Spoiler is Christ, Who 
“ before He knew to call His Father and Mother carried 
off the spoils of Samaria.” Christ is indeed a mighty 
Spoiler, Who “‘ ascending on high, led captivity captive.” 
He did not, however, take away anything from us, 
but rather “ gave gifts to men.” In this way, therefore, 
these and similar prophecies, which we perceive to be 
fulfilled in Christ, (of Whom also they were spoken), 
are recalled to our minds when we hear the announce- 
ment, “‘ Jesus Christ, the Son of God, is born in 
Bethlehem of Juda.” And there is no longer any 
necessity for us to inquire whether anything of good 
can come from Bethlehem. 

As to what concerns us, my brethren, we learn how 
He desires to be received from the fact that He chose 
Bethlehem for His birthplace. Perhaps there were then 
some who thought that a magnificent palace should 
be made ready in which the King of glory might be 
honourably entertained. But it was not for earthly 
honour that the “almighty Word leapt down from 
heaven from (His) royal throne,” where “length of 


= 


ae eS E E E EAEN 


FIRST SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS EVE 315 


days is in (His) Right Hand, and in (His) Left Hand 
riches and glory.” He possessed from everlasting an 
inexhaustible store of all such things in heaven. One 
treasure, however, He could not find there, namely, 
the treasure of poverty, of which there was on earth 


‘an abundance and a superabundance, although man 


had no suspicion of its worth. It was, therefore, for 
the sake of this that the Son of God came down from 
His throne on high, in order to choose it for Himself 
and by His appreciation to teach us its value. Adorn, 
then, O Sion, adorn thy bridal couch: but let it be 
with the ornaments of poverty and humility. These 
are the swathing-clothes which please Him best; 
these, as Mary bears witness, are the silks wherewith 
He delights to be clothed. Let us, therefore, “‘ sacrifice 
the abominations of the Egyptians to the Lord our 
God.” 

Consider lastly, my brethren, that it is in Bethlehem 
of Juda Jesus is born, and study how each one of you 
may make himself a Bethlehem of Juda, for thus He 
will not disdain to be born in you also. Now Bethlehem 
means “the house of bread,” and Juda signifies 
“ praisè” or “ confession.” Consequently, if thou 
fillest thy soul with the food of holy doctrine ; if also 
thou faithfully and with all possible devotion—un- 
worthy though it must ever be—receive that Living 
Bread ‘‘ Which cometh down from heaven and giveth 
life to the world ” (I mean the Body of the Lord Jesus) 
so that His glorified Flesh may repair and strengthen 
the “old bottle” of thy body, which thus fortified 
shall be able to hold the new wine poured into it ; 
if, finally, thou livest by faith and art never obliged 
to avow with tears that thou hast “ forgotten to eat 


316 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


thy bread ” : thou art now become a Bethlehem, and 
comparatively fit to receive the Lord, if yet confession 
be not wanting. Then let “ Judæa be thy sanctifica- 
tion,” “ put on praise and beauty,” the robes wherewith 
Christ would have His ministers clothed. The Apostle, 
lastly, commends these two things very briefly where 
he says, “ With the heart we believe unto justice and 
with the mouth confession is made unto salvation.” 
For justice in the heart is as bread in the house. That 
justice may be called bread is clear from the words of 
Christ, ‘‘ Blessed are they that hunger and thirst after 
justice, for they shall have their fill.” Let justice, 
therefore, be in thy heart, the justice which is of faith, 
for this alone “hath glory before God.” In thy 
mouth also have the confession which “is made unto 
salvation: ’’ so shalt thou be able to receive with 
confidence Him Who is born in Bethlehem of Juda, 
Jesus Christ, the Son of God.
