FOURTH SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS DAY 409 


through pride. Or perhaps the reason why he made 
special mention of what belongs to humility is this: 
in order to teach us that it is by humility more than 
by any other virtue we are to honour the Majesty of 
God. But humility is never found alone, since God 
always “ giveth grace to the humble.” Therefore the 
shepherds found “‘ Mary and Joseph, and the Infant 
lying in the manger.” For as the infancy of the Saviour 
manifestly designates the virtue of humility, in the 
same way is continence represented by the Virgin, and 
Justice by Joseph, whom the Evangelist calls “a just 
man,” and “‘ whose praise is in the Gospel.” Who does 
not know that purity is a perfection which belongs to 
one’s own flesh ? Justice, on the contrary, has regard 
to one’s neighbour, being the virtue which inclines us 
to give every man his due. And humility has relation 
to God, because it reconciles us to Him, makes us 
subject to His law, and renders us pleasing in His 
sight. Hence the holy Virgin said, “ He hath regarded 
the humility of His handmaid.”’ Consequently he who 
violates continence, sins against his own body ; he who 
violates justice, sins against his neighbour ; and he who 
by pride and self-exaltation violates humility, sins 
against God. The first degrades himself, the second 
oppresses his neighbour ; the third dishonours God, so 
far as it depends on him. The Lord says, “I will not 
give My glory to another.” But the proud man replies, 
“ Although Thou refusest to give me Thy glory, I will 
nevertheless take it.” For pride is not pleased with 
the division made by the angels, when they sang, 
“Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace to 
men of good will.” The proud man, therefore, does 
not worship God, but is rather lifted up against Him 


410 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


with manifest impiety and infidelity. What indeed is 
piety but the worship of God? And how can anyone 
really worship God unless he voluntarily subjects him- 
self to Him, so as to be able to say with the Psalmist, 
“ As the eyes of the handmaiden are on the hands of 
her mistress, so are the eyes of my heart on the Lord, 
our God ” ? 

Therefore, my brethren, let us “live soberly, and 
justly, and godlily in this world,” if we desire to find 
always in ourselves ‘‘ Mary and Joseph, and the Infant 
lying in the manger.” For it was unto this that “ the 
grace of God our Saviour hath appeared, instructing 
us’’; and if we are faithful in the practice of these 
virtues, the glory of God also shall be revealed to us. 
We have the testimony of St. Paul for this. “The 
grace of God our Saviour,” he says, “hath appeared 
to all men, instructing us that, denying ungodliness 
and worldly desires, we should live soberly, justly, and 
godlily in this world, looking for the blessed hope and 
coming of the glory of the great God and our Saviour 
Jesus Christ.” Grace hath appeared in the Little One, 
but this Little One “shall be great,” as the Angel 
Gabriel predicted of Him. And those whom the Little 
One shall have instructed unto humility and meekness 
of heart, shall hereafter be made great and glorious at 
the coming of the great and glorious God, Our Lord 
Jesus Christ, Who is over all things and blessed for 
evermore. Amen. 


FIFTH SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS DAY. 


ON MAN’S TWOFOLD MISERY AND THE TWOFOLD MERCY 
OF GOD, AND ON THE CONSOLATION WHICH THE 
POOR FIND IN THE NATIVITY. 


“‘ Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord jesus Christ, the 
Father of mercies and the God of all comfort, Who comforteth 
us in all our tribulation.’’—1 Cor. i. 3-4. 


Blessed be the Father “for His exceeding charity 
wherewith He loved us,” and sent us His own Beloved 
Son, in Whom He is well-pleased, to reconcile us by 
His death that we might “ have peace with God,” and 
to become Himself not only the Mediator but also the 
Pledge of our reconciliation. With so loving a Mediator, 
my brethren, we have no need to be disquieted ; and with 
so faithful a Pledge, there is no cause for diffidence. 
But you will say, “ What manner of Mediator is He 
Who is born in a stable, is laid in a manger, is wrapped 
in swaddling clothes like other infants, weeps like other 
infants, and lies weak and helpless like other infants ? ” 
Nevertheless, He is in truth a most powerful Mediator, 
and is seeking the things that are to our peace, not 
negligently (as we do ourselves), but zealously and 
efficaciously, even in this state of seeming impo- 
tence. He is indeed an Infant, but the infant Word 
of God, Whose very infancy is divinely eloquent. 
“ Be comforted, be comforted, My people, saith your 
God.” This is what Emmanuel, God with us, is 
saying to us now. This is what the stable is crying 
out to us, and the manger, and the Infant’s tears, and 


4II 


412 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


the clothes that cover Him. The stable announces 
that a shelter is provided for the poor man who fell 
among robbers. The manger reminds us that food is 
being ministered to the same man who “ is compared 
to senseless beasts and is become like them.” The 
tears and swaddling clothes proclaim that his raw 
wounds are being washed and bound up. For Christ 
has no need of such things. He wants none of these 
for Himself, but all are for His chosen. “They will 
reverence My Son,” said the Father of mercies. They 
will, indeed, O Lord, and they do reverence Him. But 
who? Not the Jews to whom He was sent, but the 
elect for whose sake He was sent. 

For we reverence Him in the manger, we reverence 
Him on the cross, we reverence Him in the sepulchre. 
Lovingly do we embrace our God, become a tender 
Child for us; lovingly do we honour Him, all covered 
with blood for our sakes, pale with the pallor of death 
for our sakes, laid in the tomb for our sakes. Most 
devoutly do we adore our Infant Saviour with the 
Magi; most affectionately do we clasp Him to our 
breast, with holy Simeon, ‘‘ receiving Thy mercy, O 
God, in the midst of Thy Temple.” For He is the 
Same of Whom we read, “ The Mercy of God is from 
eternity.” Surely there can be nothing co-eternal with 
the Father save the Son and the Holy Ghost. And Each 
of these Two is not so much merciful, as Mercy Itself. 
But the Father Himself is also Mercy. And these 
Three are not three mercies, but one Mercy, just as 
They are one Essence, one Wisdom, one Divinity, one 
Majesty. Nevertheless, since Godis called the ‘‘ Father 
of mercies,” who does not see that the name of Mercy 
specially and by appropriation designates the Son ? 


FIFTH SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS DAY 413 


And justly is He called the “Father of mercies,” “to 
Whom it is natural to show mercy always and to 
spare.” 

But some one may object to this, and say, “ How 
is it natural to Him ‘to show mercy always,’ since 
His ‘judgments are a great deep,’ according to the 
Psalmist ? Neither is it said that ‘all His ways are 
mercy, but that ‘all His ways are mercy and truth,’ 
that is, mercy and justice. And the same Prophet 
implies that He is not less just than merciful, in the 
words, ‘ Mercy and judgment I will sing to Thee, O 
Lord.’”’ I grant all this, my brethren. “He hath 
mercy on whom He will, and whom He will He hard- 
eneth.” Still I say it is natural for Him to show mercy, 
for He finds in His own Divine Nature the motive and 
impulse to this. But if He also judges and condemns, 
that is because we compel Him, as it were, to do so. 
And thus it is clear that mercy proceeds from His 
Heart in a manner very different from punishment. 
But listen to what He says Himself by the Prophet 
Ezechiel, ‘‘ Is it My will that a sinner should die, saith 
the Lord God, and not that He should be converted 
from his ways and live? ”?” Rightly, therefore, is He 
called, not the Father of judgments, or of vengeance, 
but the “ Father of mercies ” ; not only because mercy 
appears to belong more to a father than indignation, 
and because “‘as a father hath compassion on his 
children, so hath the Lord compassion on them that 
fear Him” ; but also and especially because (as I 
have said) He discovers in Himself alone the motive 
and impulse to show mercy, whereas when He exer- 
cises judgment or vengeance the cause is in us. 

But it may be further asked, wherefore then is He 


s 


414 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


called the “‘ Father of mercies, ' rather than the Father of 
mercy? “God hath spoken once,” says the Psalmist, 
“ these two things have I heard, that power belongeth 
to God, and mercy to Thee, O Lord.” But the Apostle 
represents to us this same mercy as manifoldin the one 
Word, in the one Son, when he calls God “ the Father,” 
not of one mercy, but “ of mercies,” and “the God ” 
not of some, but “‘ of all comfort, Who comforteth us 
in all our tribulation.” ‘‘ The mercies of the Lord are 
many,” as the Prophet Jeremias in his turn bears 
witness; and they need to be many, since “ many 
(also) are the afflictions of the just, and out of them 
all will the Lord deliver them.” One is the Son of 
God, and the Word is but one; whilst our manifold 
misery demands not only great mercy, but even “a 
multitude of tender mercies.” Yet perhaps on account 
of the two substances which are united in man, namely, 
the flesh and the spirit, and in each of which he is 
miserable, we may speak not improperly of human 
misery as twofold ; although in reality man is subject 
to a multitude of miseries, both in body and in soul. 
Many in truth are the tribulations we suffer in our 
material and in our spiritual part, but He Who “‘ made 
the whole man sound on the Sabbath day,” delivers 
us from all our necessities. Since, therefore, the one 
and Only-Begotten Son of the Father has already come 
for the sake of our souls, viz., to “‘ take away the sins 
of the world,” and is to come again for the sake of 
our bodies, to raise them up and to make them “ like 
to the Body of His glory,” it ought not to seem un- 
reasonable if I say that this twofold mercy is acknow- 
ledged when God is called the “ Father of mercies.” For 
by taking to Himself the two elements of our human 


FIFTH SERMON FOR CHRISTMAS DAY 415 


nature, the soul, namely, and the body, He, the Lord 
our God, seems to say to us, ‘‘ Be comforted, My people,” 
not once only, but with reiteration, as before through 
His Prophet, “ Be comforted, be comforted, My people.” 
Thus are we given assurance that He, Who did not 
disdain to assume both our flesh and our spirit, has 
also the will to save both. 

But whom do you think He will thus save? His 
own people, doubtless. For even now, at His first 
coming, “ He will save—not everyone, but—His people 
from their sins.” And, in like manner, it is not every 
body, but only the “ body of our lowness ” that shall 
hereafter be “made like to the Body of His glory.” 
It is therefore His own people He comforts, that is, 
“the humble people” whom He will save, as the 
Psalmist testifies, when He will “ bring down the eyes 
of the proud.”’ Shall I tell you more clearly who are 
His people? < To Thee is the poor man left,” says 
the man according to God’s own heart. And Christ 
Himself says in the Gospel, “ Woe to you that are 
rich, for you have your consolation.” Would that we, 
my dearest brethren, were always desirous to be found 
amongst that happy people, who, so far from being 
threatened with woes, are rather comforted by the 
Lord their God! Why, indeed, should He comfort 
those who have their own consolation ? No, the speech- 
less Babe in the manger offers no consolation to the 
garruolus ; the tears of the Infant Christ do not comfort 
the lovers of mirth ; His poor swaddling clothes suggest 
nothing consoling to them that “ wish to walk in long 
robes ” ; the crib and the stable have no comforting 
lesson for such as “love the first seats in the syna- 
gogues.” But perhaps they will declare themselves 


416 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


satisfied to let all this consolation go to those who 
“ wait with silence for the salvation of God,” to those 
who mourn, and to those who are clad in the livery of 
the poor. Let them know, therefore, that the angels 
also will comfort none others. For it is to the 
“ shepherds watching and keeping the night-watches 
over their flock ” that the joy of the new light is 
announced, and for them the Saviour is said to be born. 
Upon the poor and the toiling—not upon you that are 
rich and “ have your consolation,’ and with it the 
divinely-denounced woe—the “ day of sanctification has 
dawned ” amid the watches of the night, so that the 
“ night (is) illuminated as the day,” or is rather changed 
into day, since the Angel says, not “ this night,” but 
“this day is born to you a Saviour.” For “the night 
is passed and the Day is at hand,” the true Day of true 
Day, the Salvation of God, our Lord Jesus Christ, Who 
is over all things and is Himself true God, blessed for 
evermore. Amen. 


SERMON FOR THE FEAST OF THE 
HOLY INNOCENTS. 


ON THE THREE KINDS OF SANCTITY OR MARTYR- 
DOM, REPRESENTED BY ST. STEPHEN, ST. JOHN 
EVANGELIST, AND THE HOLY INNOCENTS. 


“ Out of the mouth of infants and sucklings Thou hast perfected 
praise.”-—Ps. viii. 3. 


“Blessed is He That cometh in the name of the 
Lord. The Lord is God, and hath shined upon us.” 
So speaks the Psalmist. And the Prophet Daniel says, 
“Blessed is the name of His glory, which is holy.” 
It was not in vain, my brethren, that the Holy One 
was born of Mary, seeing that thereby both the name 
and the grace of His sanctity have been so widely 
diffused. For it is by participation in His holiness 
that Stephen and John and the Innocents are holy. 
Therefore it has been wisely ordained that the solemni- 
ties in honour of these saints should immediately follow 
that of the Lord’s nativity ; because not only does such 
a succession of feasts keep alive our devotion, but it 
also makes more clearly manifest to us the fruit of 
Christ’s coming, by representing these three festivals as 
a continuation, so to speak, of Christmas. You may 
have noticed that in the same three solemnities we cele- 
brate three distinct kinds of sanctity. Nor will it be 
easy to discover any human sanctity which does not 
belong to one of the three. For in St. Stephen we have 

L 417 2D 


418 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


both the will and the act of martyrdom ; in St. John 
Evangelist we have the will without the act; whilst 
in the Holy Innocents we have the act without the 
will. All of them drank the chalice of salvation, either 
in act and will together, or in will alone, or in act alone. 
“ My chalice indeed you shall drink,” said the Lord to 
James and John, speaking no doubt of the chalice of 
His passion. And when He said to Peter, “ Follow 
Me,” inviting him, as is evident, to imitate Himself by 
martyrdom, “‘ Peter, turning about, saw that disciple 
whom Jesus loved following,” not so much with his 
bodily feet as with the feet of his fervent devotion. 
Therefore John also drank the chalice of suffering, and 
followed his Lord, like Peter, although not like Peter 
in every way. For that he so remained as not to follow 
his Master even by a visible martyrdom was due to 
the divine will, ‘‘So will I have him to remain,” 
answered Jesus to Peter, “ till I come. What is it 
to thee? ” As if He should say, “ He indeed desires 
to follow Me, but it is My will that he shall remain.” 

Who has any doubt that the Holy Innocents have 
received the martyr’s crown ? He who does not admit 
this, who does not admit that the crown of martyrdom 
has been the reward of the infants slain for Christ, 
ought also to disbelieve that infants regenerated in 
Christ are numbered amongst the children of adoption. 


For how could that Little One Who was born not — 


against us but for us, have suffered so many other little 
ones of His own time to be slaughtered on His account, 
when with the will He might have prevented it, unless 
He had prepared for them something more precious than 
the life they were robbed of? We may then suppose 
that the martyrdom they endured for Christ’s sake was 


FOR THE FEAST OF THE HOLY INNOCENTS 419 


sufficient to sanctify them, just as circumcision sufficed 
for the salvation of other infants at that time,* and as 
baptism suffices now, without any voluntary co-opera- 
tion. And if you ask what was their merit before God 
that they should be crowned, inquire also what was 
their crime against Herod that they should be slain. 
Shall the mercy and love of Christ, think you, be out- 
done by Herod’s wickedness, so that He shall not have 
power to crown the infants done to death for His 
sake, whilst the tyrant is permitted to massacre the 
innocent ? Let us say, therefore, that Stephen is a 
martyr to the eyes of men, the voluntariness of whose 
suffering was shown by this especially, that at the very 
moment of death he manifested more solicitude for his 
persecutors than for himself, as if the interior affection 
of compassion overcame in him the sense of bodily 
pain ; for he grieved more for their crimes than for his 


* Regarding the efficacy of circumcision there are three 
opinions. Some theologians, including St. Augustine, St. 
Bernard (De Baptismo, i.), St. Bonaventure, Alexander of Hales, 
and Suarez, hold that it conferred grace, like the Christian 
sacraments, that is, “ ex opere operato” or by an infallible 
decree of God. In support of this view may be cited the words 
of Pope Innocent III, who says that baptism has succeeded 
circumcision. Others, after St. Thomas, such as Bellarmin, and 
Vasquez, deny any such infallible efficacy to circumcision, and 
appeal to the authority of St. Paul and of the Councils of 
Florence and Trent. According to these circumcision was a 
purely external rite, instituted not to confer grace, but to 
distinguish the Jew from the Gentile. The third opinion is 
that of Cardinal de Lugo. This author distinguishes two 
aspects in circumcision. So far as it was a rite of the Mosaic 
law it did not communicate grace; but it did confer grace 
in so far as it contained and applied the “ remedy of nature,” 
and was an external profession of faith in the Redeemer to come; 
just as the blood of the Paschal lamb saved the Jews from 
the destroying angel, not because it was the blood of a lamb 
but because it typified the Blood of Christ. Cf. St. Thomas, 
Sum. Theol., III. q. lxii. a. 6, also lect. ii. in Ep. ad Rom. c. 4 ; 
and de Lugo, Disp. 5, s. 3, n. 40.—(Translator.) 


420 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


own wounds. Let us say that John was a martyr to the 
eyes of the angels, who, as being spiritual creatures, could 
discern more surely the interior evidences of his ardent 
desire. But the Innocents were truly and in a special 
sense Thy martyrs, O Lord, because the power and ex- 
cellence of Thy grace Thou hast clearly manifested in 
them, in whom neither men. nor angels could discover 
any merit. Thus, as the Psalmist predicted, “‘ out of 
the mouth of infants and sucklings Thou hast perfected 
praise.” “Glory to God in the highest,” sang the 
angels, “and on earth peace to men of good will.” 
That, my brethren, was great praise, indeed: but I 
make bold to affirm, it was as yet imperfect, until the 
coming of Him Who should say, “Suffer the little chil- 
dren and forbid them not to come to Me, for the kingdom 
of heaven is for such,” and until there should be peace 
for men in the sacrament of mercy even without the 
co-operation of their own will. Let those who are fond 
of disputing so contentiously about the intention and 
the act weigh these things well. Let them observe 
and consider that neither must be neglected even when 
both are not in our power ; especially because, where 
we cannot have the two, either without the other may 
suffice, not only for salvation, but even for sanctifi- 
cation. But let them be firmly persuaded of this 
also, that although the act without the intention may 
be enough for salvation, the act against the intention 
profiteth nothing ; so that the very same thing—the 
mere external act—which for the infant merits a 
crown, becomes for the hypocrite a cause of damnation. 
Furthermore, the intention is sometimes sufficient 
without the act, yet not against the act- To give 
you an example. A person dies with a will that is 


FOR THE FEAST OF THE HOLY INNOCENTS 421 


good indeed, but not perfect, not adequate to the 
enduring of martyrdom. Will anyone dare to deny him 
salvation because of this imperfection? Perhaps the 
very reason why God did not allow him to sustain so 
grievous a trial was lest he should sink under it, and so be 
lost. For were he subjected to an ordeal too trying 
for his imperfect will, and received at the same time 
no special succour of grace, can there be any doubt 
that he would yield, that he would deny the faith 
and, dying in his apostasy, would perish for ever ? 
“He that shall deny Me before men,” says the Lord, 
“I will also deny Him before my Father Who is in 
heaven.” Therefore, that imperfect will, which now is 
sufficient to save him whilst there is no opportunity 
for the act, would then be of no avail, because of the 
defect of the act when the occasion has been provided, 
or rather I should say, because of the act of defection. 
The same thing might happen through ignorance.* 
Let us, therefore, my brethren, have zeal for charity, 
and apply ourselves diligently to the practice of good 
works, never making light of any sins, even sins of 
- ignorance and frailty. Let us rather, with all fear and 
solicitude, return thanks to our most kind and generous 
Redeemer, Who seeks out occasions for saving human 
souls with such abounding love that He is delighted 


* That is to say, when one is invincibly ignorant of what 
God requires, the good will to obey Him is taken for the deed. 
Thus an honest heretic does not sin by remaining outside the 
true Church ; he may even merit by doing so, or sin by acting 
otherwise, because there is an obligation to follow the con- 
science, even should it be invincibly erroneous. Nevertheless 
when it is a question of sacramental rites and their “‘ ex opere 
operato ”? effects, the intention avails nothing without the 
external action. A sacrament invalid by reason of some defect 
in matter or form can never be validated by the recipient’s 
good will.—(Translator.) 


422 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


not only when in some He discovers both the will and 
the act, but also when He finds in others either the 
will without the act or the act without the will. For 
He “ will have all men to be saved and to come to the 
knowledge of the truth.” And “this is eternal life 
that (we) may know Thee, (O Father) the only true 
God, and Jesus Christ Whom Thou hast sent,” and 
Who is also with Thee the one true God, over all things, 
and blessed for evermore Amen. 


SERMONS FOR THE FEAST OF 
CIRCUMCISION. 


FIRST SERMON FOR THE FEAST OF 
CIRCUMCISION. 


ON THE DIFFERENT KINDS OF CIRCUMCISION ; ON THE 
NAME OF SALVATION AND THE THREE WITNESSES. 


“And after eight days were accomplished that the Child should 
be circumcised, His name was called Jesus.’’—Luke ii. 22. 


t 


You have heard, my brethren, a “ great mystery of 
godliness ” announced in a few words. You have 
heard a passage of Scripture corresponding in its brevity 
to the “abridgment ” which, according to Isaias, the 
Lord hath made of His own Word on the earth. For 
the Word of God, already “ abridged ” by the incar- 
nation, is still more ‘‘ abridged ” to-day by the rite of 
circumcision. The Only-Begotten of the Father was 
““ made a little less than the angels ” when He assumed 
our humanity, but now surely He has become, not a 
little, but a great deal less than the angels, by submitting 
to the remedy of our human corruption. We have 
here, consequently, a powerful support for our faith, 
and at the same time an illustrious example of humility. 
What need hast Thou to be circumcised, O Lord, Who 
art free from all defilement of sin, both personal and 
inherited ? That Thou hast committed no sin Thyself 
is evident from Thy infancy ; that Thou hast not con- 
tracted the stain of original sin is more evident still 
from the Divinity of Thy Father and from the integrity 


423 


424 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


of Thy Mother. Thou art that High-Priest of Whom 
it is predicted in the Law *—for it is a prophecy rather 
than a command—that He should contract defilement 
neither from Father nor Mother. For Thou hast a 
Father from all eternity, but a Father Who is God 
and consequently immune from sin; and Thou hast 
been born in time of a Mother, but of a Mother who 
preserved her virginity, and being thus incorrupt herself, 
could transmit no corruption to her Offspring. Yet, 
notwithstanding His sinlessness, the Virgin’s Child—the 
Lamb without spot—is circumcised as other infants. 
He needed it not, yet willed to endure it. Although 
not having so much as the trace of a wound, He does 
not decline the remedy. ‘‘ Not so the wicked, not so.” 
It is not in this way the perversity of human pride 
acts. We are ashamed to have our wounds bound up, 
yea, and sometimes even glory in our wounds. We are 
ashamed to do penance, yet have no shame of doing 
deeds of shame. Can any madness be greater than 
this? The Saviour Himself, Whom no man could 
“convince of sin,” submits without necessity to a 
remedy for sin as painful as it is ignominious; He 
refuses not the “ knife of stone ’’ wherewith alone the 
original corruption could be scraped away ; He “ Who 
did no sin”’ does not disdain to be reputed a sinner. 
And we, although willing enough to be sinners in fact, 
cannot bear to be so esteemed! Is it then the healthy 
that are in need of medicine, and not they that are ill ? 
I should rather have said, is it for the physician a remedy 
is sought and not for his patient ? Who amongst men 
would willingly submit to be circumcised, if conscious 
—I will not say of such glory, but—of such innocence 


* Leviticus xxi. II. 


FIRST SERMON FOR THE CIRCUMCISION 425 | 


as belonged to Christ ? But He contentedly “ paid what 
He took not away,” as the Psalmist sings, though He 
came, not to endure the purgation but, as the Apostle 
says, to “ make a purgation of sins.” However, it may 
be asked, why should not He Who is only a Little One 
endure this purgation just as other little ones? But 
it were more pertinent to ask why should not He endure 
it Who is so “meek and humble of heart” ?* Or 
why should not He Who was “ dumb as a lamb before 
His shearers,” Who was silent before His executioners, 
submit with patience to His circumciser? For to pre- 
serve His own Flesh from the knife of circumcision 
could not have been hard to Him Who was able to 
issue forth from His Mother’s womb without injury to 
her integrity. This Little One Who with all ease 
guarded His Body after death from corruption, could 
not have found it difficult to protect It living from the 
wound of circumcision. 

“ After eight days were accomplished that the Child 
should be circumcised, His name was called Jesus.” 
How great and marvellous a mystery! The Child is 
circumcised and His name is called Jesus. Wherefore 
are these two things united ? For circumcision appears 
to belong rather to those who need salvation than to 
Jesus, that is, to the Saviour ; and it seems more proper 
for the Saviour to circumcise others than to be circum- 
cised Himself. But, my brethren, recognise in this 
Infant the ‘‘ Mediator of God and men,” Who from the 
very beginning of His created existence associates things 
divine with things human, the lowest with the highest, 


* The Saint means that Christ submitted to the painful 
rite of circumcision, not because He was powerless to resist— 
for though a Little One, He still was God—but because He 
was “ meek and humble of heart.’’—(Translator.) 


426 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


He is born of a woman; but of a woman who is pri- 
vileged to bear the fruit of motherhood without losing 
the flower of virginity. He is wrapped in swaddling 
clothes; but these swaddling clothes are celebrated 
with angelic songs. He is concealed in a manger ; but 
heaven sends down a star to manifest His presence. 
Thus also His circumcision proves the reality of His 
Human Nature, whilst the name “which is above all 
names ” reveals the glory of His Majesty. He is cir- 
cumcised as being a true son of Abraham ; He is called 
Jesus as being the true Son of God. For in this Jesus 
of ours the name is not an empty title, as it was in His 
forbears. He has not the shadow but the truth of a 
great name. The Evangelist distinctly tells us that He 
received this name from heaven, for he says that it 
“ was called by the Angel before He was conceived in 
the womb.” Mark what a depth of meaning there is 
here. After the birth of Jesus He was called Jesus by 
men, by which name He had already been called by 
the Angel before He was conceived in the womb. How- 
ever, we ought not to be surprised at this, because 
Jesus is the Saviour of both angels and men. He is 
the Saviour of the angels from the moment of their 
creation ; He is the Saviour of men from the moment 
of His incarnation. 

“ His name was called Jesus, which was called by 
the angel before He was conceived in the womb,” “ that 
in the mouth of two or three witnesses every word 
may stand”; and the same Word announced by the 
Prophet as about to be “abridged,” is more clearly 
stated in the Gospel to have been made flesh. This, 
my brethren, is a parable, and has reference to our- 
selves. For Christ has no need of testimony from either 


FIRST SERMON FOR THE CIRCUMCISION 427. 


angels or men, but, as the Apostle says, “all things 
(are) for the sake of the elect.” We are instructed, 
therefore, to seek a threefold witness of our own sal- 
vation, lest perhaps we should seem to have taken the 
name of our God in vain. For we also, brethren, must 
endure to be circumcised, in order to receive the name 
of salvation.* We must be circumcised, I say, yet not 
according to the letter but “in spirit and in truth,” 
not in one part only, but in the whole body ; because 
the concupiscence which comes from the original trans- 
gression and has to be cut away, is not confined to any 
single member, but reigns throughout the flesh. “ From 
the sole of the foot unto the top of the head there is 
no soundness ” inus. No part of our body has escaped 
the infection of this poison. Consequently, although 
the chosen people, whilst it was as yet but little in 
faith and love, had enjoined on it a partial circumcision 
suited to its weakness; now, when it has increased unto 
the fulness of perfect manhood, it is commanded to 
be baptised in its whole body, which may be regarded 
as a circumcision of the entire man. Hence it is that 
the Saviour vouchsafed to undergo the Jewish circum- 
cision on the eighth day (when He was still only a 
Little One) and after His thirtieth year allowed Him- 
self to be crucified, by which every part of His Body 
was racked with pain. And according to the Apostle, 


* The meaning is, that as the name of salvation (Jesus) was 
given to the Child in the presence of three witnesses, the Angel, 
Mary, and Joseph, and given on earth only after His circum- 
cision : so too before we can presume to take to ourselves this 
name, that is, before we can consider ourselves to be of the 
saved, we must undergo a moral circumcision, and have testi- 
mony in our favour from three witnesses, from our brethren, 
from our superiors, and from Christ Himself, the Angel of the 
great Counsel, speaking in the conscience.—(Translator.) 


428 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


“ we have been planted together in the likeness of His 
death,” by our obedience to the law imposing the new 
circumcision. 

What else, therefore, is our moral circumcision except 
that which the Apostle recommends to us where he 
says, ‘‘ Having food and wherewith to be covered, with 
these we are content ’’? Assuredly we are well cir- 
cumcised by our voluntary poverty, which cuts away 
everything superfluous, by our penitential labours, by 
our observance of regular discipline. But, as I have 
said, in this moral circumcision, we have to seek a 
threefold testimony of our salvation, from the Angel, 
from Mary, and from Joseph. It is necessary first and 
before all that the “ Angel of the great counsel ” should 
impose on us the name of salvation. We have need, 
secondly, of the testimony of the community in which 
we live, and which is, as it were, the mother of each 
of us, but a virgin mother, like unto her whom St. 
Paul “‘ espoused to one Husband ” and presented “ as 
a chaste virgin to Christ.” Nor, in the third place, 
must he who desires to follow the Saviour’s example, 
neglect the testimony of His minister. For the latter 
represents Joseph, and holds the place of the Bride- 
groom, yet only as His servant and steward ;. Joseph 
is called the father of Christ, but is really nothing 
more than His guardian. 

I will endeavour to put this more clearly. It is 
necessary for us, my brethren, to have good testimony, 
both from those who are without and from Him Who 
is within. Now, the religious whose conversation is 
agreeable to all and offensive to none has undoubtedly 


the testimony of the entire brotherhood in favour of 


his salvation. So far as regards external conduct, it 


FIRST SERMON FOR THE CIRCUMCISION 429 


will be useless for the wicked “ accuser of the brethren ” 
to cite him to judgment whose innocence is attested 
by the whole community. From his superiors also he 
has good testimony, whosoever, having a sincere deter- 
mination to follow their directions, faithfully exposes 
for their judgment in a candid and humble confession 
the sins he has been guilty of whilst he lived in the 
world, as well as the faults committed in the cloister. 
“ For in many things we all offend,” unless we consider 
ourselves holier than the disciple whom Jesus loved, 
Concerning sins thus manifested there is no longer any 
cause for uneasiness, because the Lord will not judge 
again what has already been pronounced upon. But 
perhaps the accuser will attempt to impute evil to our 
intentions, and endeavour to calumniate us in a matter 
as to which the testimony alike of our spiritual father 
and of our brethren shall appear of little avail. For 
this, therefore, we have need of an internal Witness, 
Who looks rather to the heart than to the face. And 
evidently we must begin with this internal Witness, 
so that we shall conceive nothing in our souls until it 
has first received from Him the name of salvation. But 
when that which has been conceived in the mind issues 
forth into external action, it is necessary that it should 
also obtain good testimony for itself from those who 
are without. “For we provide what may be good,” 
says the Apostle, “ not only before God but also before 
men.” 


SECOND SERMON FOR THE FEAST OF 
CIRCUMCISION. 


ON THE MANNER IN WHICH THE NAME OF JESUS HAS 
BEEN POURED OUT IN THE SAVIOUR’S OTHER TITLES. 


« And after eight days were accomplished that the Child should 
be circumcised, His name was called Jesus, which was called 
by the Angel before He was conceived in the womb.” — 
Luke ii. 21. 

“ And His name shall be called Wonderful, Counsellor, God the 
Mighty, the Father of the world to come, the Prince of peace. 
His Empire shall be multiplied and there shall be no end of 
peace.’’—Is. ix. 6-7. 


From the beginning, my brethren, God has shown 
His predilection for order, and nothing out of order 
has ever been acceptable to Him Whose very Essence 
is order. Hence it is that not only has He, as Creator, 
“ ordered all things in measure and number and weight,” 
but He also prescribed an order to be observed by man, 
and gave him a precept, saying to Adam, “‘ Of every 
tree of paradise thou shalt eat ; but of the tree of know- 
ledge of good and evil thou shalt not eat.” A most 
easy commandment surely, and a most ample liberty. 
Nevertheless, man refused to observe the order thus 
established, but transgressed the limits appointed him. 
Therefore God turned away His Face from him; and 
only in the days of His friend Abraham did He begin 
to grow propitious again. At that time He instituted 
another order, and promulgated another law, but a law 
and an order altogether unlike the preceding. For 
whereas the first law was preventive, the second was 
remedial; by the first man was forbidden to admit 


430 


SECOND SERMON FOR THE CIRCUMCISION 431 


anything superfluous, the second commanded him to 
cut off and remove from him the superfluities already 
admitted ; the first concerned the tree of knowledge 
and forbade its fruit to be eaten, the second related to 
the human body, the flesh whereof it ordered to be 
circumcised. 

And although this circumcision was confined to one 
part of the flesh, it cannot be doubted that the Levi- 
athan of concupiscence had spread its poison through- 
out the whole body, and had infected the entire man 
with an inordinate and insatiable passion for pleasure. 
Nevertheless, because our human fragility, and in 
particular the tender delicacy of a new-born babe, 
could not endure a universal circumcision, the Lord in 
His loving-kindness was content with a partial. The 
eighth day after birth was appointed for the 
ceremony. This fact of itself held out a hope of the 
heavenly kingdom, inasmuch as the returning circle 
of the week seemed to represent a crown. It is for 
this reason that we celebrate with special solemnity 
the octave day after each of the principal festivals. 
And in the Lord’s sermon on the mount, the eighth 
beatitude, as you may have noticed, is united to the 
first, so that we have the same obvious suggestion of 
a crown in the repeated promise of the kingdom of 
heaven. 

Very properly, therefore, did the Child Who was 
born for us receive the name of Jesus, that is, Saviour, 
at the time of His circumcision ; because it was then 
He began the work of our salvation, by pouring out 
the first instalment of His immaculate Blood. Chris- 
tians surely have no need to be told the reason why 
our Lord Jesus Christ willed to be circumcised. He 


432 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


was circumcised for the same reason for which He was 
born and for which He died. None of these things was 
for His own sake, but “all for the sake of the elect.” 
He was not conceived in sin, He was not circumcised 
because of sin, neither did He suffer death for sins of 
His own, because He had none, but for our sins. “‘ His 
name was called Jesus, which was called by the Angel 
before He was conceived in the womb.” It was called, 
but not given. For Jesus has been His name from 
everlasting. To be a Saviour is a property of His 
Nature : He owes this title to His own Divine Essence, 
not to any creature, angelic or human. 

But, my brethren, how shall we explain the fact 
that the illustrious Prophet who predicted the various 
names that would belong to this Child, appears to have 
passed over just that one by which alone He was called, 
as the Angel foretold and as the Evangelist bears 
witness? Isaias rejoiced “ that he might see this day ; 
he saw it and was glad.” He saw it, I say, because 
full of gratitude and praising God, he cried out, “A 
Child is born to us, and a Son is given to us, and the 
government is upon His shoulder. And His name shall 
be called Wonderful, Counsellor, God the Mighty, the 
Father of the world to come, the Prince of peace.” 
Great names, no doubt, are all these. But where is 
that name which is above every name, at which “ every 
knee shall bow’’? Where is the name of Jesus? 
Perhaps we shall find it distributed in a manner, and 
poured out in these others. For it is of this name— 
Jesus—that the Spouse says in the Canticle of love, 
“Thy name is as oil poured out.’’* 


* The subject is discussed at greater length in Sermon XV 
the Canticle of Canticles.—(Translator.) e H 


SECOND SERMON FOR THE CIRCUMCISION 433 


Yes, my brethren, so it is. We have the name Jesus 
contained in a state of solution, so to speak, amongst 
all the other titles mentioned by the Prophet.* And 
if He lacked any one of these He could neither be, 
nor be called, a Saviour, that is, Jesus. But have we 
not each of us found Him truly admirable in the 
transformation He has accomplished in our wills ? 
For that was the beginning of our salvation, when we 
began to hate what we used to love, to lament what 
we used to delight in, to embrace what we used to fear, 
to pursue what we used to fly from, and to desire what 
we used to loathe. Admirable surely He must be Who 
has wrought such wonders in us. But it is necessary 
that He should likewise show Himself our Counsellor, 
so as to direct us in the practice of penance and in 
the ordering of our lives, lest otherwise our zeal should 
be without knowledge, and our good will without 
discretion. He must, in the next place, manifest Him- 
self as our God, by pardoning the sins of the past, 
because otherwise He cannot be our Saviour; and no 
one can forgive sin save God alone. Yet not even this 
will suffice for our salvation, unless He also proves 
Himself mighty in “ overthrowing them that fight 
against us.” For if the same evil passions prevail 
over us again, our “last state” shall undoubtedly “be 
worse that our first.” Does He now need anything 
more in order that He may be our Saviour? Yes, 
my brethren. That is still wanting to Him which is 
the most important of all, namely, to be the ‘ Father 

* For a fuller treatment of this subject and for further evi- 
dence of the Saint’s devotion to the name of Jesus, cf. Sermons 
on the Canticle of Canticles, vol. i. pp. 136-7. See also the 


translation of the beautiful and famous Hymn to the Holy 
Name at the end of this volume.—(Translator.) 


I. 2E 


434 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


of the world to come,” so that He may beget unto life 
everlasting those who have been begotten unto death 
by their fathers in this world. But to merit the name 
of Jesus, there is one thing more necessary : as ‘‘ Prince 
of Peace” He must reconcile us to the Father to Whom 
He is at last to deliver up the kingdom, as the Apostle 
declares. Then in truth “ His empire shall be multi- 
plied,’’ so that the very multitude of the redeemed 
shall entitle Him to the name of Saviour ; “and there 
shall be no end of peace,” so that the permanence of 
their peace shall be to the saved an evidence of the 
truth of their salvation. 


SERMON FOR THE OCTAVE OF THE 
: FEAST OF CIRCUMCISION. 


ON THE EIGHT MYSTICAL DAYS OF THE SOUL’S 
PROGRESS. 


“And after eight days were accomplished that the Child should 
be circumcised, His name was called Jesus, which was called 
by the angel before He was conceived in the womb,’’—Luke 
UZ 


In the circumcision of the Lord, my brethren, there 
is something that claims our love, something that excites 
our wonder, and something too that calls for our imi- 
tation. Manifest in that mystery is the unspeakable 
grace of the divine condescension ; and for this we give 
thanks; but there is also something concealed in’ it 
which we are expected to fulfil in ourselves. For the 
Lord Jesus came not only to redeem us by shedding 
His most precious Blood, but also to instruct us by 
word and example. As it would be of little use for 
us to know the way home, if we were kept chained in 
prison, so likewise redemption from bondage would 
have availed us nothing, were we left in ignorance of 
the. way, and liable to be led back to captivity by the 
first person who chanced to meet us. At a riper age 
the Saviour will give us manifest examples of patience, 
humility, and of the various other virtues, but, above 
all, of charity ; the same virtues are now exhibited in 
His infancy, although veiled as yet under figures and 
types. 

But before entering upon a discussion of these, it 

435 


436 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


will be well to say something on the great and visible 
condescension whereby the Lord of glory became man 
and suffered for us. The angels have a glory pure and 
perfect, but neither are we ourselves left altogether 
without glory. For even now “we see His glory, 
the glory, as it were, of the Only-Begotten of the 
Father,’ the glory of mercy and of an affection 
truly paternal, the glory of Him Who, proceeding 
from the Bosom of the Father, reveals to us 
what is indeed a fatherly Heart. “For all have 
sinned,” as the Apostle says, “ and do need the glory 
of God.” And in another place he exclaims, “ God 
forbid that I should glory save in the cross of our 
Lord Jesus Christ.” For what could be a greater glory 
to us than the fact that God has esteemed us so highly 
as to endure the cross for our salvation? Or what 
greater glory can He have Himself than the glory of 
this condescension, of this loving-kindness, which is 
all the more sweet because so entirely gratuitous ? For 
He “ died for the ungodly,” as St. Paul bears witness. 
Consider, then, my brethren, how much He has suffered, 
and for whom He has suffered. Consider for whom He 
has suffered that you may not become proud; and 
how much He has suffered that you may not despair. 
And in order that we may be found to “‘ have received, 
not the spirit of this world, but the Spirit That is of 
God, that we may know the things that are given us 
from God,” let us “ not become like the horse and the 
mule, who have no understanding,” but rather like 
beasts in the pious sense, after the example of him who 
says, “ I am become as a beast before Thee, and I am 
always with Thee.” For beasts of this kind know 
their “ Owner ” and their ‘‘ Master’s crib,” in which He 


FOR THE OCTAVE OF THE CIRCUMCISION 437 


Himself, Who is the Bread of angels, is offered them 
for their food as sweetest hay. He is the Living Bread. 
on Which man ought to live. But since man is ‘‘ com- 
pared to senseless beasts and made like to them,” the 
Bread has become Hay, so that even thus he might 
live thereon. 

The mystery of this change is celebrated on the day 
of the nativity, because it was then the Word was 
made flesh, and we know that “ all flesh is grass.” On 
that day, consequently, “ He was made a little less 
than the angels ” and was “in habit found as a man.” 
But to-day I hear of something still more marvellous. 
I hear now that He has been made,not a little, but a 
great deal less than the angels, by assuming not only 
the form of a man, but even the form of a sinner, and 
by allowing Himself to be branded as a criminal. For 
what is circumcision save an indication of superfluity 
and sin? But, O Lord Jesus, what can be superfluous 
in Thee and needing to be cut away? Art not Thou 
true God of God the Father, and true Man, born im- 
maculate of a Virgin Mother? And you who circum- 
cise Him, what are you about? Do you suppose that 
this Child falls under the law commanding that “ the 
male who shall not be circumcised, that soul shall be 
destroyed out of his people’’? Can the Father forget 
the Son of His own Bosom? Or shall He be unable 
to recognise Him unless He bears the mark of circum- 
cision? But surely, if there could be anything that 
might conceivably prevent Him from recognising the 
Son in Whom He is always well pleased, it would be 
the fact of finding in Him this sign of circumcision, 
which He has prescribed for transgressors as a remedy 
for sin. Nevertheless it is nothing wonderful that the 


438 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


Head receives for the members the medicine which It 
does not need for Itself. Even with regard to our own 
bodies, does it not sometimes happen that when the 
ailing part is too tender the medicament is applied to 
another? Thus, the head is sick, and the poultice is put 
on the arm; the reins are distempered, and it is the 
leg that is doctored. In the same manner here, the 
remedy for the corruption of the whole mystical body 
is applied to the Head. 

And what wonder that the Lord should condescend 
to be circumcised for us for whom He refused not to 
die ? He has given Himself to me wholly and is wholly 
expended for my profit. For I was a captive. And 
hearing that the Son of the Great King was passing 
before my prison-house, I raised my voice in groans 
and lamentations, piteously calling out, “ Son of God, 
have mercy on me.” He, in accordance with His 
native benignity, stopped and inquired the meaning of 
the sobbing and weeping which he heard. His com- 
panions made answer, saying to Him, “ This is the 
traitor Adam whom Thy Father has ordered to be kept 
in close confinement until He decides by what torments 
he is to be put to death.” What now will the Prince 
do, Whose nature is goodness, and “to Whom it is 
proper to have pity always and to spare” ? He de- 
scends into my dungeon, He comes down in order to 
lead forth the poor thrall from the place of his cap- 
tivity. But the Jews, ever mindful of the hatred 
wherewith they hate His Father, take this opportunity 
to vent it on the Son. Hence He says of them Himself, 
“They have hated both Me and My Father.” What 
then will these wicked men do to Him, the mere sight 
of Whom they cannot bear? “ This is the Heir,” they 


FOR THE OCTAVE OF THE CIRCUMCISION 439 


whisper to each other, “let us kill Him and we shall 
have His inheritance.” Accordingly they slew the Lamb 
of God, to their own destruction, but to our salvation. 
For they indeed shed the Blood of the Lamb, yet used 
It not for their own profit, whilst we hastened forward 
and drank It. We took the “chalice of salvation,” that 
“ chalice which inebriateth, how goodly it is!” This 
is the cause of our thanksgiving. Not many days ago 
‘we celebrated the descent of the Son of God into the 
prison of this world, I refer to the feast of the nativity ; 
and to-day is kept holy in honour of the mystery of 
humiliation whereby He took upon Himself our fetters 
and chains. For on this day He Who “ did no sin ” 
put His own Hands in manacles in order to deliver the 
guilty : the Lawgiver Himself was “ made under the 
law.” 

But I have next to explain to you how in this cir- 
cumcision there is mystically indicated something which 
we must do ourselves, and what that something is. 
For not without reason was it prescribed in the law 
that the ceremony of circumcision should be performed 
on the eighth day, and not without reason was this law 
fulfilled in the incarnate Word. But “ who hath known 
the mind of the Lord? Or who hath been His coun- 
sellor? ” May the Holy Spirit Who “searcheth the 
deep things of God,” invoked by your prayers, be pro- 
pitious to our desires, and vouchsafe to reveal to us 
the mystery of this eighth day. We are not unaware 
that “‘ unless a man be born again he cannot see the 
kingdom of God,” since it was in order to render this 
new birth possible for us that the Only-Begotten of 
the Father willed to be born a second time. For we 
have all been born in sin, and consequently require to 


440 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


be re-born in grace. This gift of divine grace we re- 
ceived at our baptism, but alas! we lost all its benefit 
during our life in the world. Now for the first time, 
through the mercy of God, the power of grace is operat- 
ing in us in such a manner as to enable us to “ walk 
in newness of life.” Therefore I may say that then a 
man is spiritually born, when the Divine Sun of Justice, 
rising in his soul, illuminates the darkness of his sins, 
manifests to his interior eye the terrors of God’s judg- 
ment, and, to establish him securely in holy fear, 
reveals to him the shortness of the present life and 
the uncertainty of its ending. This is manifestly the 
“ evening ” in which according to the Psalmist, “ weep- 
ing shall have place.” But it must be followed by the 
gladness of that morning whereof the same Prophet 
says to God, “ Cause me to hear Thy mercy in the 
morning.” Thus “there is evening and morning one 
day.” Now this is the day of justice, when to everyone 
is attributed that which belongs to him ; mercy to God, 
and misery to man. It is the day in which, as I have 
said, the child of grace is brought forth spiritually, 
whilst in the manner and by the means just mentioned 
our hearts are moved to a love of penance and a hatred 
of sin. 

But we are in danger of relapsing if we desire per- 
chance to do penance amidst the distractions of the 
world, where some will endeavour to entice us to sin 
by evil counsels, others by worse examples ; some by 
their flattery will tempt us to vainglory, and others 
by their slanders will provoke us to impatience. It is 
necessary, therefore, that the light of prudence should 
shine upon us. By the illumination of this virtue, we 
shall perceive how many and how perilous are the 


FOR THE OCTAVE OF THE CIRCUMCISION 441 


opportunities and occasions of evil which the world 
provides, particularly in the present generation; and 
how weak are our souls for resisting the same, above 
all, if we have been living hitherto in habits of sin. 
Accordingly, my brethren, we should determine in this 
day of prudence to flee away out of the world and its 
wickedness, saying with the Psalmist, “ I have hated 
the assembly of the malignant, and with the wicked 
I will not sit.” But not even now are we entirely 
secure from self-deception. For perhaps we shall feel 
inclined to choose a desert solitude, not weighing suffi- 
ciently our own infirmity and the risk of engaging the 
devil in single combat. For what can be so full of 
peril as to attempt to fight alone against the craft of 
the ancient enemy, to whom we are visible, whereas 
he is invisible to us? Hence we have need of another 
day, the day of fortitude, in the light of which 
we shall perceive that it is «unto God our fortitude 
must be guarded” and that it must be sought in 
the company of many who are fighting side by side. 
For there we shall have as many helpers as comrades, 
who can say with St. Paul, “ We are not ignorant 
of his—namely, Satan’s—devices.””’ A community like 
this is in its fortitude “terrible as an army set in 
battle array.” But “ woe to him that is alone, for 
when he falleth he hath none to lift him up.” We 
read, it is true, of some of the ancient fathers, how 
they received the call and the grace to live in 
solitude. Nevertheless, no one ought to expose him- 
self rashly to the many dangers incident to this 
manner of life, no one ought to tempt God. Hence 
our master, St. Benedict, describes the anchorites as 
men, “who, not in the first fervour of the religious 


442 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


life, but after long probation in the monastery, have 
learned by the help and experience of others to fight 
against the devil.” * Consequently, in this day of 
fortitude we may add to what we said in the preceding 
day of prudence, namely, “ I have hated the assembly 
of the malignant, and with the wicked I will not sit,” 
these other words which follow in the same psalm, 
“I will wash my hands among the innocent.” 

But when one elects to live in the society of many 
brethren, surely he who has not yet been a disciple 
does not wish to be at once made a master, and to 
teach what he has never learned. For being untrained 
in the ways of the spirit, how should he be able to 
curb the irrational movements of his own mind or of 
the minds of others? As the Apostle says, “ No man 
ever hateth his own flesh.” If then such a one becomes 
his own master, how can you doubt that he will some- 
times indulge himself too easily, and that the more 
because he is able to do it unobserved? The day of 
temperance, therefore, must dawn for him, so that he 
may study how to bridle and restrain the irregular 
motions of carnal concupiscence, the purposeless motions 
of unreasonable curiosity, and the rebellious motions of 
stiff-necked pride. He must, like David, “‘ choose to be 
an abject in the house of the Lord,” and to be subject 
to a master, by whom his will shall be broken and his 
wantonness checked by the curb of obedience. Then 
shall he be able to say to God with the same Prophet, 
“ Thou hast placed men over our heads.” Nor ought 
he to consider as unworthy of him such submission of 
which the Lord has given us an example in His own 


* Holy Rule, chap. i. Cf. Sermon LXIV on the Canticle of 
Canticles. 


FOR THE OCTAVE OF THE CIRCUMCISION 443 


Person, because “‘ the disciple is not above his Master.” 

For when Jesus had advanced “in wisdom and age 
and grace with God and man,” being now twelve years 

old, He remained behind in Jerusalem; but having 

been found by the holy Virgin and His reputed father, 

St. Joseph, “in the midst of the doctors, hearing 

them and asking them questions,” “ He went down 

with them and came to Nazareth, and was subject to 

them.’’ Therefore, do thou also, my brother, be subject 

to thy superiors for His sake. 

But perhaps even now, when thou art in the way of 
obedience, thou shalt meet with trials that are painful 
and hard to endure. Thou shalt sometimes perhaps 
have commands laid upon thee, which, although they are 
good and salutary, may nevertheless seem harsh. Now 
if thou beginnest to submit to these with an ill-humour, 
to criticise thy superior, to murmur in the secret of thy 
heart, even though thou shouldst outwardly fulfil what 
is enjoined, that is not true obedience, but rather a 
cloak for malice.* Consequently, thou art still in want 
of the day of patience, which shall enable thee to 
embrace with a tranquil mind everything hard and 
disagreeable, condemning and sharply reproving thyself 
because of thy disrelish for things so salutary, and in 
thy thoughts always taking the side of the superior 
against thyself, endeavouring always and in all things 
—so far as it may be possible—to excuse him and to 
blame thyself. 

But at this stage thou shalt need, as I think, to be 
on thy guard against pride. For to have thus over- 
come thyself is certainly no small achievement. “The 
patient man,” says Solomon, “is better than the 


* Cf. Holy Rule of St. Benedict, chap. v. 


444 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


valiant; and he that ruleth his spirit than he that 
taketh cities.” Observe how manifestly the Royal 
Prophet teaches us that humility is necessary after 
patience, when he says, “ But be thou, O my soul, 
subject to God, for from Him is my patience.” Does 
it not seem from this that his very patience became for 
him an occasion of temptation to pride? It is therefore 
necessary that the day of humility should enlighten 
thy heart and show thee plainly what thou art of 
thyself and what thou owest to the grace of God, so 
that thou mayest not be high-minded. For, as St. 
James bears witness, “God resisteth the proud and 
giveth grace to the humble.” 

And when thou hast been long exercised in these 
virtues, pray now that the light of devotion may be 
granted thee, that most serene day, that sabbath of the 
soul, in which, like a soldier honourably discharged thou 
mayest live without labour in the midst of all labour, 
and deserve to have applied to thee the words of the 
Psalmist, “ I have run the way of Thy commandments 
when Thou didst enlarge my heart.” Then thou shalt 
discover an ineffable sweetness and delight in those 
very practices which in the beginning thou wert wont 
to go through with bitterness and repugnance of mind.* 
This, unless I mistake, is the grace David is pleading 
for where he cries out, ‘‘Oh, forgive me that I may 
be refreshed.” As if he should say, “ How long shall I 
be afflicted with this labour and sorrow? Until when 


* « This path (of perfection) is indeed narrow in the beginning ; 
but when thou shalt have advanced therein, and grown strong 
in faith, thy heart shall be enlarged, and thou shalt run in the 
way of God’s commandments with that unspeakable delight 
which charity imparts to the soul.’’—Preface to the Holy Rule 
of St. Benedict (Mount Melleray Translation), 


FOR THE OCTAVE OF THE CIRCUMCISION 445 


shall death pursue me all the day long? Oh, forgive 
me that I may be refreshed!” But it seems to me 
that only a few attain during the present life to this 
degree of perfection. And even if one sometimes dis- 
covers in himself probable signs of his having arrived 
thus far, he must not be too ready to believe it, par- 
ticularly if he is a novice and has not yet passed through 
all the preceding degrees. For Jesus Christ, our most 
loving-kind Lord, is wont to attract such beginners 
with graces of peace and consolation similar to those 
enjoyed by the perfect. But persons thus favoured 
should know that these graces are rather lent to them 
than given, in order that “in the day of good things 
(they) may not be unmindful of evils, and in the day 
of evils (they) may not be unmindful of good things.” 
Far different is the manner according to which souls 
well practised in virtue are allowed to enjoy the sweetness 
of heavenly consolation. But, as I have said, there 
are many who never in their whole lives attain to this 
happy state. Such as these may rest assured that if 
they continue their efforts with all devotion and per- 
severance, they shall obtain, at least in the moment 
after death, that which during life was withheld from 
them for their good. For then the grace of God alone, 
without any co-operation on their part, will advance 
them in an instant to that point, which with the assist- 
ance of the same grace they have been so long and so 
vainly struggling to reach. Thus “ made perfect in a 
short space,” they shall “ fulfil a long time.” 

Now, as for those who have attained to this grace 
of devotion, there is, as I think, one more danger 
lying in their way. They have need to be on their 
guard against the ‘“‘noon-day devil.” ‘‘ For Satan 


446 ST. BERNARD’S SERMONS 


himself,” as the Apostle tells us, “ transformeth himself 
into an angel of light.” This, then, is a danger to be 
feared by such as discharge all their duties with sen- 
sible devotion: following their attraction, they may 
injure their health by indiscreet austerities, and thus 
be obliged—not without much spiritual loss—to occupy 
themselves with the care of a weakened body. Con- 
sequently he who would run without danger of falling 
must be enlightened by the day of discretion, which 
is the mother of virtues, and the crown of perfection. 
By discretion we are taught to avoid excess in every- 
thing: and this is the eighth day in which the child 
is circumcised. For discretion truly circumcises, by 
keeping us between the extremes of too much and too 
little. He who exceeds rather cuts off than circumcises 
the fruit of a good work ; and the same is true of him 
who through sloth is deficient. In this eighth day, 
accordingly, the name is imposed, and it is the name 
of salvation, viz., Jesus. Nor have I any hesitation in 
saying that whoso lives in the practice of the above- 
mentioned virtues is indeed a Jesus, a saviour, and is 
working out the salvation of his own soul. Until this 
day of discretion none but the angels, the confidants of 
God’s secrets, have been able to call him by this saving 
name, which I now give him for the first time, yet 
with all security. Nevertheless, since such perfection 
is a very rare bird on earth * let obedience stand to 
you, my brethren, in place of discretion,f so that you 
shall do nothing more, nothing less, nothing other- 
wise than has been commanded. 


* “ Rara ista avis in terra,” quoted from the poet Juvenal, 
Sat. vi.—(Translator.) 

i It was a saying of St. Ignatius of Loyola that “ discretion 
is for superiors, obedience for inferiors.” —(Translator.)

“ JuBILUs RHYTHMICUS SANCTI BERNARDI.” 


I 


The very thought of Jesus thrills 

With transports sweet this heart of mine, 
But oh! what joy, what rapture fills 

The soul that sees His Face divine! 


II 


No sweeter sound can soothe the ear, 
The voice no sweeter song can sing, 
No sweeter thought the soul can cheer, 
Than Jesus, Son of heaven’s King. 


III 


Jesus, the sinner’s hope of grace, 
To all who ask how loving kind ! 

How good to all who seek Thy Face! 
But what, oh, what to them that find ! 


IV 


O Jesus, sweetness of my heart, 

The Fount of life, and light, and love, 
To me the joy of joys Thou art, 

A joy all hope and thought above.

Vv 


Nor tongue nor pen can ever tell, 
How eloquent soe’er they be, 

’Tis only he that loves Thee well 
Can know the joy of loving Thee. 


VI 


I’ll seek Him where He loves to hide, 
Within the cloister of my breast, 

TPI seek at home, I’ll seek outside, 
I’ll seek with love that cannot rest. j 


Vil 


With Mary, at the dawn of day, 
I'll seek the tomb where Jesus lies, 
In spirit, not with eyes of clay, 
Lamenting with love’s plaintive cries.

My sobs and groans shall fill the place, 
Pll cast me down at Jesus’ Feet 

And clasp them close in fond embrace. 


IX 


How good a King our Jesus is! 
How great a Conqueror of hell! 
All beautiful whate’er is His 
And sweet beyond my power to tell.

x 


Stay with us still, Lord Jesus, stay, 

To guide our steps life’s desert through ; 
Drive from our souls all mists away, 

And let Thy love the world subdue. 


XI 


The sun of truth shines bright and clear 
Within the soul where Thou art guest ; 
How vile the things of earth appear 
When Thy sweet love inflames the breast ! 


XII 


The love of Jesus brings delight 
And ravishment exceeding great, 
Its tenderness no man aright, 
Though thousand-tongued, can celebrate. 


XIII 


His Passion is a proof of this, 

His streaming Wounds attest the same, 
Whereby He bought us heaven’s bliss 

And saved us from the vengeful flame. 


XIV 


Acknowledge Jesus, mortals all, 
The grace to love Him boldly claim, 
With fervour seek Him, great and small, 
And let the search your hearts inflame.

XV 


Yea, love Him Who so loveth you, 
Let love’s debt liquidated be, 

Give love for love and so pursue 
The odour of His charity. 


XVI 


O Jesus, Source of clemency, 
Our only joy and hope Thou art ; 

All grace and sweetness flows from Thee, 
The true delight of every heart. 


XVII 


Jesus, my Treasure, let me feel 
The fullest force of love divine, 
Oh, let me in Thy presence kneel 
And see the glory that is Thine! 


XVIII 


I cannot speak in worthy wise 
Of Thee, yet shall not silent be ; 

The will and daring love supplies, 
For all my soul is set on Thee. 


XIX 


Thy love, O Lord, is mystic meat, 

Which brings no weariness to the mind; 
It fills us with refreshment sweet, 

Yet leaves the hunger still behind.

For he that eats Thee hungers still,

One only good attracts his will: 
With him ’tis Jesus last and first.

How Jesus savours none can tell 
But he whom love possesses whole ; 
How blest who Jesus loves so well 
That all things else disgust his soul !

Thy love which ever in me lives, 


With languor constant grieves the mind,

Of life, by limits none confined.

No passion can oppose its sway, 
It never dies, it never wanes,

And fills the soul with pure delight.

Abides in me, a welcome guest, 

It sets on fire my very soul, 
And makes a heaven in my breast. 


XXXIV 


O happy flame of charity ! 
O blissful ardour of desire ! 

O love of God’s sweet Son! In thee 
Is shelter safe from passion’s fire.

Thy savour, Lord, gives such delight, 
Thy perfume is so sweet withal, 

My love-sick soul longs for the sight 
Of Thee, Who art her all in all. 


XXXIX 


For Thou love’s consummation art, 
The bliss and sunshine of my mind, 


The pride and glory of my heart, 
O Jesus, Saviour of mankind.

HYMN TO THE NAME OF JESUS 


XL 


Reseat Thee at the Father’s Right, 
Beloved Lord, Thy work is done ; 

Thy foes Thou hast subdued with might, 
And now enjoy the kingdom won. 


XLI 
Yet we can never parted be, 
For Thou hast stolen away my heart : 


Go where Thou wilt, I'll follow Thee, 
Sweet Lord, who ail my glory art. 


XLII 


Lift up your gates and run to meet, 
Ye angels all, your King and mine; 

And thus the glorious Victor greet : 
“Hail Jesus, hail our King divine.” 


XLIII 


Great King of glory, Lord of might, 
Immortal Congueror of hell, 

Sole Splendour of the land of light, 
And Giver of all grace as well ; 


XLIV 


Yea, mercy’s never-failing source, 
Eternal Brightness of the blest : 

Drive clouds and sadness from our course 
And let Thy light upon us rest. 


455 


456 


HYMN TO THE NAME OF JESUS 


XLV 


The angels with unwearying voice 
Shall sing Thy praises evermore, 
For that Thou didst the world rejoice 
And peace ’twixt God and man restore. 


XLVI 


The Prince of peace is Jesus mine, 

Of peace no mind can comprehend; 
For this my soul shall ever pine, 

To this do all her strivings tend. 


XLVII 


Our Jesus has the skies resought, 
To rule as King o’er friends and foes, 
And my poor heart has with Him brought, 
Which follows Him where’er He goes. 


XLVIII 


Oh, let us honour Him with prayer, 

With praise, and vows, and hymns of love, 
That through His mercy we may share 

His glory in the realms above. 


Printed by Browne and Nolan, Ltd., Dublin.
